Okra

Abelmoschus esculentus

CulinaryCookedFruitLeafSeed

A warm-season pod vegetable in the mallow family — kin to mallow, and to hibiscus and cotton — its pale-yellow flowers show it. The young pods are picked tender; left alone they grow tough and woody.

Varieties

Clemson Spineless

Skin
Flesh

The standard: dark green, spineless pods on plants about 4 feet tall. An open-pollinated heirloom and All-America Selections winner (1939). Pick at 3–4 inches; pods can reach 9 inches but toughen.

Red Burgundy

Skin
Flesh

Red stems and burgundy pods that turn green as they cook. Ornamental enough for a flower bed, and tender to about 7 inches. Ready in about 55 days.

Baby Bubba

Skin
Flesh

A dwarf hybrid, just 3–3.5 feet tall, bred for containers and small gardens. Dark green pods about 3 inches long, ready in about 53 days.

Annie Oakley II

Skin
Flesh

An early hybrid (about 50–55 days), 4–4.5 feet tall, with long, slender, spineless green pods. Heavy yields, and the pods hold well on the plant between pickings.

Around the World

Louisiana — gumbo: okra is one of the stew's classic thickeners, alongside filé and roux.
West Africa — okra soups, drawn silky with the pod's own mucilage, eaten with fufu and other staples.
India — bhindi masala: okra fried with onion, tomato and spices, cooked dry so it stays crisp rather than slick.
Middle East — bamia: okra simmered in a garlicky tomato stew, often with lamb.
Brazil — caruru: okra cooked with dried shrimp, peanuts and dendê oil, an Afro-Brazilian dish of Bahia.
American South — fried okra, sliced, cornmeal-crusted and crisped.

Origin & Lineage

  • Okra's exact birthplace is still argued over. The Ethiopian highlands — reaching into Eritrea and Sudan — are the most favoured origin, with West Africa and South Asia also claimed; what is certain is that it was cultivated in Egypt by the 12th–13th centuries, where it was called bamya, a name still used across the Middle East.
  • It reached the Americas in the 17th century through the transatlantic slave trade — recorded in Brazil by 1658 and in the American South by the late 1680s — carried by African people who kept their foodways alive through the crossing. It remains a cornerstone of African diaspora cooking.
  • Even its English names carry the journey: “okra” comes from the Igbo ọ́kụ̀rụ̀, and “gumbo” from the Bantu ki ngombo.
  • Modern varieties branch from that heritage — heirloom Southern standards, red-podded ornamentals, and dwarf hybrids bred for pots.
  • The leaves are eaten as a cooked green in parts of Africa and Asia. The seeds were roasted and ground as a coffee substitute in the past, and are pressed for oil.
  • Low in calories (33 kcal per 100 g raw) and a good source of fibre — about 3.2 g per 100 g — including the mucilage, a soluble, gel-forming fibre that gives okra its famous silkiness and slows the absorption of sugar and cholesterol in the gut.
  • A good source of vitamin C (23 mg per 100 g, about 26% of the daily value), vitamin K (about 26%), folate (about 15%), magnesium and manganese.
  • Pooled analyses of small clinical trials have found modest reductions in fasting blood sugar and LDL cholesterol with okra preparations — but the trials used concentrated powders and extracts (around 3,000 mg a day), not plate-sized portions of the vegetable. Promising, and a good reason to enjoy okra often — but it is not a treatment for diabetes or high cholesterol.
  • Caution: okra may interfere with how the diabetes medicine metformin is absorbed — if you take it, keep okra at ordinary food portions, space it away from the dose, and mention it to your clinician.
  • Caution: okra contains oxalates, natural compounds found in many plants that can add to kidney stones in people prone to them, so people prone to kidney stones may want to moderate portions.
  • When to plant: Sow outside once the soil is warm, after all frost — late spring into early summer. In cool districts, start in pots a few weeks earlier.
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Rich, well-drained soil; happy in most garden soils once they are warm
  • Environment: A true heat-lover — it shrugs off the hottest weeks, and frost kills it
  • Planter or ground: Both — dwarf kinds such as Baby Bubba suit big pots; full-size plants do best in the ground
  • Spacing and depth: Sow 2.5 cm (1 in) deep; thin to 30–45 cm apart
  • Water: Average — the deep taproot tolerates dry spells once settled
  • Companion plants: Peppers and basil are good neighbours
  • When to pick: About 50–60 days from sowing; pick pods at 3–4 inches, every day or two

Garden notes

  • A true heat-lover: sow or transplant only once the soil is warm, and it will shrug off the hottest weeks that stall other crops.
  • The hibiscus-like flowers are visited by bees and other pollinators before each one becomes a pod.
  • A deep taproot helps it tolerate dry spells once established, and lets it draw minerals up from below the reach of shallow roots.
  • Pick every day or two at 3–4 inches — frequent picking keeps the plant producing all season.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for okra, raw
  • Frontiers in Nutrition meta-analysis (2024) — okra preparations and blood sugar and cholesterol in trials, at extract doses
  • Pooled analysis of okra trials (2026) — fasting-glucose reduction from concentrated okra powders
  • NC State Extension and Cornell variety references — okra variety notes

Peppers

Capsicum (five domesticated species)

CulinaryMedicinalCraftFreshDriedCookedFruit

One plant family that runs the whole scale — from the sweet bell, with no heat at all, to the habanero, where a sliver flavours a pot. Peppers are a single genus, Capsicum, with five domesticated species; the everyday kinds are mostly Capsicum annuum, with the habanero from Capsicum chinense. The heat comes from capsaicin, concentrated in the pale membrane inside the fruit — not, as most people guess, mainly in the seeds.

Varieties

Heat in Scoville units (SHU) — bell pepper is 0; pure capsaicin is about 16 million.

Bell

Skin
Flesh

Sweet, thick-walled and juicy; green bells are simply unripe, and ripen to red, orange or yellow with more sweetness and vitamins. The everyday stuffing and salad pepper.

Heat: 0 SHU

Banana

Skin
Flesh

Long, pale-yellow and mild, with a gentle tang. Best known pickled in rings for sandwiches and salads; sweet types are good fresh too.

Heat: 0–500 SHU

Poblano

Skin
Flesh

Broad, dark green and rich, with earthy depth under mild heat. The roasting and stuffing pepper of Mexico (chiles rellenos); dried, it becomes the ancho.

Heat: 1,000–2,000 SHU

Jalapeño

Skin
Flesh

Bright, grassy, everyday heat; picked green, or left to redden. Smoked and dried, it becomes the chipotle.

Heat: 2,500–8,000 SHU

Cayenne

Skin
Flesh

Long, thin and red, with a sharp, clean burn. The classic drying pepper — ground into cayenne powder and shaken into hot sauces.

Heat: 30,000–50,000 SHU

Habanero

Skin
Flesh

Small, lantern-shaped, usually orange — very hot, but with a fruity, almost floral flavour under the fire. A little goes a very long way. (Capsicum chinense.)

Heat: 100,000–350,000 SHU

Around the World

Mexico — poblanos roasted and stuffed as chiles rellenos; jalapeños pickled en escabeche; a salsa for every region.
Hungary — paprika, from sweet to hot, is at the heart of goulash; the peppers arrived via Ottoman trade and were bred into national varieties.
Korea — sun-dried chillies ground into gochugaru flakes give kimchi its red heat — a New World pepper at the heart of an old tradition.
Thailand — bird's eye chillies pounded into curry pastes and stirred through dishes for bright, fierce heat.
Spain — smoked pimentón from La Vera, and padrón peppers blistered in olive oil and salted.
Louisiana — cayenne-based hot sauces, fermented and bottled, shaken over everything.

Origin & Lineage

  • Wild peppers began in the eastern Andes, in what is now Bolivia and southern Peru, and people have eaten them since at least 7,500 BC. Capsicum annuum — the species behind bells, jalapeños, poblanos and cayennes — was domesticated in Mexico some 6,000 years ago.
  • Columbus met peppers in 1492 and misnamed them after black pepper. The mistake stuck; the plant did not wait around. Within fifty years Spanish and Portuguese traders had carried it to Africa, India and Southeast Asia; it reached China by the mid-1500s and Hungary by Ottoman trade routes, where it became paprika.
  • By 1600 peppers were established from Korea to West Africa — arguably the fastest global spread of any New World food, because they slotted into the place black pepper and local hot spices already held.
  • A naming footnote: Capsicum chinense, the habanero's species, is South American through and through — a botanist wrongly assumed it came from China, and the error is preserved in its Latin name.

Craft

  • Dried peppers are strung into ristras — long strings of red pods hung in kitchens and doorways across Mexico and the American Southwest. They began as a way of storing the harvest; they are now as much ornament as store cupboard.
  • Ripe red bell peppers are among the richest common sources of vitamin C — about 128 mg per 100 g (142% of the daily value), more than oranges by weight. Green bells give about 80 mg; peppers gain vitamin C and vitamin A as they ripen from green to red.
  • Red peppers are also a good source of vitamin A (about 157 µg RAE per 100 g), supporting normal vision and immunity.
  • Capsaicin, the heat compound, has one firmly established medical use: applied to the skin in creams and patches, it eases nerve and joint pain. That is a medicine use of the concentrated compound — eating peppers is not the same thing.
  • Claims that eating chillies meaningfully boosts metabolism or burns fat come from small, short-lived effects in trials; they are too slight to state as a benefit, so they are not claimed here.
  • Caution: hot peppers can aggravate reflux and sensitive stomachs. When preparing hot varieties, wear gloves if your skin is sensitive, wash hands well, and never touch your eyes — the heat sits in the pale membrane, so removing membrane and seeds tames a pepper more than rinsing does.
  • When to plant: Start indoors 8–10 weeks before the last frost; plant out into warm soil after frost
  • Sun: Full sun — the warmest, sunniest spot you have
  • Soil: Rich, well-drained soil
  • Environment: Heat-loving and slow to start; cold nights stall it
  • Planter or ground: Both — smaller kinds do well in a pot of 10 litres or more
  • Spacing and depth: 45 cm apart
  • Water: Average — steady moisture while the fruit is swelling
  • Companion plants: Basil, onions and tomatoes are good neighbours, and marigolds help too
  • When to pick: About 60–80 days from planting out; pick green to keep the plant cropping, or leave fruit to ripen red for sweetness

Garden notes

  • Heat-loving and slow to start: in cooler climates, sow indoors 8–10 weeks before the last frost and transplant into the warmest, sunniest spot.
  • Peppers are self-pollinating, but bees working the flowers improve fruit set; a flowering plant draws pollinators into the garden.
  • Fruit left to ripen fully — green to red — gains sweetness and vitamins; harvesting green encourages the plant to keep producing.
  • Save seed from open-pollinated varieties, but grow hot and sweet types apart if you want the seed to come true — peppers cross-pollinate readily.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for red and green bell peppers
  • NC State Extension — pepper variety references
  • Cornell — pepper variety references

Sweet Potato

Ipomoea batatas

CulinaryCookedRootLeaf

A sweet, starchy storage root — a root that swells to store the plant's food, and the part we eat — from tropical America, and a member of the morning glory family — the trailing vines and trumpet flowers give it away. It is not a true yam, and not a potato either: it is its own plant, and a nourishing common food.

Varieties

Beauregard

Skin
Flesh

Rose-copper skin, deep orange flesh. Moist, reliably sweet, a little fibrous. The American standard and an all-rounder: pies, purées, roasting.

Garnet (Red Garnet)

Skin
Flesh

Dark reddish-purple skin, bright orange flesh. Especially moist and soft, sweet with a hint of squash-like savouriness. Often sold as a “yam.”

Jewel

Skin
Flesh

Copper skin, bright orange flesh. Moist and dependable, slightly less sweet than Beauregard, and it holds its shape — good for cubes and hashes.

Japanese / Murasaki

Skin
Flesh

Purple-red skin, white flesh that turns golden as it cooks. Dry, fluffy and starchy, with a chestnut-and-vanilla sweetness. Best roasted whole. (Murasaki itself was bred in Louisiana from Japanese stock.)

Okinawan Purple

Skin
Flesh

Plain tan skin hiding vivid magenta-purple flesh. Dense and drier, mildly sweet and earthy-chestnut in flavour. A staple of Okinawa, used in desserts.

Around the World

Peru — camote is served alongside ceviche, its sweetness set against the lime and chilli.
Japan — yakiimo: whole satsumaimo sweet potatoes stone-roasted until syrupy, sold from trucks in winter.
Philippines — kamote cue: skewered slices caramelised in brown sugar.
American South — candied “yams” and sweet potato pie, a holiday table standard.
New Zealand — kūmara, a Māori staple traditionally cooked in a hāngī earth oven.

Origin & Lineage

  • Sweet potatoes were domesticated in tropical America at least 5,000 years ago, most likely in Central America; dried remains found in Peru's Chilca Canyon date back some 10,000 years.
  • Long before European ships, the plant crossed the Pacific: sweet potatoes were growing in Polynesia by about 1000–1100 CE, and genetic studies of old herbarium specimens support a pre-Columbian journey from the Peru–Ecuador coast. Even the names echo each other — Quechua kumar and Polynesian kumara / kūmara.
  • After 1492 it travelled the other way too, with Spanish and Portuguese traders carrying it to Africa, India and Asia. In Mexico and the Philippines it is still camote, from the Nahuatl camotli.
  • Nearly all modern varieties branch from that American ancestry — bred in Louisiana, Japan, Okinawa and beyond for colour, sweetness and texture.

The root

  • Exceptionally rich in vitamin A: one medium baked sweet potato provides about 1,096 µg RAE — RAE is the measure of vitamin A the body can use — roughly 122% of the daily value — in the form of beta-carotene, which the body converts as needed. Vitamin A supports normal vision and the immune system.
  • A good source of fibre — about 3.8 g in a medium potato — which supports digestion and steadier blood sugar. Boiled sweet potato sits in the moderate range of the glycaemic index — the scale of how quickly a food raises blood sugar — at around 41–50.
  • Provides vitamin C (about 20–25% of the daily value) and potassium (about 542 mg per medium potato), which helps keep blood pressure in the normal range as part of a balanced diet.
  • In a randomised trial, orange sweet potato improved vitamin A stores in schoolchildren — one reason it is used in public-health programmes where vitamin A deficiency is common.
  • Purple-fleshed varieties carry anthocyanins, the same family of pigments that colour blueberries; the orange varieties carry the beta-carotene. Colour is a fair guide to what a variety offers.

The leaves

  • The leaves are a food in their own right. Cooked as a green across Asia, Africa and Latin America, they are rich in beta-carotene, B vitamins, iron, calcium and protein, and carry more polyphenols — protective plant compounds — than many shop-bought vegetables.
  • Caution: sweet potatoes contain moderate oxalates, natural compounds found in many plants that can add to kidney stones in people prone to them, so people prone to kidney stones may want to moderate portions. Eating a great deal can tint the skin slightly orange — harmless, and it fades.
  • When to plant: Plant slips — rooted sprouts from a stored root — after all frost, into warm soil
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Loose, sandy, well-drained soil; heavy clay makes misshapen roots
  • Environment: Frost-tender and heat-loving — the longer and warmer the season, the better the roots size up
  • Planter or ground: Ground best; big pots and grow bags work
  • Spacing and depth: Set slips 30–45 cm apart, a few inches deep
  • Water: Average — water while the slips settle in, then only in dry spells
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: About 90–120 days; dig when the leaves yellow, and before frost

Garden notes

  • Grown from slips — rooted sprouts from a stored root — rather than seed; plant after all frost, in warm, loose soil.
  • The vigorous vines make a living ground cover, shading the soil, holding moisture and crowding out weeds through the summer.
  • Frost-tender and heat-loving: the longer and warmer the season, the better the roots size up.
  • The young leaves are edible too, cooked as a green in many of the cultures that grow the roots.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for sweet potato baked in skin
  • NIH Office of Dietary Supplements — vitamin A values for baked sweet potato
  • Randomised controlled trial of orange-fleshed sweet potato in schoolchildren — improved vitamin A stores
  • PNAS (2013) — herbarium genetics supporting pre-Columbian transfer of sweet potato to Polynesia
  • NC State Extension, Cornell and LSU AgCenter cultivar releases — variety notes

Tomato

Solanum lycopersicum

CulinaryFreshCookedFruit

A tender fruit eaten as a vegetable, and a member of the nightshade family — kin to the Peppers, and to potatoes and aubergines. The ripe fruit is the food; the rest of the plant is not. Few garden plants have travelled further, or been more wrongly accused along the way.

Varieties

Brandywine

Skin
Flesh

The heirloom beefsteak: large pink fruit, often a pound or more, on potato-leaved plants. Rich and sweet-tart, slow and not a heavy bearer — grown for flavour, not quantity. Slicing and sandwiches.

Cherokee Purple

Skin
Flesh

A pre-1890 Tennessee heirloom, shared through Seed Savers Exchange in 1990: dusky rose-purple shoulders over deep red flesh, 10–12 oz fruit. Rich, smoky-sweet and full-flavoured. Fresh eating and salads.

San Marzano

Skin
Flesh

Italy's sauce tomato, from the Sarno valley near Naples: long plum fruit with thick flesh, few seeds and little water. The standard for Neapolitan pizza sauce and passata. Cooking and canning.

Roma

Skin
Flesh

The everyday plum: firm, meaty and few-seeded, bred for sauces and paste. Dependable and widely grown where San Marzano seed is hard to find.

Sungold

Skin
Flesh

An orange cherry with very sweet flavour and heavy crops over a long season. Eating off the vine and salads.

Around the World

Mexico — the tomato's cultivated homeland: salsas raw and roasted, and tomatoes simmered into the sauces of a hundred regional dishes.
Italy — San Marzano tomatoes crushed for Neapolitan pizza and slow Sunday sauces; the tomato became so Italian that its journey to America was really a return trip.
Spain — gazpacho, the cold tomato soup of Andalusia, and pan con tomate: ripe tomato rubbed over grilled bread with olive oil and salt.
India — tomato cooked into the base of countless curries and dals, and chopped fresh into kachumber salads with onion and lime.

Origin & Lineage

  • The wild tomato began in the Andes of western South America, in what is now Peru and Ecuador — a currant-sized fruit on a scrambling vine. It travelled north and was domesticated in pre-Columbian Mexico, where the Aztecs grew many kinds and called it tomatl, the root of the word “tomato.”
  • Spanish ships carried it to Europe in the early 1500s. Spain and Italy took to it first; France and northern Europe grew it as an ornamental curiosity and feared it as a food, because botanists recognised it as a relative of deadly nightshade — it picked up the nickname “poison apple.”
  • The fear was misplaced for the ripe fruit, but not baseless for the plant: the leaves, stems and roots carry bitter glycoalkaloids — the plant's own chemical defences — and are genuinely not food. By the 1700s and 1800s Italy had made the tomato its own, and from there it went everywhere.
  • Modern varieties branch from that one Mesoamerican domestication into beefsteaks, plums, cherries and heirlooms by the thousand.
  • The tomato's signature compound is lycopene — the red pigment, and an antioxidant, a compound that helps protect the body's cells from damage. Raw tomatoes carry about 2.6 mg per 100 g. Cooking tomatoes in a little oil breaks down their cell walls and makes the lycopene far easier to absorb, and concentrated tomato paste carries many times more per spoonful.
  • A useful everyday source of vitamin C — about 14 mg per 100 g (15% of the daily value) — and of potassium (237 mg per 100 g), which helps keep blood pressure in the normal range as part of a balanced diet.
  • Also provides vitamin K (about 8 µg per 100 g), folate and a little vitamin A, at only 18 kcal per 100 g — a tomato is mostly water.
  • Diets rich in tomatoes are associated in population studies with lower rates of prostate cancer and heart disease. That is a pattern observed in how people eat, not proof from trials that tomatoes prevent either disease — the honest claim is that tomatoes sit squarely in the way of eating that is linked with better health.
  • Caution: eat only the ripe fruit. The leaves, stems and unripe green fruit carry the plant's glycoalkaloid defences and are not food.
  • Caution: tomatoes are acidic and can aggravate reflux in people prone to it.
  • When to plant: Start seed indoors 6–8 weeks before the last frost; plant out only into warm soil
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Rich, well-drained soil with compost worked in
  • Environment: A warm-season crop through and through; cold nights stall it
  • Planter or ground: Both — bush (determinate) kinds suit pots of 20 litres or more; vining kinds need stakes
  • Spacing and depth: 45–60 cm apart; plant deep, burying part of the stem — it roots along it
  • Water: Likes steady water — uneven watering causes blossom end rot; a mulch helps
  • Companion plants: Basil, marigolds, carrots and onions are good neighbours. Keep apart from potatoes — they share the same diseases
  • When to pick: About 60–85 days from planting out; pick when fully coloured and slightly soft

Garden notes

  • A warm-season crop through and through: start seed indoors 6–8 weeks before the last frost and plant out only into warm soil; cold nights stall it.
  • Two habits to choose between. Indeterminate types vine on and crop until frost — stake them, and pinch out the side shoots (“suckers”) in the leaf joints for order. Determinate types grow as a bush and ripen their crop in one flush — better for pots and for paste-making.
  • Uneven watering shows up as blossom end rot: a dark, sunken patch at the fruit's base, where calcium could not keep up with growth. Steady moisture and a mulch prevent most of it.
  • The flowers are pollinated by bumblebees, whose buzzing shakes the pollen loose — a flowering garden helps feed itself.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for ripe tomatoes and tomato paste, including lycopene
  • Encyclopaedia Britannica — tomato origin, the "poison apple" years and poisonous leaves
  • NIH / National Cancer Institute — evidence for lycopene preventing prostate cancer insufficient
  • Seed Savers Exchange — Cherokee Purple provenance; Appalachian Seeds catalogue variety notes

Onion

Allium cepa

CulinaryCraftFreshCookedRootLeaf

A bulb of layered leaves in the allium family — kin to garlic, leeks, chives and shallots — and the base note of nearly every cuisine on earth. Cultivated for more than 5,000 years, it is one of the oldest truly global vegetables, and it flavours a dish raw, sweated, caramelised, pickled or fried.

Varieties

Yellow (storage types such as Patterson)

Skin
Flesh

The workhorse: firm, pungent and golden-brown, bred to keep for months — up to 10–12 months for the best storage types, properly cured. The cooking onion of soups, stews and caramelising.

Red (such as Redwing)

Skin
Flesh

Deep red-purple rings, milder eaten raw, and the richest in quercetin and anthocyanins. Salads, pickling and grilling; the colour fades with long cooking.

White (such as White Bermuda)

Skin
Flesh

Crisp and bright, a little sharper than yellow; the onion of Mexican cooking, fresh salsas and sharp raw garnishes.

Walla Walla

Skin
Flesh

The famous sweet onion from Walla Walla, Washington: huge, mild and juicy, sweet enough to slice and eat. A summer onion, eaten fresh — too watery to store for long.

Shallot

Skin
Flesh

A dividing member of the same species: clusters of small, copper-skinned bulbs with a fine, mild flavour somewhere between onion and garlic. The sauce-maker's allium, and it stores very well.

Around the World

France — soupe à l'oignon: onions cooked slowly to deep sweetness, capped with bread and Gruyère.
India — onions browned in ghee or oil form the base of countless curries, and sliced onion bhajis are a tea-time favourite.
Mexico — cebolla encurtida: red onions pickled in lime juice or vinegar, the bright pink condiment spooned over tacos and cochinita pibil.
Ethiopia — wat stews begin with a mountain of onions cooked down dry and slow, before the berbere spice and the butter go in. The onion is the sauce.

Origin & Lineage

  • The onion's exact wild ancestor is unproven, but the plant was domesticated in Central Asia — the region of modern Iran and Pakistan — and was already ancient when the Egyptians revered it, laying onions in tombs as a symbol of eternity.
  • From there it travelled every road: Greek athletes ate onions for strength, Roman armies marched on them, and medieval Europe grew them in every garden. Columbus carried onions to the Americas, where they joined the chilli and the tomato.
  • All modern onions branch from that one species: dry storage bulbs, sweet fresh-eating types, bunching onions grown for their green tops, and shallots that divide into clusters of small bulbs.
  • The family resemblance is chemistry. All alliums arm themselves with sulfur compounds, released when the cells are cut; in onions, one of them rises as a gas and makes the cook cry — the price of admission for everything the onion gives back.

Craft

  • The dry outer skins are a traditional dye, giving gold, rust and warm brown shades on wool and on eggs. The part the kitchen throws away is the dyer's part of the plant.

The bulb

  • Onions are among the richest everyday sources of quercetin — an antioxidant plant pigment, concentrated in the outer rings and just under the skin. Red onions carry the most, along with the anthocyanin pigments that colour them. A medium onion carries roughly 20–50 mg of quercetin, though the amount swings widely by variety. What is established is the onion's place as a top dietary source; quercetin as a treatment is not established.
  • The bulb's fibres include fructans — chains of fruit sugars that pass undigested to the gut's bacteria, which ferment and feed on them: a prebiotic fibre, fibre that feeds the friendly gut bacteria. About 2 g per 100 g.
  • The sulfur compounds that make eyes water belong to the same family of compounds studied in garlic for heart and circulation effects. In onions the human evidence is thinner, so enjoy them as food, not medicine.

The green tops

  • Useful everyday amounts of vitamin C (7.4 mg per 100 g), vitamin B6 and folate, at 40 kcal per 100 g. The green tops, eaten as spring onions, carry more vitamin C and vitamin A than the bulb itself.
  • Caution: those same fructans are FODMAPs — fermentable carbohydrates that trigger bloating and discomfort in many people with irritable bowel syndrome. Thorough cooking softens the effect, and the green tops alone are much gentler.
  • Caution, for the household: onions are toxic to dogs and cats — alliums damage their red blood cells. Keep onion scraps and onion-heavy dishes away from pets.
  • When to plant: Sow seed indoors in late winter, or plant sets or seedlings outside in spring. Long-day kinds suit northern gardens, short-day kinds the south — match the kind to your latitude
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Rich, well-drained soil; the roots are shallow, so a weed-free bed matters
  • Environment: Cool-season and hardy; bulbs swell in answer to the long days of summer
  • Planter or ground: Ground best; wide pots work for small crops
  • Spacing and depth: Sets 10 cm apart, just under the surface; seed sown thinly and thinned to the same
  • Water: Likes water — shallow roots dry out fast
  • Companion plants: Carrots are the classic neighbour — each confuses the other's fly. Keep apart from beans and peas, which onions hold back
  • When to pick: Harvest when the tops fall over; cure in a dry, airy place until the necks are papery

Garden notes

  • Onions bulb up in answer to day length: long-day types suit northern gardens, bulbing in the long days of summer; short-day types suit the south. Match the type to your latitude, or you get tops and no bulbs.
  • Grow them from seed, from sets (small dormant bulbs) or from transplants. The roots are shallow and thirsty, so steady water and a weed-free bed matter more than feeding.
  • The whole plant earns its place: young thinnings from the row are spring onions; the flowering stalks of a bolting plant can be eaten like chives; and the dry outer skins make a traditional golden-brown dye for eggs and cloth. Only the roots go to the compost.
  • Harvest when the tops fall over, cure the bulbs in a dry, airy place until the necks are papery, and eat the sweet types first — storage types keep for months, sweets for only a few weeks.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for onions, raw, and green onion tops
  • USDA flavonoid database — onions among the richest common sources of quercetin
  • Monash University FODMAP guidance — onion fructans and IBS; green tops as the low-FODMAP part
  • ASPCA — Allium species toxic to dogs and cats
  • Encyclopaedia Britannica — onion history and cultivation

Green Beans

Phaseolus vulgaris

CulinaryCookedFruitSeed

The green bean is the young, tender pod of the common bean — the same species that gives the world kidney beans, black beans and pintos, picked weeks early, before the seeds inside have swelled. Eaten pod and all, it is one of the heaviest-cropping plants in the garden, and one of the few vegetables that feeds the soil while it feeds the gardener.

Varieties

Blue Lake (bush)

Skin
Flesh

The classic all-purpose bean: straight, stringless, dark green pods about 6 inches long on compact bushes, cropping heavily all at once. Bred from the older Blue Lake pole bean; the standard for canning and freezing.

Kentucky Wonder (pole)

Skin
Flesh

An American heirloom from before the Civil War, first sold as Texas Pole and renamed in 1877. Vigorous vines climb to 8 feet and give long, meaty pods with deep, old-fashioned bean flavour. Needs a trellis and repays it all season.

Provider (bush)

Skin
Flesh

Among the earliest and most dependable: it sprouts in cool soil that stalls other beans and gives tender pods in about 50 days. A first bean of spring for short seasons.

Romano (flat-podded types)

Skin
Flesh

Broad, flat Italian pods with a rich, almost meaty flavour that stands up to slow cooking. The bean for braising in tomato sauce; pick at 4–6 inches.

Haricots Verts / French filet (such as Maxibel)

Skin
Flesh

Extra-thin pods picked while pencil-slim. The French ideal of a green bean; best barely cooked, while still bright.

Cherokee Wax (yellow wax type)

Skin
Flesh

Golden-yellow pods, mild and buttery, tender even when large. The colour helps: the beans are easy to spot among green leaves at picking time.

Around the World

France — haricots verts, blanched until just tender and dressed simply, or folded into salade niçoise with tuna, egg and olives.
China — Sichuan dry-fried green beans (gān biān sì jì dòu): blistered in a hot, nearly dry wok until wrinkled, then tossed with chilli, Sichuan pepper and seasoned minced pork.
Lebanon & the Middle East — loubieh bi zeit: green beans braised gently in olive oil with tomato, onion and garlic, eaten warm or at room temperature.
American South — green beans simmered long and slow with ham until silky — and, since 1955, baked into the green bean casserole that anchors the Thanksgiving table.

Origin & Lineage

  • The common bean was domesticated twice, independently, more than 7,000 years ago — once in Mesoamerica, in what is now Mexico, and once in the Andes of South America. Those two beginnings survive as the bean's two great gene pools, and nearly every bean grown today descends from one or the other.
  • In North America, beans became one of the Three Sisters, planted by Indigenous farmers with corn and squash: the corn gave the bean a pole to climb, the squash shaded the ground, and the bean fed the soil.
  • Beans reached Europe after 1492 and spread quickly — a plant whose dry seed stored for winter and whose young pods made a fresh summer vegetable. The pods were stringy, though, until breeders removed the tough string in the late 1800s; almost every garden bean since is stringless.
  • A green bean is a use, not a separate species: any common bean picked young can be one, but the varieties below were bred for the pod itself — round or flat, green, yellow or purple.

The pods

  • Low in calories (31 kcal per 100 g raw) with useful fibre — about 2.7 g per 100 g — which supports digestion and helps keep blood sugar steadier.
  • A good source of vitamin K — about 43 µg per 100 g, roughly a third of the daily value. Vitamin K is the vitamin the body uses to clot blood normally and to build and maintain bone. (People who take warfarin are usually advised to keep their vitamin K intake steady rather than avoid it — green beans are a meaningful source, so portions are best kept regular.)
  • Provides vitamin C (12.2 mg per 100 g, about 14% of the daily value), folate (about 33 µg, 8% of the daily value) and some vitamin A, plus lutein and zeaxanthin — the yellow pigments, found in green vegetables, that collect in the eye.

The seeds

  • The whole plant gives twice: pods picked young are the green bean, and pods left to ripen on the same plant yield dry beans — the protein-rich staple that has fed more people than almost any other seed.
  • Caution: like other beans, green beans carry natural compounds called lectins, which cooking breaks down. Green beans are at their best — and safest — cooked or lightly blanched, not eaten raw by the handful.
  • When to plant: Sow outside after the last frost, into warm soil — beans dislike being transplanted. Sow a short row every two or three weeks for a steady supply
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Ordinary, well-drained soil — beans fix their own nitrogen, so rich feeding is wasted on them
  • Environment: Frost-tender; they sulk in cold, wet ground
  • Planter or ground: Both — bush kinds suit pots; pole kinds need a trellis
  • Spacing and depth: Sow 5 cm (2 in) deep, 10–15 cm apart
  • Water: Average — water well while the pods are swelling
  • Companion plants: Corn is the classic partner — the Three Sisters planting. Keep apart from onions and garlic, which hold beans back
  • When to pick: About 50–60 days; pick young and pick often, before the seeds inside swell

Garden notes

  • Beans fix their own nitrogen. Bacteria living in nodules — small bumps — on their roots pull nitrogen gas from the air and share it with the plant, so beans leave the soil richer than they found it. This is the quiet work behind the Three Sisters planting.
  • Sow seed directly where it will grow, after the last frost, into warm soil. Beans dislike being transplanted and sulk in cold, wet ground.
  • Bush types crop heavily over a few weeks, so sow a short row every two or three weeks for a steady supply. Pole types crop lighter but longer, up a trellis or a corn stalk.
  • Pick young and pick often. A pod left to swell with seed tells the plant its work is finished, and production slows.
  • The leaves are not eaten. With beans, the harvest is the pod and the seed inside it — nothing else on the plant is food.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for green snap beans, raw
  • Bitocchi et al. (2013) — parallel domestication of the common bean in Mesoamerica and the Andes
  • Oxford University, Plants 400 — Phaseolus vulgaris profile and the Three Sisters system

Carrot

Daucus carota subsp. sativus

CulinaryFreshCookedRootLeaf

A sweet storage root in the parsley family — kin to parsnips, celery and coriander — and the vegetable with the most surprising colour history in the garden. The root is the famous part, but the feathery tops are food too.

Varieties

Scarlet Nantes

Skin
Flesh

The French standard: cylindrical, blunt-tipped, 6–7 inches, nearly coreless and exceptionally sweet. The fresh-eating carrot, and it copes with heavier soil better than most.

Danvers 126

Skin
Flesh

An American heirloom from Danvers, Massachusetts (1870s): conical and deep orange with broad shoulders. Tolerates heavy soil, stores well — the roasting and stew carrot.

Purple Haze

Skin
Flesh

A modern hybrid with dark purple skin over a bright orange core. Sweet, and best raw or lightly cooked, since long cooking bleeds the purple. A living link to the original carrot colours.

Yellowstone

Skin
Flesh

A yellow variety: mild and sweet, with less of the earthy note some people taste in orange carrots. Colour for the plate, and lutein in the mix.

Parisian Market

Skin
Flesh

Round as a golf ball: bred for shallow, stony or clay soil where long roots fork and fail. Fast, and good roasted whole.

Around the World

France — carottes râpées: carrots grated and dressed with lemon, olive oil and parsley — the bistro staple that proves a carrot needs almost nothing.
India — gajar ka halwa: carrots simmered slowly in milk with ghee, sugar and cardamom into a warm, fudge-like pudding.
Central Asia — plov, the celebratory rice pilaf, is built on carrots — traditionally yellow ones — cooked sweet and soft in the oil before the rice goes in.
Morocco — spiced carrot salads: cooked carrots dressed with cumin, garlic, lemon and herbs, served among the small dishes that open a meal.

Origin & Lineage

  • The wild carrot still grows along roadsides as Queen Anne's lace: a thin, pale, bitter root under a froth of white flowers. The cultivated carrot was bred from it in Central Asia, in the Persian world of modern Iran and Afghanistan, around the 10th century.
  • Those first carrots were purple and yellow, not orange. They travelled west with the Islamic world into Spain and across Europe, and east to China, where the carrot was nicknamed the “Iranian turnip.”
  • The orange carrot is a latecomer: Dutch growers of the 16th–17th centuries selected it from yellow stock, fixing the deep orange colour and sweetness that then spread everywhere and pushed the older colours aside. The story that they bred it to honour William of Orange is often told — treat it as a good story, not a settled fact.
  • Modern breeding has circled back: purple, yellow, red and white carrots are returning to the seed catalogues, each colour carrying a different pigment and something slightly different.

The root

  • The orange colour is beta-carotene, which the body converts into vitamin A as needed: 100 g of raw carrot carries about 835 µg RAE — roughly 93% of the daily value — plus alpha-carotene, a close relation. Vitamin A supports normal vision and the immune system.
  • One honest boundary on vision: a lack of vitamin A does damage eyesight, and carrots genuinely help where that lack exists. But the famous claim that carrots gave wartime RAF pilots superhuman night vision was a British cover story, told to hide radar. Carrots support normal vision; they do not upgrade it.
  • Fibre at 2.8 g per 100 g and potassium at 320 mg, for 41 kcal — a crunchy, filling, inexpensive staple.
  • Cooking carrots and eating them with a little fat makes their carotenes easier to absorb. Raw sticks are good; a roasted carrot in oil gives up more of its vitamin A.

The leaves

  • Colour is a guide here too: purple carrots add anthocyanins, the blueberry pigments; yellow carrots carry more lutein, a pigment concentrated in the eye. And the tops are edible and nutritious in their own right — carrot-top pesto and chimichurri are the traditional ways to use them.
  • Caution: eating very large amounts of carrots, or drinking a lot of carrot juice, over weeks can tint the skin orange — carotenemia. It is harmless and fades when the intake eases.
  • When to plant: Sow outside from early spring, where they will grow — carrots resent transplanting
  • Sun: Full sun, or a little shade in hot summers
  • Soil: Deep, loose, stone-free soil; a root that meets a stone forks around it
  • Environment: Cool-season and hardy; sweetness improves after cold nights, so autumn carrots are the sweetest
  • Planter or ground: Ground best; deep pots work for short, round kinds
  • Spacing and depth: Sow 1 cm deep, thinly; thin seedlings to 5 cm apart, then to 10 cm
  • Water: Average — keep the seedbed moist until they sprout, which can take three weeks
  • Companion plants: Onions and leeks are good neighbours — they confuse the carrot fly. Keep apart from dill and parsnips, which share its troubles
  • When to pick: About 70 days; roots are ready when the shoulders show colour at the surface

Garden notes

  • Sow directly where they will grow — carrots resent transplanting — in deep, loose, stone-free soil; a root that meets a stone forks around it. Thin without mercy: crowded carrots stay small.
  • The tops are food, not waste. Pick outer leaves for pesto and salads while the roots size up — just never strip one plant bare.
  • Carrots are biennial: the first year makes the root, the second the Queen Anne's lace flower. Leave one plant standing and you will see the family resemblance, and get seed.
  • Sweetness improves after cold nights, which turn some of the root's starch into sugar, so autumn carrots are the sweetest eating and the best keepers. Lift them, twist off the tops, and store cool and humid for months.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for carrots, raw
  • Population-genomics study of carrot domestication (2023) — Central Asian origin, purple and yellow first cultivars
  • Imperial War Museum and Smithsonian accounts — the wartime night-vision story as radar cover
  • University of Kentucky extension guidance — cooking, storage and variety notes

Cabbage

Brassica oleracea (var. capitata)

CulinaryCraftFreshCookedLeaf

A cool-season leaf vegetable bred into a tight head — and one branch of the most versatile species in the vegetable garden. Cabbage is cheap, keeps for weeks, feeds a crowd, and turns into something new again in the fermenting crock.

Varieties

Golden Acre

Skin
Flesh

The classic early green: round, tight 3–4 lb heads, sweet and tender, ready sooner than most. Fresh eating and slaw; an heirloom standard for smaller gardens.

Ruby Perfection

Skin
Flesh

A fall red: deep purple-red, firm heads bred to store into winter. Higher in vitamin C, it colours salads and slaws and braises to a deep wine shade.

Savoy (such as Famosa)

Skin
Flesh

Crinkled, blistered deep-green leaves; milder and more tender than smooth cabbage, and the leaves wrap without cracking. The cook's cabbage for rolls and gentle braises.

Napa

Skin
Flesh

A close cousin rather than a true cabbage (a different species, Brassica rapa): pale, barrel-shaped, mild and juicy. The kimchi cabbage of Korea and a stir-fry staple in China.

Around the World

Germany — sauerkraut: cabbage salted and fermented in its own juice, served with everything — the ferment that crossed oceans.
Korea — kimchi: napa cabbage salted, seasoned with chilli, garlic and ginger, and fermented; so central that gimjang, the communal making of the winter supply, is its own season.
El Salvador — curtido: a lightly fermented relish of cabbage, carrot, onion and oregano, spooned over pupusas to cut their richness.
Poland — gołąbki: cabbage leaves wrapped around meat and rice and simmered in tomato sauce; and bigos, the hunter's stew of fresh and fermented cabbage together.

Origin & Lineage

  • The ancestor is wild cabbage, a loose-leafed plant of the sea cliffs of coastal southern and western Europe. People gathered and grew it for thousands of years before anyone bred a head.
  • Cabbage belongs to Brassica oleracea, a single species that growers have pulled in every direction: select for leaves and you get kale and collards; for side buds, Brussels sprouts; for a swollen stem, kohlrabi; for flower clusters, cauliflower and broccoli. Cabbage is the branch bred for one great bud — a head of leaves wrapped around the growing point.
  • Heading cabbages took shape in medieval Europe. The crinkled savoy type was refined in Italy's Savoy region in the 16th–17th centuries, and red cabbage travelled the same European roads.
  • Because a whole head keeps for months in a cool store, and ferments into sauerkraut that keeps far longer, cabbage became the winter vegetable of whole continents. Sauerkraut's vitamin C also made it famous at sea, where it was carried on long voyages — Captain Cook's ships among them — against scurvy.

Craft

  • Red cabbage is a dye plant. Its leaves give pink, purple and blue shades on eggs and cloth, and the colour shifts with acid and alkali — the same pigments that make cabbage water a kitchen test for acids.
  • It gives a lot of nutrients for very few calories: 25 kcal per 100 g raw, with vitamin C at about 37 mg (roughly 40% of the daily value), vitamin K at 76 µg (about 63%), folate at 43 µg and fibre at 2.5 g.
  • The colours divide the strengths. Red cabbage carries more vitamin C (about 57 mg per 100 g) and adds anthocyanins, the purple pigments that also colour blueberries. Green cabbage leads on vitamin K and folate; savoy leads on fibre (3.1 g) and folate (80 µg). Colour is a fair guide, as it is with sweet potatoes.
  • Cabbage carries glucosinolates — the sharp, mustardy sulfur compounds behind its bite. When the leaves are chopped or chewed, these convert into isothiocyanates, compounds studied for how they help the body deal with harmful substances. Population studies link eating cruciferous vegetables regularly with lower rates of some cancers; that is an observed pattern across the whole vegetable family, not a proven prevention by cabbage alone.
  • Fermented cabbage — sauerkraut, kimchi — keeps much of the vitamin C and, in unpasteurised versions, carries the live cultures of a true ferment.
  • Caution: cabbage is high in vitamin K, so people taking warfarin or similar blood thinners should keep their intake steady rather than swinging between none and a great deal, and mention it to their clinician.
  • Caution: very large amounts of raw cabbage can interfere with the thyroid's use of iodine — a realistic concern mainly where iodine is already lacking. Ordinary portions, and cooking, are not a problem for most people. If raw cabbage makes you gassy, cooked and fermented forms are usually gentler.
  • When to plant: Start indoors 4–6 weeks before the last frost, or sow outside in spring; sow again in late summer for autumn heads
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Rich, firm soil that holds moisture — a hungry crop
  • Environment: Cool-season; flavour sweetens after a light frost. Net from planting day against cabbage white butterflies
  • Planter or ground: Ground best; one plant to a very large pot
  • Spacing and depth: 45 cm apart; sow 1 cm deep
  • Water: Likes water — steady moisture makes solid heads
  • Companion plants: Onions, garlic, and herbs such as rosemary and sage are good neighbours
  • When to pick: About 70–90 days; cut when the head feels firm and solid

Garden notes

  • A cool-season, hungry crop: plant for spring or late summer, give it rich soil and steady water, and it heads up as the weather cools — flavour sweetens after a light frost.
  • More of the plant is food than the head alone: the loose outer leaves cook like any green, and the core is sweet eaten raw. The roots and the woody stalk base are for the compost heap, not the kitchen.
  • Expect company: cabbage white butterflies lay their eggs on the leaves, and their caterpillars are the price of an unnetted crop. Fine mesh from planting day is the simple answer.
  • Cabbages are biennial — left to a second year they bolt, flower and set seed. Rotate them around the beds, because the soil disease clubroot follows brassicas grown in one place too long.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for green, red and savoy cabbage
  • Kew — cabbage, kale, collards and their kin as one species, Brassica oleracea
  • Captain Cook's journals and naval histories — sauerkraut carried against scurvy at sea

Banana

Musa (chiefly Musa acuminata and its hybrids)

CulinaryCraftFreshCookedLeafFlowerFruit

The banana is a staple food for much of the tropics and a snack for everywhere else — and one of the strangest crops on earth. The “tree” is a giant herb, not a tree at all; the fruit is seedless and sterile; and almost every banana in every shop is a cutting of a cutting of a single variety, the Cavendish. That sameness is the banana's triumph, and its danger.

Varieties

Cavendish

Skin
Flesh

The world's banana: medium-sized, mild and sweet, shipped green and ripened to yellow on arrival. Nearly half of all bananas grown. Its dwarf form is the one sold for pots and small gardens.

Gros Michel

Skin
Flesh

The lost export king. Larger, creamier and more fragrant than the Cavendish, with a thick peel that shrugged off sea voyages. Panama disease ended its trade by the 1960s, but it still grows in gardens and small farms from Thailand to Latin America. To taste one is to taste the banana your great-grandparents knew.

Lady Finger

Skin
Flesh

Small, thin-skinned and very sweet, with pale flesh that does not brown when cut. A favourite in Australia, and the banana for fruit salads.

Blue Java (“Ice Cream Banana”)

Skin
Flesh

Fruit with a silvery blue-green bloom that ripens to plain yellow; the flesh is soft and creamy, nicknamed for vanilla ice cream. Unusually cold-tolerant for a banana, so it is the variety most often attempted in cooler gardens.

Red (Red Dacca)

Skin
Flesh

Short, plump fruit in a deep red-purple skin; the flesh inside is cream to pale pink, sweet, with a slight raspberry taste. Eaten fresh wherever it grows.

Plantain

Skin
Flesh

Not a dessert banana at all, but a starchy cooking banana (an AAB hybrid) that must be cooked: fried green into tostones, or black-ripe into sweet maduros. For hundreds of millions of people, this — not the sweet banana — is the banana that matters.

Around the World

Uganda — matooke: green highland bananas peeled with a knife, wrapped in banana leaves and steamed for hours into a soft, pale mash. It is the national dish, eaten daily, with beans or groundnut stew.
Puerto Rico & the Caribbean — green plantains fried twice into crisp, salted tostones; ripe ones caramelised into maduros; and banana leaves lining the pasteles of the Christmas table.
Philippines — turon: saba bananas and jackfruit wrapped, fried and coated in caramelised sugar; and banana cue, skewered bananas grilled in the same crackling sugar.
India — meals served on banana leaves across the south; the blossom (vazhaipoo) chopped and spiced; the stem cooked as a vegetable; and in Kerala, thin banana chips fried in coconut oil.

Origin & Lineage

  • Bananas were among the first fruits ever farmed. At Kuk Swamp in the highlands of New Guinea, traces of banana cultivation reach back about 7,000 years. Wild bananas are packed with hard seeds and little flesh; early farmers found rare seedless plants and kept them alive by planting the side shoots — suckers — that rise around the parent stem.
  • The dessert banana descends mostly from Musa acuminata, crossed in places with the hardier Musa balbisiana. Breeders still label the family lines by letters — A for acuminata, B for balbisiana — so a plantain is AAB and the Cavendish AAA. Hundreds of local varieties branch from those crosses across Asia, the Pacific and Africa.
  • Bananas reached Africa well over a thousand years ago and settled so deeply that East Africa bred its own highland cooking bananas. Spanish and Portuguese ships carried the plant to the Americas in the 1500s.
  • The export trade first ran on a single variety, the Gros Michel — larger than today's banana, richer-tasting, with a thick peel built for sea voyages. A soil fungus called Panama disease destroyed its plantations through the first half of the 1900s, and by the 1960s the trade had torn it out and replanted with a variety the fungus could not kill: the Cavendish.
  • The Cavendish now accounts for nearly half of all bananas grown in the world and almost the whole of the export trade — and every one of those plants is a clone, traced back to a single plant grown under glass at Chatsworth House in England in the 1830s. A newer strain of the same fungus, Tropical Race 4, kills Cavendish, and it is spreading through Asia, Africa and Latin America. With no variety inside the crop, there is nothing resistant to select from. That is the banana's lesson, written twice in a century: variety is not decoration. It is insurance.

Craft

  • The stem — really a tight roll of leaf sheaths — gives a strong fibre. Stripped and dried, it is woven into cloth, mats and baskets, twisted into rope and made into paper, above all in India and the Philippines, where banana fibre has been worked for centuries.

The fruit

  • A medium banana (about 118 g) provides about 105 kcal, 3.1 g of fibre, and 422 mg of potassium — about 9% of the daily value. Bananas are famous for potassium, and the fame is only half earned: the amount is genuinely useful, but beans and leafy greens give more. What a banana offers is potassium in the most portable package in the fruit bowl.
  • Its standout vitamin is B6 — about 0.43 mg per fruit, a quarter of the daily value. Vitamin B6 helps the body release energy from food and build the chemical messengers the brain runs on.
  • Also provides vitamin C (10.3 mg, about 11% of the daily value), manganese and a little magnesium.
  • Ripeness rewrites the fruit. A greener banana holds much of its starch as resistant starch — starch that passes through the small intestine undigested and is fermented by gut bacteria in the large intestine, working much like fibre. As the banana yellows, that starch turns to sugar: sweeter, softer, faster to digest.

The leaves and flowers

  • Nearly the whole plant is used somewhere in the world: the blossom is cooked as a vegetable, the leaves wrap and serve food, the stem is eaten in parts of India, and the peel goes into regional dishes and compost. See Around the World.
  • Caution: people with advanced kidney disease are often told to limit potassium-rich foods, bananas among them. That is a matter for their own clinician — it is not a reason for anyone else to pass the fruit bowl by.
  • When to plant: Plant pups — the suckers that rise around the parent stem — in spring or early summer in warm climates; in pots, start any time and move out after frost
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Rich, deep, well-drained soil with plenty of compost
  • Environment: Tropical through and through: heat and abundant water, frost cuts it to the ground, and the great leaves need shelter from wind
  • Planter or ground: Ground in the tropics; elsewhere a dwarf kind in a big pot, wheeled under cover for winter
  • Spacing and depth: Give a clump room — 3 m or more from other plants
  • Water: Likes water — never let it dry out for long
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: The bunch is ready 3–6 months after the flower appears; cut it when the fruits plump out and their edges round, and ripen indoors

Garden notes

  • Bananas are tropical through and through: heat, abundant water and rich feeding, and frost cuts them to the ground. Outside the tropics they are a container adventure — a dwarf variety in a big pot, wheeled under cover for winter.
  • They grow not from seed but from suckers, called pups, that rise around the parent stem. A clump of stems of different ages is a mat, and a well-fed mat can live and crop for decades.
  • Each stem fruits once and then dies back. Cut the spent stem out and the pups around it take its place — the planting renews itself, as long as the mat is fed, watered and thinned to a few strong stems.
  • Shelter them from wind. Those enormous leaves tear in a gale — a torn leaf looks dramatic and harms nothing; the plant simply grows through it.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for bananas, raw
  • Denham et al. (2003) — early banana cultivation at Kuk Swamp, about 7,000 years ago
  • FAO — Cavendish share of world production and exports, and Tropical Race 4 reporting

Apple

Malus domestica

CulinaryCraftFreshCookedFruit

The apple is a fruit of the rose family with the most branching family tree of any food plant: every named variety in the world descends in part from wild forests still standing in the mountains of Kazakhstan. It is also a fruit with a rule — an apple never grows true from its own seed, so every variety in existence is a clone, carried down the centuries by grafting from one original tree.

Varieties

Honeycrisp

Skin
Flesh

Red over yellow, exceptionally crisp and juicy, sweet with a balancing tartness. Bred by the University of Minnesota and released in 1991; the apple that reset what shoppers expect from crunch. Best eaten fresh.

Granny Smith

Skin
Flesh

Bright green, hard and very tart, with dense flesh that holds its shape in a pie. From a chance seedling raised by Maria Ann Smith in Australia in 1868. The classic cooking and salad apple, and it keeps for months.

Fuji

Skin
Flesh

Dense, very sweet and crisp, red-blushed over yellow-green. Bred in Japan from Red Delicious and Ralls Janet and released in 1962. Stores exceptionally well; a lunchbox standard around the world.

Gala

Skin
Flesh

Mild, sweet and aromatic, striped orange-red over yellow. Bred in New Zealand in the 1930s and now among the most planted apples on earth. An easy first apple for children.

Cox's Orange Pippin

Skin
Flesh

Russet-flecked and orange-red, with a complex, almost spiced flavour no modern apple quite matches. Raised by Richard Cox in England around 1830. The connoisseur's eating apple — and famously temperamental to grow.

Around the World

France — Normandy turns its apples into cider and Calvados brandy, and the tarte Tatin — apples caramelised in butter and sugar under pastry — comes from the Loire Valley.
Austria — Apfelstrudel: spiced apple and raisin rolled in pastry stretched thin enough, bakers say, to read a newspaper through.
United States — apple pie became the emblem of home cooking, though the pie itself, the apples and the saying all have older and wider roots.
England — cider orchards in the West Country, and a long tradition of named dessert apples eaten by the hand — Cox's Orange Pippin, above, first among them.

Origin & Lineage

  • The apple's wild ancestor is Malus sieversii, which still grows in great forests on the forested slopes of the Tian Shan mountains in Kazakhstan. The name of the country's largest city, Almaty, is often translated as “father of apples.” Domestication began there somewhere between 4,000 and 10,000 years ago.
  • Seeds travelled west along the Silk Road — in saddlebags and discarded cores — and along the way the young domestic apple crossed with the local crabapples of Siberia, the Caucasus and Europe. Today's apple carries that mixed ancestry in every fruit.
  • Greek and Roman growers mastered grafting: joining a living cutting of a good tree onto the roots of another, so the fruit stays exactly itself. Without grafting there could be no named varieties at all, because a pip grows into a new and usually sour unknown apple.
  • More than 7,500 named varieties are now recorded worldwide — eating apples, cooking apples, cider apples — yet shops stock only a handful. The old orchard varieties survive largely in the care of collectors and small growers.
  • In North America, John Chapman — Johnny Appleseed — planted seedling orchards across the frontier in the early 1800s. Because seedlings do not come true, most of his apples were cider apples; the famous eating varieties crossed the ocean as grafted trees.

Craft

  • The wood is a craft wood: hard, dense and fine-grained, it is carved and turned, and used for tool handles. It comes from pruned branches and felled orchard trees — the craft use follows the fruit, not the other way round.
  • A medium apple (about 182 g, eaten with its skin) provides about 95 kcal and 4.4 g of fibre — much of it pectin, a soluble fibre, meaning the kind that forms a gentle gel in the gut. That fibre supports digestion and can modestly help keep LDL cholesterol — the “bad” cholesterol — in check.
  • Eat the peel. Nearly half the apple's fibre and most of its quercetin — a plant pigment studied for its antioxidant effects — sit in or just under the skin. Peel the apple and the fibre falls by almost half (from 2.4 g to 1.3 g per 100 g).
  • Provides vitamin C (8.4 mg per medium apple, about 9% of the daily value) and potassium (195 mg) — modest amounts that add up across a diet with fruit in it daily.
  • In large population studies, people who eat apples and pears regularly tend to have lower rates of type 2 diabetes and heart disease than people who rarely eat them. These are patterns across whole diets, not proof that an apple by itself prevents disease — but they point the same way as the fibre and pigment content.
  • The whole tree gives in turn: blossom for the bees in spring, fruit for people in autumn, and pruned wood sweet enough for the smoke of a barbecue. The seeds are the one part not eaten — see the caution below.
  • Caution: apple seeds contain a compound called amygdalin, which can release cyanide when seeds are chewed and crushed. The dose in one apple is tiny — a fruit holds only five to eight seeds, and seeds swallowed whole pass through undigested. A person would have to chew the seeds of dozens of apples at one sitting to approach harm. Spit the seeds out or swallow them whole, either is fine; just do not grind up seeds on purpose.
  • When to plant: Plant a grafted tree while dormant, in spring — or autumn in mild districts. Only grafting keeps the variety true, and the rootstock decides the tree's final size
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Well-drained soil; most garden soils suit if they are not waterlogged
  • Environment: Needs winter chill to fruit well, and varieties are bred for different amounts of it — match the tree to your winters
  • Planter or ground: Ground; dwarf rootstocks suit a large patio pot
  • Spacing and depth: By rootstock: dwarf trees 2.5–3.5 m apart, full orchard trees 6 m or more
  • Water: Water well in the first two years and in dry spells; settled trees need little
  • Companion plants: Chives, garlic and nasturtiums are the classic orchard neighbours. Most kinds also need a different apple variety flowering nearby to set fruit
  • When to pick: Pick when the fruit parts from the tree with a gentle twist and the pips are brown — autumn for most kinds

Garden notes

  • Buy a grafted tree. The variety name is a promise only grafting can keep, and the rootstock — the roots the variety is grafted onto — decides the tree's final size, from a full orchard tree down to a patio dwarf.
  • Most varieties cannot pollinate themselves and need a different apple variety flowering nearby. One tree alone may blossom beautifully and set almost nothing; bees do the carrying between the two.
  • Apples need winter chill to fruit well, and varieties are bred for different amounts of it. Match the tree to your winters before you plant.
  • Plant in full sun, prune in winter to keep the centre of the tree open to light, and thin the young fruit in early summer to one apple per cluster — the tree will size those well and bear every year instead of every other.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for apples, raw, with skin
  • USDA flavonoid data — quercetin concentrated in the peel
  • Nature Genetics genome consortium (2010) — apple origin from Malus sieversii in the Tian Shan
  • Pooled Nurses' Health Study / Health Professionals cohort data — apple intake and diabetes and heart associations, stated as population patterns
  • Bolarinwa et al. (2014) — amygdalin content of apple seeds

Mango

Mangifera indica

CulinaryCraftFreshDriedFruitSeed

The mango is the fruit of a big evergreen tree in the cashew family — kin to cashew and pistachio, and a far-off cousin of poison ivy. It has been grown in South Asia for more than 4,000 years, and it is now the everyday fruit of the tropics: eaten ripe and sweet, green and sour, dried, juiced and pickled — and even its seed is pressed into a butter for the skin.

Varieties

Alphonso

Skin
Flesh

The prized mango of India's west coast, around Ratnagiri in Maharashtra: rich, creamy, almost fibre-free flesh with a scent nothing else matches. Its season is short — April to June — and it is priced like it. The mango other mangoes are judged by.

Kesar

Skin
Flesh

From Gujarat, named for its saffron-coloured flesh — kesar is the word for saffron. Sweet, smooth and juicy, it is India's other great eating mango, and the one most often canned as pulp.

Haden

Skin
Flesh

The Florida grandfather: raised in Coconut Grove in 1910 from an Indian Mulgoba seed, it parented most modern shipping mangoes. Firm, mild, sweet flesh under a red-blushed skin.

Tommy Atkins

Skin
Flesh

The supermarket mango of the Americas, a Florida seedling of Haden from the 1920s. Very firm, fibrous and mild, with a long shelf life and colour that promises more than the flesh gives — bred for shipping, not for taste. Best in smoothies, salsas and salads.

Kent

Skin
Flesh

A Florida variety of the 1940s: big, rich and sweet, with soft, low-fibre flesh. It stays mostly green even when ripe, so it is judged by feel and scent, not colour. A late-season fruit.

Keitt

Skin
Flesh

Another Florida variety of the 1940s, and the latest of all: large fruit that stays green when ripe, sweet with a little tartness and almost no fibre. It stretches the mango season into early autumn.

Ataulfo (Honey)

Skin
Flesh

Mexico's small golden mango, named for the grower Ataulfo Morales: smooth, fibre-free, honey-sweet flesh and a thin seed, so there is more fruit to the bite. Sold as the honey mango, or champagne mango.

Nam Dok Mai

Skin
Flesh

Thailand's best-known mango: long and slender, very sweet, with smooth, low-fibre flesh. Eaten ripe with coconut sticky rice — and eaten green and crisp, dipped in chilli sugar.

Around the World

India — aamras, pressed Alphonso pulp eaten with puffed puris in Maharashtra; and the green mango at work everywhere: sharp pickles, and amchur powder seasoning dishes all year.
Thailand — khao niao mamuang: ripe Nam Dok Mai over sweet coconut sticky rice; and crisp green mango with chilli-sugar dips at street stalls.
Mexico — mango on a stick from street carts, cut like a flower and dressed with lime, salt and chilli; the golden Ataulfo comes from the Soconusco coast of Chiapas.
Philippines — the Carabao mango, eaten ripe and sweet, dried into chewy export sweets; and green mango with bagoong, a salty shrimp paste.

Origin & Lineage

  • The mango's first home is the land between north-east India, Bangladesh and Myanmar, where wild mango trees still grow in the foothills. Growers there took it into gardens more than 4,000 years ago, and India has been its heartland ever since — it is the country's national fruit.
  • The name tells the journey: from the Tamil word for the fruit, māṅkāy, through Portuguese manga to mango. The Latin name, Mangifera indica, means roughly "the mango-bearer of India".
  • From India the mango travelled both ways. Traders and monks carried it east through Southeast Asia well over a thousand years ago; Persian and Arab sailors took it to the East African coast by about the 900s. Much later, Portuguese ships carried it to Brazil in the 1700s, Spanish galleons brought Southeast Asian seed from the Philippines to Mexico, and it reached Florida in the 1800s.
  • There are two great families of mango. Indian kinds carry one embryo in the seed and give rich, coloured, strong-flavoured fruit; a seedling grows up different from its parent, so every named Indian variety is kept alive by grafting. Southeast Asian kinds carry several embryos, grow true from seed, and give paler, very sweet fruit with less resin in the taste.
  • Florida breeders crossed the two families in the early 1900s and produced the firm, colourful shipping mangoes — Haden first, in 1910, then its seedlings Kent, Keitt and Tommy Atkins — that fill supermarket shelves today.

History

  • Tradition in India holds that the Buddha was given a mango grove at Vaishali by the courtesan Amrapali, and rested in its shade. That is tradition, told here as it is told.
  • Fresh mango leaves are still strung over doorways across India at festivals — a toranam, a hanging of leaves for welcome and good fortune — and mango leaves and blossom appear in offerings. The tree is treated as a tree of plenty.
  • The Mughal emperor Akbar, in the 1500s, is said to have planted the Lakhi Bagh near Darbhanga: an orchard of a hundred thousand mango trees. A lakh is a hundred thousand — the name alone tells the scale of the planting.
  • In Ayurveda, India's old system of medicine, the ripe fruit, the leaves and the bark each had their uses, and unripe mango was dried into amchur to keep its tang in the kitchen all year. These are historical uses, recorded as history.
  • The paisley pattern's curved teardrop is widely said to be drawn from the shape of a mango. The claim is repeated so often that it is best carried as tradition.

Traditional medicine

  • The leaves are used fresh in garlands and in strings over doorways in India (see History). As a tea they are a folk remedy in parts of Asia and the Caribbean — tradition, not tested medicine.
  • The bark is astringent, was used in folk medicine, and gives a brown dye. Neither leaf nor bark is a food.
  • Vitamin C leads: about 36 mg per 100 g of flesh, roughly 40% of the daily value, at only about 60 kcal per 100 g.
  • The deep orange colour is carotenoids, chiefly beta-carotene — the pigment the body turns into vitamin A. A 100 g serving gives about 54 µg of vitamin A, roughly 6% of the daily value. This flesh is where the mango's vitamins A and C truly live.
  • Fibre is modest but real — about 1.6 g per 100 g, much of it soluble — and it supports digestion. Against that, the flesh is sweet, about 14 g of sugar per 100 g, so portions are worth a thought for anyone watching blood sugar.
  • In the background: potassium (about 168 mg per 100 g) and folate (about 43 µg, roughly a tenth of the daily value).
  • Green, unripe mango is a food in its own right — crisp, sour and rich in vitamin C — eaten with salt and chilli across Asia, and dried into amchur, the sour powder that seasons Indian cooking.
  • Inside the flat stone sits the kernel. Pressed, it gives a pale, nearly scentless fat — mango butter — that melts at 32–36 °C, right around skin temperature, so it spreads on skin and melts in. It is used in skin care and in soap making.
  • The butter carries a little vitamin E (tocopherols, up to about 148 mg in a kilogram of the fat) — a modest amount. It does not carry the fruit's vitamins A or C in any real amount: those stay behind when the fat is pressed out of the kernel, so claims that mango butter is rich in vitamins A and C mix up the flesh with the fat.
  • The kernel itself is ground into flour and eaten in parts of India, mostly in lean times. It is bitter and astringent, and it is not a common food.
  • Caution: the peel, and the milky sap in the fruit's stem, carry small amounts of the same family of irritant oils found in poison ivy — the mango's own family. Most people eat mango without trouble, but anyone who reacts strongly to poison ivy, cashew or pistachio may find the peel makes the lips or skin itch. The flesh inside is the safe part: peel the fruit and keep the sap off your skin. If your mouth or throat swells, stop — that is an allergy, and it should be taken seriously.
  • When to plant: Plant a grafted tree in spring, after all frost risk — a grafted tree can bear in about three years; a seedling is an unknown tree and may take six to eight
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Deep, well-drained soil; it tolerates poor soil once settled
  • Environment: It wants the tropics: heat and no frost — a young tree is damaged near freezing. A dry spell helps it flower
  • Planter or ground: Ground only in frost-free places; small grafted kinds such as Cogshall and Pickering suit large pots elsewhere
  • Spacing and depth: Garden trees need 6–10 m; prune to picking height
  • Water: Water young trees regularly; settled trees stand drought but fruit better with summer water
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Pick mature and firm, about 3–5 months after flowering, and ripen on the counter — a ripe mango gives a little under the thumb and smells sweet at the stem end

Garden notes

  • A mango tree wants the tropics: full sun, heat, and no frost. A young tree is damaged near freezing and even an old one suffers in a hard frost, so in the ground it belongs only in the warmest places — south Florida, Hawaii, Puerto Rico and their like. Elsewhere, a small grafted variety in a large pot, moved under cover for winter, is the way.
  • Left alone, a seedling tree can pass 30 m (100 ft) tall with a canopy as wide, and trees said to be 300 years old still fruit. Garden trees are grafted kinds, pruned to picking height; kinds bred small for pots and patios include 'Cogshall' and 'Pickering'.
  • Plant a grafted tree if you want the variety on its label: it can bear in about three years. A seedling of an Indian kind is a new, unknown tree that may take six to eight years to fruit — though Southeast Asian kinds grow true from seed.
  • It flowers in the dry season and ripens its fruit in summer. A dry spell helps it flower; wet weather at flowering feeds anthracnose, a fungal disease that spots blossom and fruit, and the mango grower's main enemy.
  • Pick mature and firm, and ripen on the counter: a ripe mango gives a little under the thumb and smells sweet at the stem end. Kent and Keitt stay green, so judge those by feel and scent.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for mangoes, raw
  • Morton (1987) — "Mango", in Fruits of Warm Climates; origin, spread and the Florida varieties
  • Mwaurah et al. (2020) — mango kernel fat reviewed; tocopherols (vitamin E) up to about 148 mg/kg of the fat
  • Berghea et al. (2021) — mango in the cashew family; skin reactions in people already sensitive to poison ivy

Burdock

Arctium lappa

MedicinalCulinaryCraftCookedRootLeaf

A biennial pioneer of disturbed ground — waste places, roadsides and field edges. The root is a food as much as a medicine, eaten as a vegetable in Japan, and the purple, thistle-like flowers feed bees through the summer.

History

  • Burdock has been a medicine in Europe for centuries. The older herbals call it a blood purifier — a herb taken to clear the blood — and reach for it for skin troubles and stiff joints. That name records a belief, not a tested finding.
  • China uses a different part. The seed, called niubangzi, has a long place in Chinese medicine, used for sore throats, colds and skin rashes.
  • Japan turned the root into food. Grown there for centuries as gobo, it is bred long and thin, dug young and cooked as a vegetable — the same root Europe took as a medicine.
  • Medieval herbals used the root and leaves for swellings and sores, and the leaf stayed in folk practice as a poultice long after.
  • The plant is native to Europe and Asia. It crossed to North America with European settlers and now grows on waste ground across the temperate world.

Traditional medicine

  • European regulators recognise the root as a traditional herbal medicine to increase the flow of urine and flush the urinary tract, as an aid in minor urinary complaints.
  • The same monograph lists it for temporary loss of appetite.
  • It is also traditionally used for seborrhoeic skin conditions — oily, flaky skin.
  • Older herbals call burdock a “blood purifier” and a detox herb; that idea comes from tradition, not testing — there is no evidence it treats cancer, diabetes or infections.
  • In folk medicine, the leaves were used as a poultice.

Craft

  • Burdock is a dye plant: its leaves give yellow to tan shades on wool and cloth — soft, earthy colours from a plant most people only dig out.
  • The root is a food as much as a medicine — in Japan it is eaten as a vegetable, called gobo, and it is rich in the fibre inulin.
  • The young leaves can be cooked and eaten, though they are bitter.
  • Caution: buy burdock root rather than gathering it wild — the root is easily confused with the root of deadly nightshade (belladonna), and commercial burdock teas have been poisoned by that mix-up. Burdock is in the daisy family, so avoid it if you are allergic to daisy-family plants such as chrysanthemums, and avoid it in pregnancy, as it may stimulate the womb. Take care alongside diabetes medicines — burdock lowered blood sugar in animal studies — and herbal references advise care with blood-thinning medicines too.
  • When to plant: Usually gathered wild. To grow it, sow seed outside in spring where it will stay — the deep taproot resents moving
  • Sun: Full sun or some shade
  • Soil: Deep, loose soil, so the long root can grow straight; it tolerates poor, disturbed ground
  • Environment: A hardy biennial — leaves the first year, flowers the second; a pioneer of waste places and roadsides
  • Planter or ground: Ground only — the root grows to about three feet
  • Spacing and depth: Sow 1 cm deep; thin to 45 cm apart
  • Water: Average — drought-tolerant once the taproot is down
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Dig the root in the autumn of the first year, while it is still tender; young leaves are picked in spring

Garden notes

  • A biennial pioneer of disturbed ground — waste places, roadsides and field edges — covering bare soil where little else is established.
  • Its deep taproot, growing to about three feet, drives down through compacted soil and helps break it up.
  • The purple, thistle-like flowers feed bees and other pollinators through the summer.
  • An honest downside: the hooked burrs — the fastening that inspired Velcro — tangle in fur and feathers and have trapped small birds and bats, and the long-lived seed makes burdock hard to remove once it settles in.

Sources

  • European Medicines Agency, burdock root monograph — traditional use for urinary flushing, loss of appetite and seborrhoeic skin
  • Memorial Sloan Kettering, About Herbs — no evidence for cancer, infection or diabetes claims
  • Bryson et al. (1978) — burdock tea contaminated with belladonna root (atropine)
  • NC State Extension, Plant Toolbox — plant habit, taproot and seed dispersal

Calendula

Calendula officinalis
