MedicinalCulinaryCraftFreshCookedFlower

The classic salve flower — calendula-infused oil is the heart of many herbal salves and balms. The petals are edible too, with a mild peppery tang.

History

  • The name is a calendar. Calendula is tied to the Latin calendae, the first day of the month, because the plant flowers through so much of the year that blooms could be found at the start of almost any month.
  • Its written record goes back at least to the 1100s. Hildegard of Bingen, the German abbess and herbal writer, described it in her book of medicines.
  • In medieval gardens it was the pot marigold: a kitchen-garden flower whose petals coloured broths, rice and cheese a golden yellow — the poor cook's saffron.
  • Herbalists used the flowers on the skin for wounds, burns and sores, and that wound use carried on through the centuries into the salves it is still known for.
  • Because it flowers from late spring to the first frosts, it became one of the most planted cottage-garden flowers in Europe, and travellers carried it to gardens worldwide.

Traditional medicine

  • European regulators recognise the flower as a traditional herbal medicine for minor inflammations of the skin, such as sunburn.
  • The same monograph lists it as an aid in the healing of minor wounds — a use documented since at least the 12th century.
  • It is also traditionally used for minor inflammation in the mouth or throat, as a gargle or rinse.

Craft

  • The petals dye wool and cloth a clear yellow, and were once used to colour butter and cheese.
  • Calendula-infused oil — petals steeped in oil until it turns deep gold — is the base of homemade salves, balms and soaps.
  • The petals are edible, with a mild peppery tang, and have long been used to add golden colour to foods — the “poor man's saffron”.
  • Caution: calendula is in the daisy family — do not use it if you are allergic to daisy-family plants such as ragweed or chamomile. The EMA does not recommend it in pregnancy or while breastfeeding, as its safety there has not been established.
  • When to plant: Sow outside in spring, or start indoors 4–6 weeks before the last frost; it self-seeds freely and returns year after year
  • Sun: Full sun, or a little shade in hot summers
  • Soil: Ordinary, well-drained soil; not fussy
  • Environment: A hardy annual, in bloom from late spring to the first frosts
  • Planter or ground: Both — pots suit it well
  • Spacing and depth: Sow 1 cm deep; 25–30 cm apart
  • Water: Average
  • Companion plants: A valued companion among vegetables: it works as a decoy, drawing aphids onto itself, and pulls in the hoverflies and lacewings whose young hunt aphids
  • When to pick: Pick the flowers as they open fully, all season — keep picking to keep them coming

Garden notes

  • One of the longest-flowering plants in the garden — in bloom from late spring to the first frosts, feeding bees and other pollinators for months.
  • The flowers draw in hoverflies and lacewings, whose larvae hunt aphids — a standing patrol for the vegetable garden.
  • Grown as a companion plant among vegetables, it also works as a decoy, drawing aphids onto itself and away from crops.
  • It self-seeds freely and returns year after year in the same spot, though the seedlings are shallow-rooted and easy to pull where they are not wanted; its taproot helps loosen the soil.

Sources

  • European Medicines Agency, calendula flower monograph — traditional use for minor skin inflammation, minor wounds, and mouth and throat inflammation
  • European Medicines Agency, calendula assessment report — documented skin use since the 12th century; Commission E approval (1986)
  • Garden Planner companion guide — pollinator value and aphid trap-crop use

Cannabis

Cannabis sativa

MedicinalCulinaryCraftFlowerLeafRootSeed

A tall, fast-growing annual in the hemp family, Cannabaceae — the same family as hops — native to Central Asia and now grown in temperate regions all over the world. Cannabis is a single species with two very different crops bred from it: hemp, grown for fibre and seed and bred to make almost none of the intoxicating compound THC, and drug cannabis, bred to make a great deal of it. Which of the two a plant is depends on its breeding and its chemistry, not on a different species name. Most plants are either male or female, and it is the female flower that carries the plant's richest store of active compounds, held in a sticky resin coating the flowers.

The parts divide by purpose. The female flower is the medicinal and intoxicating part. The seed — hemp seed — is a straightforward food, rich in protein and oil, and carries no meaningful amount of THC or CBD, because those compounds are made in the flower, not in the seed. The stem gives one of the oldest textile fibres in the world. The leaf holds far less of the active compounds than the flower and is mostly a by-product, and the root has a small place in old medicine and in modern laboratory research.

The plant makes over a hundred related compounds called cannabinoids. Two dominate. THC (tetrahydrocannabinol) is the one that intoxicates: it changes perception, mood, appetite and the sense of time. CBD (cannabidiol) does not intoxicate, and is the basis of one licensed medicine. The plant also makes terpenes — the aromatic compounds that give many plants their scent, the same family of compounds found in lemon peel, pine resin and lavender. Terpenes give each cannabis variety its characteristic smell; whether they change the plant's effects in people is not established.

History

  • One of the oldest cultivated plants in the world. Cannabis was domesticated in Central or East Asia thousands of years ago: hemp was among China's earliest fibre crops, its seed eaten and its stem spun, and the plant appears in the earliest Chinese herbal traditions.
  • The Greek historian Herodotus, writing around 440 BCE, described the Scythians of the steppe throwing cannabis seed onto hot stones inside tents and inhaling the vapour — an early written record of the intoxicating use standing alongside the fibre use.
  • From Asia the plant travelled every trade route: into India, where it entered Ayurvedic medicine and ritual; through the Middle East; into Europe as a fibre crop; and to the Americas with colonists, who grew hemp for rope and sailcloth.
  • Western medicine took it up in the 1840s, when the Irish physician William O'Shaughnessy brought cannabis preparations back from India and reported their use for pain, spasm and convulsions. Cannabis tinctures then sat in Western pharmacies for the rest of the century.
  • The twentieth century reversed that. The United States taxed cannabis out of ordinary commerce in 1937 and prohibited it outright in 1970, and the 1961 United Nations Single Convention spread prohibition worldwide. Hemp farming was mostly swept up with the drug plant.
  • In recent decades the two crops have separated again in law: the United States removed hemp from its controlled-substances law in 2018, several countries license cannabinoid medicines, and a few — Uruguay and Canada first — have legalised the drug plant itself for adults.

Craft

  • The stem gives hemp fibre, one of the oldest textile fibres in the world: stripped, retted and spun, it makes cloth, rope, twine and paper. Fibre hemp is bred tall and straight for exactly this — a separate crop from drug cannabis.
  • The strongest evidence in this entry belongs not to the plant but to medicines made from single compounds taken out of it. A purified CBD medicine, Epidiolex, is licensed for seizures in two rare, severe childhood epilepsies — Lennox-Gastaut syndrome and Dravet syndrome — where trials showed it reduced seizure frequency substantially more than placebo. That is evidence for that medicine, at that dose, in those conditions. It is not evidence for over-the-counter CBD oil, and not for the plant.
  • Laboratory-made versions of THC (dronabinol and nabilone) are licensed medicines for the nausea and vomiting caused by cancer chemotherapy, and dronabinol also for appetite loss in people with HIV/AIDS. The trial evidence behind them is older and moderate in strength.
  • A mouth spray combining THC and CBD (nabiximols) is licensed in several countries — though not the United States — for muscle stiffness and spasm in multiple sclerosis. Reviews find it probably reduces how severe the spasticity feels in the short term; measured stiffness improves less clearly.
  • For long-term pain, pooled reviews find that cannabis and cannabinoid medicines give a small average benefit: in one large review of non-cancer pain, 29% of people taking them achieved a meaningful 30% reduction in pain, against 26% on placebo. Real, but small — and bought with more side effects, such as dizziness and drowsiness.
  • For sleep, the evidence is modest and mixed. A major evidence review found moderate support for short-term sleep improvement in people whose sleep is disturbed by particular conditions — chronic pain, multiple sclerosis, fibromyalgia and sleep apnoea — and little beyond that. Cannabis is not an established treatment for insomnia, and heavy long-term use can make sleep worse.
  • For anxiety, the evidence points both ways. Small, short studies suggest CBD alone may ease anxiety in specific situations, such as public speaking. THC, by contrast, commonly causes anxiety and panic at higher doses, and heavy cannabis use is associated with higher rates of anxiety disorders. No anxiety treatment is established here.
  • Hemp seed is a food, and a good one: roughly a quarter protein and a third oil, with an unusually favourable balance of the essential fats omega-6 and omega-3, plus a rarer fat, gamma-linolenic acid, that few foods carry. It does not intoxicate and makes no medicinal claims.
  • Caution — dependence: cannabis can be habit-forming. About one in ten people who use it develop dependence, rising to roughly one in six among those who start as teenagers, and higher still in daily users. Withdrawal is real but milder than with alcohol or opioids — irritability, poor sleep and low appetite for a week or two.
  • Caution — mental health: the most serious risk sits with the mind. Large studies consistently link cannabis use — above all daily use of high-strength, high-THC cannabis, and use beginning in the teenage years — with higher rates of psychosis, including schizophrenia. Whether cannabis causes the illness or brings it forward in people already vulnerable is still argued, but the association is strong and grows with dose and strength, and it is the reason teenagers and anyone with a family history of psychosis are advised to avoid cannabis entirely.
  • Caution — pregnancy: do not use cannabis in pregnancy or while breastfeeding. THC crosses the placenta and passes into breast milk, use in pregnancy is linked with lower birth weight, and obstetric guidance advises against it outright.
  • Caution — driving: cannabis impairs driving and machinery use — reaction time, attention and coordination all suffer, and crash risk rises. The impairment outlasts the feeling of being affected.
  • Caution — interactions and lungs: CBD and THC interfere with the liver system that clears many medicines, and can change blood levels of blood thinners such as warfarin, some epilepsy and transplant medicines, and some antidepressants; CBD at medicine doses has also been linked with liver injury. Their effects add up with alcohol and sedative medicines. Smoked cannabis irritates the airways, and regular smoking is linked with chronic bronchitis.
  • Caution — the law: the law on cannabis differs sharply from place to place and changes often. The same plant that is a licensed medicine or a legal product in one jurisdiction is a criminal offence in another. Hemp is the legal exception in many places, defined by a cap on THC — 0.3% in United States law. Check the law where you are.
  • When to plant: Sow or set out in spring, after frost. Check the law where you are first — it differs sharply from place to place
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Deep, fertile, well-drained soil close to neutral; it sulks in wet, heavy ground
  • Environment: A temperate annual with a deep taproot that breaks up compacted soil; wind-pollinated, and the pollen is a notable allergen for some people
  • Planter or ground: Ground; large pots for single plants
  • Spacing and depth: 1 m or more apart — set out in spring, it can pass three metres by late summer
  • Water: Average — water young plants in dry spells
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Seed is gathered when the hulls harden, in autumn; fibre hemp is cut at flowering

Garden notes

  • Native to Central Asia and happiest in temperate climates: it wants full sun and a deep, fertile, well-drained soil close to neutral, and sulks in wet, heavy ground.
  • A true annual with a deep taproot: set out in spring it can pass three metres by late summer, and the root breaks up and aerates compacted soil as it goes.
  • The fast, dense canopy shades out weeds — one reason fibre hemp is usually grown with few pesticide sprays, since few pests trouble it.
  • Wind-pollinated: male plants shed clouds of pollen in late summer, a notable allergen for some people. The seed is rich bird food, and hemp seed has long gone into bird feed.
  • The plant takes up heavy metals from the soil into its tissues, which is why it is studied for cleaning contaminated land and has been trialled for that purpose, including around Chernobyl. That same appetite is a reason food crops of it belong on clean soil.

Sources

  • Kew, Plants of the World Online — Cannabis sativa, family placement and native range
  • National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine (2017) — The Health Effects of Cannabis and Cannabinoids, the comprehensive evidence review
  • Whiting et al. (2015) — systematic review and meta-analysis of cannabinoids for medical use, JAMA
  • Di Forti et al. (2019) — study of daily and high-potency cannabis use and psychotic disorder across Europe, The Lancet Psychiatry
  • Callaway (2004) — hempseed as a nutritional resource, Euphytica
  • Clarke & Merlin (2013) — Cannabis: Evolution and Ethnobotany, domestication and spread
  • American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists (2017) — committee opinion on cannabis use in pregnancy and breastfeeding
  • US Food and Drug Administration (2018) — approval of Epidiolex for Lennox-Gastaut and Dravet syndromes
  • National Center for Complementary and Integrative Health (2019) — Cannabis (Marijuana) and Cannabinoids: What You Need To Know

Chickweed

Stellaria media

MedicinalCulinaryFreshCooked

A low, mat-forming spring plant. The whole plant is edible and has long been eaten as a spring green, and it has a traditional place on the skin as well as in teas.

History

  • The name tells who ate it first. Chickweed is named for the chickens that feed on it eagerly, and bird keepers gathered it for caged birds too.
  • People ate it as well. For centuries in Europe it was a spring green, picked young and eaten raw or cooked when little else was growing.
  • The herbal writers of the 1500s and 1600s — Gerard and Culpeper among them — praised it as a cooling herb, laid on hot, itchy skin.
  • That skin use stayed in folk practice: a fresh poultice of chickweed for rashes, bites and irritation is still its best-known home remedy. It rests on tradition, not on trials.

Traditional medicine

  • Traditionally used on the skin — as a fresh poultice, wash or salve — to soothe itching, minor rashes, irritation and insect bites. This use rests on tradition; it has not been tested in controlled human trials.
  • Traditionally taken as a tea to soothe the throat and digestive tract.
  • The whole plant is edible and has long been eaten as a spring green. It contains vitamin C, flavonoids and minerals including calcium, iron and potassium.
  • Contains natural saponins, the soap-like compounds behind its soothing, slippery feel on the skin.
  • Caution: eaten in large amounts, the saponins can upset the stomach. Gather only from clean ground — plants from heavily fertilised soil can build up nitrates — and avoid medicinal use in pregnancy, as its safety has not been established.
  • When to plant: Usually gathered wild. To grow it, scatter seed in spring on fertile, moist ground and let it form its mat
  • Sun: Some shade suits it; it grows in sun where the soil stays moist
  • Soil: Fertile, moist soil — its presence is a sign of both
  • Environment: A cool-season plant; it fades in hot, dry weather and returns in autumn
  • Planter or ground: Ground; a wide pot keeps it tidy
  • Spacing and depth: Scatter seed on the surface and press it in — it needs light to sprout
  • Water: Likes water — keep it moist
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Gather the leafy tops while they are fresh and green, in spring and again in autumn

Garden notes

  • Forms a dense, low mat that works as a living mulch, shading the soil, holding moisture and protecting the surface from erosion.
  • Its presence is a sign of fertile, moist soil.
  • The tiny seeds are eaten by songbirds such as sparrows, finches and doves, and the flowers give early nectar and pollen to small bees and hoverflies.
  • When it dies back or is turned into the soil, it returns the minerals it gathered to the topsoil.

Sources

  • Singh et al. (2022) — review of chickweed pharmacology; evidence from animal and laboratory models
  • Drugs.com, chickweed monograph — traditional uses; no clinical evidence to guide dosage
  • WebMD, chickweed — no good scientific evidence for metabolic claims
  • Plants For A Future — ground cover, erosion control and wildlife value

Cleavers

Galium aparine

MedicinalCulinaryCraftFresh

A scrambling, tangled spring herb — also called goosegrass, because geese and poultry graze the leaves. It is gathered fresh in spring, as a classic spring tonic herb in European tradition.

History

  • Greek and Roman writers knew the plant: Dioscorides described a clinging Galium, used in the medicine of his time.
  • Cleavers sits in the coffee family, the Rubiaceae — the same family as coffee and madder.
  • Its seeds were once roasted and ground as a coffee substitute, the closest thing to coffee the hedgerow offers.
  • In European herbals it was a spring herb. Culpeper, writing in the 1600s, set it down as a spring tonic, taken when the plant comes up fresh to clear the body after winter.
  • Country practice kept that rhythm: cleavers was gathered each spring for teas and spring greens, and used as a wash for irritated skin.

Traditional medicine

  • A classic spring tonic herb in European herbal tradition, gathered fresh in spring.
  • Traditionally used as a gentle diuretic — to support urine flow and ease minor fluid retention and urinary discomfort.
  • Herbalists traditionally class cleavers as a lymphatic herb, used for swollen glands. This rests on traditional practice alone; there are no human trials showing that it changes lymph flow.
  • Traditionally used as a wash or poultice to calm irritated skin.

Craft

  • Cleavers sits in the madder family, and like madder its roots hold a red dye. The roots are thin and fiddly to gather, so it was always a small-scale dye — pink-red shades on wool for those with patience.
  • The young shoots have traditionally been eaten as a spring green, and the plant contains vitamin C.
  • Caution: cleavers has not had formal safety testing. It is not recommended in pregnancy or while breastfeeding, and care should be taken alongside diuretic medicines.
  • When to plant: Usually gathered wild. To grow it, sow seed in spring or autumn in a corner where it can scramble
  • Sun: Some shade — it is a hedgerow plant
  • Soil: Moist, rich soil
  • Environment: A hardy annual that climbs and tangles over its neighbours, giving low cover for wildlife
  • Planter or ground: Ground — give it a fence or shrubs to climb
  • Spacing and depth: Sow 1 cm deep; a few plants fill a corner
  • Water: Likes moisture
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Gather the young shoots in spring, before the stems toughen

Garden notes

  • A food plant for the caterpillars of many moths and butterflies, including the hummingbird hawk-moth.
  • Birds eat the seeds, and geese and poultry graze the leaves — the source of its other name, goosegrass.
  • Its scrambling, tangled growth gives low cover and shelter for small insects and other wildlife.
  • Quickly covers bare, disturbed ground, helping to hold the soil in place.

Sources

  • EarthWise, Cleavers Clinical Guide — traditional spring tonic and diuretic use; almost no modern human research
  • Herbal Reality and PeaceHealth herbal references — mild diuretic traditional use
  • Plants For A Future — larval food plant and wildlife value
  • Dowlati Beirami et al. (2024) — cleavers extract and wound closure in cultured skin cells (laboratory only)

Clover

Trifolium repens

MedicinalCulinaryDriedLeafFlower

White clover is a creeping perennial in the pea family, native to Europe and Central Asia and now naturalised in lawns and pastures worldwide, rooting as it spreads to form a soft green carpet. Its flowers and leaves are the parts traditionally used — and it should not be confused with red clover, a different plant with different uses.

History

  • In folk practice, a tea of the flowers and leaves was a spring tonic and a remedy for coughs and colds — a gentle household herb rather than a strong medicine.
  • Tradition says the rare four-leaf clover brings luck. Most leaves have three leaflets; the four-leafed ones are uncommon enough that finding one became a sign of good fortune.
  • Tradition also links clover to Ireland's shamrock, the three-leafed plant of St Patrick. Which plant the true shamrock is has been argued over for centuries — white clover is one of the plants claimed, and the question is still open.
  • Its farming history spread it around the world. From the 1600s and 1700s, farmers sowed white clover into pastures with grass, and wherever pasture farming went, clover followed.

Traditional medicine

  • In folk herbalism, a tea of the flowers and leaves has traditionally been used as a gentle expectorant — a remedy that helps loosen phlegm — for coughs, colds and chesty congestion. This is a traditional use; white clover has barely been studied in clinical research.
  • The same flower tea was a traditional spring “tonic,” taken as a general pick-me-up in folk practice.
  • The flowers and leaves are edible and mildly nutritious: foraging guides note small amounts of vitamins A and C, and the plant contains a range of antioxidant plant compounds.
  • When to plant: Sow seed outside in spring, or late summer, into a prepared patch or lawn
  • Sun: Full sun or some shade
  • Soil: Ordinary soil, even poor — it fixes its own nitrogen and feeds the soil as it grows
  • Environment: A hardy perennial; its creeping roots and dense cover loosen and protect the surface soil
  • Planter or ground: Ground — lawns, patches and green cover
  • Spacing and depth: Scatter seed thinly and rake it in lightly
  • Water: Average — drought-tolerant once settled
  • Companion plants: Grass is its classic partner in lawns and pasture; it feeds the plants growing with it
  • When to pick: Pick the flowers as they open through summer; the leaves are gathered young

Garden notes

  • Bacteria living in nodules on its roots take nitrogen from the air and turn it into a form plants can use, steadily fertilising the clover itself and the plants growing with it — the reason clover has been sown into pastures for centuries.
  • Its creeping roots and dense cover loosen and protect the surface soil, and ground under clover is typically lively with earthworms.
  • The flowers are one of the most important nectar sources for honeybees and bumblebees in many regions.

Sources

  • Kew, Plants of the World Online — accepted name and native range (Europe and Central Asia; introduced and naturalised worldwide)
  • Ogle & St. John (2008) — USDA NRCS Plant Guide for white clover: root-nodule nitrogen fixation, erosion-control cover, pasture use, and honeybees collecting nectar and pollen
  • Plants For A Future — white clover entry: leaves and flowers edible; an infusion traditionally used for coughs and colds; the plant classed as a tonic
  • van Eekeren et al. (2009) — earthworm abundance and burrowing greater under white clover than under grass alone
  • Kicel & Wolbiś (2012) — twelve flavonoids isolated from the flowers and leaves, several showing strong antioxidant activity in laboratory tests

Comfrey

Symphytum officinale

MedicinalCraftExternal use only

Comfrey is for the skin only — never eat it, and never drink it as a tea. Its old folk name is “knitbone”, from its traditional use on injuries, and its root and leaves contain allantoin, a compound also used in skin creams.

History

  • Comfrey has been used on injuries for about 2,000 years. Dioscorides, the Greek physician whose herbal guided medicine for centuries, described it in the first century for wounds and broken bones.
  • Its Latin name carries the claim. Symphytum comes from the Greek symphyo — to grow together — the name of a plant meant to knit flesh and bone back together.
  • In England that claim became its folk name: knitbone. Country practice laid comfrey poultices on sprains, bruises and fractures for centuries.
  • Monastery and physic gardens grew it through the Middle Ages, and the herbal writers who followed kept it in their books as a wound herb.
  • The old use was on the skin, and that is where it has stayed: the root and leaves were poultice herbs, and modern comfrey is for external use only.

Traditional medicine

  • European regulators recognise the root as a traditional herbal medicine for the skin: for the relief of minor sprains and bruises, applied as a thin layer twice a day.
  • Its old folk name is “knitbone”, from its traditional use on injuries — but a broken bone needs medical care, not a poultice.

Craft

  • The leaves give a yellow-green dye for wool and cloth — one more use for a plant that makes leaves faster than most gardens can use them.
  • Comfrey is for the skin only — never eat it, and never drink it as a tea.
  • In clinical trials of regulated pharmacy ointments made from comfrey root, it eased ankle sprains about as well as a standard anti-inflammatory gel, and helped back and knee pain — that evidence belongs to those tested preparations, not to home-made poultices.
  • The root and leaves contain allantoin, a compound also used in skin creams, which is part of why comfrey has long been a salve herb.
  • Caution: comfrey contains pyrrolizidine alkaloids that can seriously damage the liver. In 2001 the US Food and Drug Administration advised makers to take comfrey products meant to be swallowed off the market, and regulators require “External Use Only” warnings. Use it only on unbroken skin — never on cuts or broken or irritated skin — for no more than 10 days at a time, and not in pregnancy or while breastfeeding, or for anyone under 18. Be certain of the plant before using it: comfrey leaves have been mistaken for foxglove, which is poisonous.
  • When to plant: Plant root cuttings or a young plant in spring. Plant the sterile kind Bocking 14, which does not spread by seed
  • Sun: Full sun or some shade
  • Soil: Deep, rich, moist soil; the taproot can go down several feet, drawing up minerals other plants cannot reach
  • Environment: A very hardy perennial — and extremely vigorous, and very hard to remove once established, so plant it where it can stay for good
  • Planter or ground: Ground only — far too deep-rooted and vigorous for pots
  • Spacing and depth: 90 cm apart — one plant makes a large clump
  • Water: Likes water, though the deep roots find much of their own
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known — but its cut leaves feed the whole garden as a mulch, a compost activator and a liquid feed
  • When to pick: Cut the leaves from late spring, two or three times a season, leaving some for the bees

Garden notes

  • A “dynamic accumulator”: its deep taproot — often several feet down — draws up potassium, calcium and phosphorus from deep in the soil where other plants cannot reach.
  • The cut leaves are rich in potassium and are used as a mulch, a compost activator and a liquid plant feed.
  • The hanging bell flowers are worked through spring and summer by long-tongued bumblebees, which can reach the nectar.
  • An honest warning for the garden: comfrey is extremely vigorous and very hard to remove once established — the sterile variety ‘Bocking 14’ is the one to plant, as it does not spread by seed.

Sources

  • European Medicines Agency, comfrey root monograph — external use only, minor sprains and bruises, with the 10-day and unbroken-skin limits
  • US Food and Drug Administration (2001) — manufacturers advised to remove comfrey products for internal use; liver damage from pyrrolizidine alkaloids
  • Memorial Sloan Kettering, About Herbs — hepatotoxicity and confusion with foxglove
  • Cochrane review of topical herbal therapies (2013) — trial support limited to regulated comfrey root ointments

Curly Dock

Rumex crispus

MedicinalCraftDriedRoot

Also called Yellow Dock. A pioneer of disturbed ground with a strong taproot that can reach about four feet down, and tall stems of reddish-brown seed.

History

  • Dock roots were used in Greek and Roman medicine, and dock has stayed in herbal practice ever since — a tonic and a gentle laxative.
  • Native American peoples used curly dock in their own practice: the root as a tonic, and the plant for skin troubles and sores.
  • In lean times the seeds were food. Gathered in quantity and ground, they made a dark flour for bread and porridge when grain ran short.
  • The root was once a shop herb — dried and sold as a tonic and laxative — and its other name, yellow dock, comes from the yellow root.
  • It crossed the Atlantic with European settlers and spread across North America, where it now grows on waste ground almost everywhere.

Traditional medicine

  • The root was a traditional gentle laxative, listed by the British Herbal Pharmacopoeia for constipation — its anthraquinone compounds, a family of plant compounds that stimulate the bowel, are what move the bowels.
  • The same pharmacopoeia listed the root for liver congestion and for skin eruptions associated with constipation — the old idea that clearing the bowels clears the skin.
  • Native American tribes used the root as a general tonic and “blood purifier”, and as a source of iron for women. The root does contain iron, but in moderate amounts, and dock is not a treatment for anaemia.
  • Traditionally used as a tonic herb — the root was once sold as a tonic and laxative.

Craft

  • The roots give a yellow dye for wool and cloth — the same strong root that medicine uses, put to the dyer’s work.
  • Caution: the plant contains oxalates, natural compounds found in many plants that can add to kidney stones in people prone to them. As a laxative it is for short-term use only — overuse causes diarrhoea and digestive disturbance — and it should be avoided in pregnancy and while breastfeeding, and not combined with other laxatives.
  • When to plant: Usually gathered wild. To grow it, sow seed in spring in a contained corner — its seed stays alive in the soil for decades
  • Sun: Full sun or some shade
  • Soil: Almost any soil, including heavy, compacted ground; the strong taproot can reach about four feet down
  • Environment: A hardy perennial, and a pioneer of disturbed ground
  • Planter or ground: Ground only
  • Spacing and depth: Sow on the surface or just below; 45 cm apart
  • Water: Average — the deep root finds moisture
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Pick the leaves young, in spring; dig the root in autumn

Garden notes

  • Its strong taproot can reach about four feet down, drawing up moisture from deep in the soil and helping to break up and aerate compacted ground.
  • The tall stems carry masses of reddish-brown seed that songbirds — sparrows and red-winged blackbirds among them — feed on.
  • The leaves are food for caterpillars, and the plant is a larval host for copper butterflies.
  • A pioneer of disturbed ground whose seeds stay viable in the soil for decades — up to sixty years — which is why dock keeps reappearing in gardens.

Sources

  • GLOBINMED, curly dock monograph — root constituents and traditional uses
  • British Herbal Pharmacopoeia — dock root for constipation and related traditional uses
  • Plants For A Future — gentle purgative and tonic actions recorded
  • University of Colorado — larval host for copper butterflies; seed longevity and oxalate toxicity to livestock

Dandelion

Taraxacum officinale

MedicinalCulinaryCraftFreshDriedRootLeafFlower

The dandelion is a daisy-family perennial from Europe and Asia, carried by wind and people to lawns and roadsides on every continent. Almost uniquely among familiar plants, the whole thing is used — leaves, root and flowers are all food, and the root and leaf are both traditional medicines, in different ways.

History

  • The name is French: dent-de-lion, lion's tooth, for the jagged edges of the leaves.
  • Physicians in the Arab world wrote about the dandelion around 1,000 years ago, and from their books it passed into European medicine.
  • In Europe it became a spring food and a household remedy — the young leaves eaten as a salad green, the bitter root and leaf used to wake up digestion.
  • The roasted root gave a drink of its own. Dried, roasted and ground, it was brewed as dandelion coffee — a coffee substitute with no caffeine, well known by the 1800s.
  • The flowers gave another: dandelion wine, a country wine made in spring from the yellow heads, is a long tradition in Britain.
  • Settlers carried the plant to North America, where it spread into every lawn — and kept its place in the kitchen and the herb cupboard.

Traditional medicine

  • The root’s bitter compounds are traditionally used to wake up digestion: bitter tastes prompt the flow of saliva, stomach juices and bile, and dandelion root is a recognised traditional remedy in Europe for mild indigestion, fullness and temporary loss of appetite.
  • The leaf is the part traditionally used to increase urine flow and flush the urinary tract in minor urinary complaints.

Craft

  • The flowers dye wool and cloth yellow, and the roots give browner shades — a dye plant hiding in the lawn.
  • The root stores inulin, a gentle fibre that passes undigested to the large intestine, where it feeds beneficial gut bacteria — the kind of fibre called a prebiotic. Roots dug in autumn can be up to 40% inulin.
  • The leaves are a nutritious green, rich in beta-carotene (which the body converts to vitamin A), vitamin K and potassium, and the whole plant contains antioxidant polyphenols — protective plant compounds.
  • In laboratory and animal studies, dandelion extracts show anti-inflammatory activity; this has not been established in people.
  • In laboratory studies, dandelion extracts have prompted cell death in certain cancer cell lines — this has not been shown in people, and dandelion is not a cancer treatment.
  • When to plant: Usually gathered wild. To grow it, sow seed in spring wherever you want it — it asks for nothing
  • Sun: Full sun or some shade
  • Soil: Any soil, even heavy and compacted — the long taproot opens channels through it
  • Environment: A hardy perennial, and a pioneer of bare, poor ground; its flowers open early in spring, when bees need them most
  • Planter or ground: Ground; a deep pot keeps it contained
  • Spacing and depth: Sow on the surface and press it in
  • Water: Average — drought-tolerant once the taproot is down
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Leaves are best young, in spring, before flowering; roots are dug in autumn, when they hold the most inulin

Garden notes

  • The long taproot grows down through heavy, compacted soil, opening channels that improve aeration and drainage, and draws minerals up from deep layers; when leaves and roots die back, those minerals are returned to the surface soil for other plants.
  • The flowers open early in spring, when little else is blooming, and give bees, butterflies and other insects a crucial early source of nectar and pollen.
  • It is a pioneer plant — one of the first to colonise bare, poor ground — holding the soil and beginning the process that lets other plants follow.

Sources

  • European Medicines Agency, dandelion root, leaf and root-with-herb monographs and assessment reports — traditional use for mild indigestion, appetite and urinary flushing; root inulin content
  • NCCIH — dandelion evidence summary: no compelling scientific evidence for any condition
  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for dandelion greens, raw

Echinacea

Echinacea purpurea

MedicinalFreshRoot

One of North America's classic medicinal plants — better known in the garden as purple coneflower. It is a native of the rocky prairies and open woodlands of central and eastern North America.

Traditional medicine

  • One of North America's classic medicinal plants — better known in the garden as purple coneflower. Native American peoples used it for coughs, colds, sore throats and toothache, and it is still the herb most people reach for at the first sign of a cold.
  • The common cold is its most studied use. Reviews of the trials find that taking echinacea may slightly reduce the chance of catching a cold — but the results are mixed, and whether it shortens a cold once you have one is still unclear.
  • European regulators accept the pressed juice of the fresh flowering herb for the short-term prevention and treatment of colds, taken for no more than ten days. Traditional uses they recognise include the root for cold symptoms and for spots and pimples from mild acne, and the juice in ointments for small superficial wounds.
  • It is sold as an immune-support herb, on the idea that it stimulates the immune system. Laboratory studies support immune activity, but the effect seen in people so far is modest.
  • Caution: echinacea is in the daisy family and closely related to ragweed. Allergic reactions are possible and can occasionally be severe, so do not use it if you are allergic to daisy-family plants such as ragweed, chamomile or chrysanthemums.
  • Caution: because it may stimulate the immune system, it can work against medicines that suppress immunity, such as those taken after an organ transplant — talk to a clinician first if you take immunosuppressants or have an autoimmune condition. Its safety in pregnancy and while breastfeeding is not established, so it is best avoided then too.
  • When to plant: Sow seed in autumn, or in spring after a few weeks in the cold of a fridge — the seed needs a spell of cold to sprout well. Young plants can go out in spring
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Lean, dry, well-drained soil; rich, wet ground shortens its life
  • Environment: A hardy perennial of the rocky North American prairies; drought-tolerant once established. Leave the seed heads standing — goldfinches feed on them
  • Planter or ground: Ground best; large pots work
  • Spacing and depth: Sow on the surface or just below; 45 cm apart
  • Water: Dry once settled — water only in long droughts
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Cut the flowers in summer; dig the root in autumn from plants three or four years old

Garden notes

  • A native of the rocky prairies and open woodlands of central and eastern North America, growing wild across the Great Plains and eastward — a natural fit for prairie-style and wildlife plantings.
  • A first-rate pollinator plant: the US conservation service rates its value to pollinators as high, and the flowers feed bees, butterflies and skippers from midsummer into autumn.
  • Leave the seed heads standing when the flowers fade — goldfinches and other songbirds feed on the seed through autumn.
  • Happy in lean, dry, well-drained soil in full sun, and drought tolerant once established. Wild echinacea has thinned with habitat loss and over-harvesting, so garden-grown plants help take the pressure off wild stands.

Sources

  • NCCIH — echinacea evidence summary: may slightly reduce the chance of catching a cold
  • European Medicines Agency, echinacea herb and root monographs — cold prevention and treatment; traditional uses
  • Karsch-Völk et al. (2014) — Cochrane review of echinacea for preventing and treating the common cold
  • David & Cunningham (2019) — systematic review and meta-analysis of echinacea and respiratory infections
  • Memorial Sloan Kettering, About Herbs — immunosuppressant interaction and allergy cautions
  • USDA Natural Resources Conservation Service, Plant Guide (Stevens, 2006) — native range, Plains Indian use and pollinator value

Goldenrod

Solidago spp.

MedicinalCraftDriedFlower

One of the most important late-season flowers in North America. Goldenrod is usually blamed for late-summer hay fever, but its pollen is heavy and sticky and carried by insects — wind-pollinated ragweed, flowering at the same time, is the usual culprit. The flowering tops are the part used.

History

  • The Latin name is a claim: Solidago comes from solidare, to make whole, because goldenrod was used on wounds that would not heal.
  • European goldenrod was a wound herb and a urinary tea in European practice for centuries.
  • Native American peoples used the North American goldenrods in their own medicine — in teas and poultices for wounds, fevers and sore throats.
  • After the Boston Tea Party of 1773, American colonists turned away from taxed British tea and brewed goldenrod instead. The drink was called Liberty Tea, and goldenrod was even shipped abroad as a tea in those years.
  • That is the thread that runs through its whole history — a wound herb first, and a tea herb after.

Traditional medicine

  • European regulators recognise goldenrod herb as a traditional herbal medicine to increase the flow of urine and flush the urinary tract, as an aid in minor urinary complaints — a use that rests on long-standing tradition; the diuretic effect has not been proven in proper trials.
  • The monograph covers European goldenrod (Solidago virgaurea); the North American goldenrods share the same folk tradition as urinary teas.
  • The monograph's traditional course runs two to four weeks, with plenty of fluids drunk alongside.

Craft

  • Goldenrod is one of the leading natural dye plants: the flowering tops give strong, clear yellows on wool — among the brightest yellows a home dyer can get.
  • Goldenrod is usually blamed for late-summer hay fever, but that blame is misplaced: its pollen is heavy and sticky and is carried by insects, not wind. Wind-pollinated ragweed, which flowers at the same time, is the usual culprit.
  • Caution: goldenrod is in the daisy family — do not use it if you are allergic to daisy-family plants. The EMA does not recommend it for children under 12, does not recommend combining it with synthetic water tablets (diuretics), and advises against it where fluid intake must be restricted, as in severe heart or kidney disease. It is not recommended in pregnancy or while breastfeeding, where there is no safety information.
  • When to plant: Plant young plants or divide clumps in spring; seed can be sown in spring too
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Ordinary, well-drained soil, even poor
  • Environment: A hardy perennial that spreads — give it room, or a contained corner. Its autumn flowers feed migrating monarchs and fill the bees' winter stores
  • Planter or ground: Ground; large pots for the smaller kinds
  • Spacing and depth: 45–60 cm apart
  • Water: Dry once settled
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Gather the flowering tops as they open, in late summer and autumn

Garden notes

  • Conservation groups list goldenrods among the top autumn nectar plants, fuelling migrating monarch butterflies and filling honey bees' winter stores.
  • Specialist bees that collect pollen from almost nothing else — several mining bees and their relatives — depend on goldenrod in the autumn.
  • More than a hundred kinds of insects have been recorded visiting the flowers, and the seed feeds goldfinches, sparrows and juncos well into winter.
  • Left standing, the dry stems shelter overwintering insects through the cold months.

Sources

  • European Medicines Agency, European goldenrod monograph — traditional use for urinary tract flushing
  • Xerces Society — goldenrod as a keystone fall nectar plant; insect-pollinated, not a hay-fever cause
  • University of Illinois Extension — ragweed, not goldenrod, causes late-summer hay fever
  • Monarch Joint Venture — goldenrod nectar for migrating monarchs

Ground Ivy

Glechoma hederacea

MedicinalCulinary

An evergreen perennial ground cover for shady places. Before hops took over, ground ivy was used to flavour, clarify and preserve ale — one of the oldest brewing herbs in Britain.

Traditional medicine

  • A traditional herb for coughs and chesty complaints — long used as an expectorant and anticatarrhal, to help loosen phlegm in coughs and bronchial complaints. Health Canada's herbal monograph allows these uses on the basis of tradition; ground ivy taken by mouth has not been tested in clinical trials.
  • Traditionally used as a stomachic — a bitter digestive aid — and as an astringent — a drying, tightening herb — for diarrhoea.
  • Traditionally used as a diuretic, and for haemorrhoids.
  • On the skin, it has traditionally been used to help heal minor cuts and wounds and to soothe inflammation — Health Canada has a separate monograph for this use on the skin.
  • Before hops took over, ground ivy was used to flavour, clarify and preserve ale — one of the oldest brewing herbs in Britain.
  • Caution: not for use in pregnancy or while breastfeeding, and avoid it if you have a kidney condition. Ground ivy is poisonous to horses if they eat a lot of it, fresh or in hay — other livestock mostly leave it alone for its bitter taste.
  • When to plant: Plant rooted pieces in spring, in a shady corner where it can spread — it is vigorous and can swamp smaller plants
  • Sun: Shade or some shade — a plant for banks and under trees
  • Soil: Moist soil; it holds ground where grass struggles
  • Environment: An evergreen, hardy perennial ground cover; its violet flowers open early, from March to May
  • Planter or ground: Ground; a pot keeps it contained
  • Spacing and depth: Set pieces 30 cm apart — they soon knit into a mat
  • Water: Likes moisture
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Gather the leaves and flowering tops in spring, as the flowers open

Garden notes

  • An evergreen perennial ground cover for shady places — it mats over banks and under trees, holding the soil where grass struggles.
  • Its violet flowers open early, from March to May, and are worked by bees — a good early bee plant, visited by species such as Anthophora and Osmia.
  • The foliage is rich in iron, and the plant has been recommended as an addition to the compost heap.
  • It spreads vigorously and can swamp smaller plants, so it needs room or watching; it is listed as a noxious weed in Connecticut.

Sources

  • Health Canada, Natural Health Products monographs — traditional uses of ground ivy, oral and topical, with pregnancy and kidney warnings
  • Barnes, Anderson & Phillipson, Herbal Medicines — traditional properties and livestock poisoning record
  • USDA Natural Resources Conservation Service, Plant Guide — ale flavouring before hops; toxic to horses in quantity
  • Bond, Davies & Turner (2007) — biology of ground ivy; iron content and ground-cover habit

Lamb's Quarters

Chenopodium album

CulinaryMedicinalCookedLeafSeed

Valued first as a food: a wild spinach whose leaves are eaten as a green, and whose seeds — from a wild relative of quinoa — were gathered and cooked like a grain for thousands of years.

History

  • People have eaten this plant for thousands of years. Its seeds were gathered and cooked like a grain long before it had a name in any book.
  • In North America it was more than gathered. Thousands of years ago, farmers in the east grew a chenopod of this group as a seed crop — one of the crops of the Eastern Agricultural Complex, the farming built there before maize arrived.
  • In Europe and Asia the leaves were a pot herb and the seeds a famine food, ground into flour in hard years.
  • In Britain it was also called fat hen — a name that says plainly that it fed people well.
  • When maize and wheat farming spread, the farmed chenopod was dropped and the plant went back to being a weed of waste ground — the wild spinach gardeners still pick today.

Traditional medicine

  • Traditionally used as a mild laxative, and as a poultice to soothe minor burns and insect bites.
  • Valued first as a food: a wild spinach whose leaves are rich in vitamins A, C and K, and a good source of calcium, potassium, iron and protein.
  • Caution: the leaves are high in oxalates, natural compounds found in many plants that can add to kidney stones in people prone to them, so eat in moderation — and people prone to kidney stones should limit them. Cooking, especially boiling, lowers the oxalate content.
  • The seeds are edible too — a wild relative of quinoa, gathered and cooked like a grain for thousands of years.
  • Caution: it can build up nitrates from rich or contaminated soil, so gather only from clean ground, away from sprayed or heavily fertilised areas.
  • When to plant: Usually gathered wild. To grow it, scatter seed in spring on rich ground and thin the seedlings into the kitchen
  • Sun: Full sun or some shade
  • Soil: Rich soil — it often appears where nitrogen is plentiful
  • Environment: A hardy annual, and a pioneer of bare, disturbed ground
  • Planter or ground: Ground; a pot works
  • Spacing and depth: Scatter thinly; thin to 25 cm apart — the thinnings are the first harvest
  • Water: Average
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Pick the young leaves and tips through summer, before the plant flowers

Garden notes

  • A pioneer plant — one of the first to arrive on bare, disturbed ground, where its growth helps stabilise the soil.
  • Its many seeds are an important food for birds such as finches, sparrows and doves, and for small mammals.
  • Deep roots draw up minerals from below the surface, which return to the topsoil as the plant breaks down.
  • Its foliage supports insect life, and it often appears in nitrogen-rich soil.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for lambsquarters, raw
  • Cornell University weed identification programme — lamb's quarters as a nitrate-accumulating plant
  • Foster & Duke, Peterson Field Guide to Medicinal Plants and Herbs — traditional poultice use
  • Published compositional reviews of Chenopodium album — high leaf oxalate content; boiling reduces soluble oxalates

Mallow

Malva sylvestris

MedicinalCulinaryCraftFreshCookedRootLeafFlower

A soft-leaved flowering plant in the same family as hibiscus and okra, native to Europe, North Africa and western Asia and now found almost everywhere. Leaves, flowers and roots are all used, and the whole plant is edible; what it gives is mucilage, the slippery gel it makes when soaked, which coats and soothes.

History

  • Greek physicians used mallow early — Dioscorides described mallows among his remedies about 2,000 years ago.
  • The Romans took it up as food and medicine. Pliny, the Roman writer on nature, described its uses, and the poet Horace counted mallows among his own simple country foods.
  • Its name fits it. Malva is linked to the Greek word for soft, and softening and soothing is what the plant has always been used for.
  • Through the Middle Ages it was a standard herb of monastery and cottage gardens, used for coughs, sore throats and irritated skin.
  • It stayed a cottage remedy into modern times — a tea and poultice herb for coughs and sore skin — and it is still gathered wild across Europe.

Traditional medicine

  • The mucilage in the leaves and flowers coats the lining of the mouth and throat, easing irritation and the dry cough that comes with it. European regulators accept this as a traditional use, based on long-standing practice rather than clinical trials.
  • A tea or rinse held in the mouth was used to soothe mouth sores and a sore, irritated throat in the same way, by coating the tissue.
  • The same coating action was traditionally used to calm mild discomfort in the stomach and gut.
  • In traditional herbal practice, mallow was also used as a mild, soothing diuretic — a substance that gently increases urine flow.
  • On the skin, mallow has a long traditional use as a softening poultice or wash for irritated, inflamed skin, minor wounds and insect bites; early laboratory and animal research supports its soothing, wound-supporting character, but it has not been well tested in people.

Craft

  • The flowers give a soft purple-blue dye for cloth. The colour is gentle and fades with time, but it is a true dye from a common plant.
  • The whole plant is edible — young leaves in salads, older leaves and shoots cooked as greens — and the leaves supply vitamin C, carotenoid antioxidants (the plant pigments the body can turn into vitamin A), vitamin E compounds, and minerals such as potassium, calcium and iron. The leaves also contain small amounts of alpha-linolenic acid, a plant omega-3 fat, though not in amounts that make mallow a rich source of it.
  • The root carries the same soothing mucilage as the leaf. Marsh mallow root — from a close kin of this plant — was the original base of the French marshmallow sweet.
  • When to plant: Sow seed outside in spring, where it will grow
  • Sun: Full sun or some shade
  • Soil: Ordinary soil, even poor and disturbed ground
  • Environment: Hardy; its strong roots help hold soil in place on banks, and the flowers draw bees through a long season
  • Planter or ground: Both
  • Spacing and depth: Sow 1 cm deep; 30–45 cm apart
  • Water: Average — drought-tolerant once settled
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Pick the leaves young, through the season, and the flowers as they open in summer

Garden notes

  • Its strong roots help hold soil in place on banks and disturbed ground.
  • The flowers draw bees and other pollinating insects through a long flowering season.
  • Mallow is unusually sensitive to ozone, a form of air pollution: its leaves show visible speckled injury at levels other plants tolerate, so scientists have used it as a living warning sign — a bioindicator — of ozone in the air.

Sources

  • European Medicines Agency, mallow flower and leaf monographs — traditional use for mouth and throat irritation, dry cough and mild gut discomfort
  • Barros et al. (2010) — nutrient composition of mallow leaves, flowers and stems
  • Gasparetto et al. (2012) — review of mallow phytochemistry and pharmacology
  • Bergmann, Bender & Weigel (1995) — mallow as the most ozone-sensitive native species tested; ozone bioindicator

Mullein

Verbascum thapsus

MedicinalCraftDriedLeafFlower

A classic pioneer of open, disturbed ground — dry, sandy or gravelly soils, rail banks and waste places. Its tall yellow flower spikes are visited by bees through the summer.

History

  • Mullein is an old medicine. Dioscorides described it about 2,000 years ago, and it has been used for coughs and chest complaints ever since.
  • Its tall dried stalks had a second use. Dipped in tallow, they burned as torches — a country candle carried at funerals and processions in parts of Europe.
  • The soft leaves were used as insoles in worn shoes, to cushion and warm the feet.
  • In Ireland it became a valued remedy for consumption — tuberculosis — and was grown and sold for that use in the 1800s.
  • European settlers carried mullein to North America, and Native American peoples took it up — some smoked the leaves for coughs. There it escaped to roadsides and dry ground everywhere, the same pioneer it had always been in Europe.

Traditional medicine

  • The flowers are a traditional remedy for the symptoms of sore throat that come with a dry cough and a cold — this is the use in the European Medicines Agency's herbal monograph, and it rests on long-standing use, not clinical trials.
  • Mullein leaf and flower have a long tradition in teas for coughs and chest complaints generally.

Craft

  • The dried stalks were dipped in tallow and burned as torches — the plant's old name, hag taper, comes from the custom.
  • The flowers give a yellow dye for wool and cloth.
  • The soothing effect is the flower's mucilage — a few percent of slippery, coating plant fibres that calm an irritated throat.
  • Strain mullein teas well, through fine cloth: the fine hairs on the leaves can irritate the throat and mouth.
  • Herbal ear drops containing mullein eased children's ear pain as well as anaesthetic drops in two clinical trials — but mullein was one of several herbs in olive oil, so the effect cannot be credited to mullein alone.
  • Caution: the seeds are poisonous and must never be eaten — they were once used to stun fish. The EMA does not recommend mullein flower for children under 12, or in pregnancy and while breastfeeding, where its safety has not been established, and advises using it for no more than a week at a time.
  • When to plant: Usually gathered wild. To grow it, scatter seed in spring on bare, dry ground — it needs light to sprout, and it spreads freely by seed
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Dry, sandy or gravelly soil; rich soil makes it soft
  • Environment: A hardy biennial — a flat rosette the first year, a tall flower spike the second. It is classed as a noxious weed in Colorado and Hawaii, so watch it where it is not wanted
  • Planter or ground: Ground only — too tall and deep-rooted for pots
  • Spacing and depth: Scatter on the surface; 60 cm apart
  • Water: Dry once settled
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Gather the leaves in the first year; pick the flowers as they open in the second summer

Garden notes

  • A classic pioneer of open, disturbed ground — dry, sandy or gravelly soils, rail banks and waste places — covering bare soil where little else will grow.
  • Its seed is very long-lived: in a buried-seed experiment begun in 1879, mullein seeds were still sprouting after more than 140 years, and a single plant can leave a seed bank that outlasts the gardener.
  • The tall yellow flower spikes are visited by bees and other pollinators through the summer, and the seed is eaten by finches and sparrows, including the American goldfinch.
  • Left standing, the dead stems shelter overwintering insects, and the plant hosts the mullein moth. It spreads freely and is classed as a noxious weed in Colorado and Hawaii, so it needs watching where it is not wanted.

Sources

  • European Medicines Agency, mullein flower monograph — traditional use for sore throat with dry cough and cold
  • Sarrell et al. (2001) — combination herbal ear drops and ear pain in children
  • Sarrell et al. (2003) — combination herbal ear drops compared with anaesthetic drops
  • Kew, Grow Wild — seed toxicity, fish-stunning history and the long-running buried-seed experiment

Nettle

Urtica dioica

MedicinalCulinaryCraftFreshDriedCookedRootLeafSeed

The stinging nettle is a tall perennial of rich, damp ground, native to temperate Europe and Asia and now found worldwide; its hollow hairs act like tiny needles, injecting a sting that cooking or drying completely removes. Its leaf, root, stem fibre and seed are all used. Its genus holds roughly 80 species, and the wider nettle family over 2,600.

History

  • Nettle is one of the oldest cloth plants in Europe. A cloth found in a Bronze Age burial in Denmark, about 2,800 years old, was woven from nettle fibre.
  • Nettle cloth went on being woven in northern Europe and Scotland for centuries — a linen-like cloth for sheets, shirts and table linen, made from a weed of the hedgerow.
  • When cotton ran short in the First World War, Germany turned back to nettle: the fibre was gathered on a large scale and woven into cloth.
  • As food, the young spring leaves were a tonic after winter — nettle soup and nettle tea are old spring customs across Europe.
  • As medicine, folk practice used it widely: teas for the joints and the waterworks, and the fresh sting itself rubbed on painful joints — a harsh remedy recorded for centuries.
  • One plant, three trades — cloth, food and medicine — which is why the nettle patch by the cottage was kept, not cleared.

Traditional medicine

  • The leaf is traditionally used as a mild diuretic, increasing urine flow to flush the urinary tract in minor urinary complaints — a use European regulators accept on the basis of long-standing practice.
  • The leaf has a traditional use as a rinse for an oily scalp and hair.
  • In folk medicine, the seed was used as a tonic.

Craft

  • The stems give one of the great plant fibres. Stripped, retted and spun like flax, nettle fibre makes cloth, rope, twine and fishing nets. Nettle cloth is thousands of years old, and armies fell back on it when cotton ran short in the First World War.
  • The root — not the leaf — is the part used for the prostate: nettle root preparations are traditionally used, and recognised by European regulators, for easing the urinary symptoms of a benign enlarged prostate (such as a weak stream and frequent night-time trips), once a doctor has ruled out anything serious. A large six-month trial found root extract improved those symptoms more than a placebo, though the evidence overall is still rated limited.
  • The leaf is a genuinely rich food: high in vitamins A, C and K, calcium, iron and protein for a leafy green, and full of antioxidant plant compounds.
  • The leaf is also a traditional remedy for minor joint pain, and rubbing the fresh plant on painful joints is an old folk practice that one small trial found helpful for thumb-joint arthritis pain.
  • For hay fever, nettle leaf is often called a natural antihistamine: in laboratory tests its extract blocks histamine receptors, and one small early trial of freeze-dried leaf suggested it may ease symptoms — promising, but not yet well proven.
  • The seed is eaten too, sprinkled on food.
  • When to plant: Plant root pieces or young plants in spring, in a contained corner — a patch spreads and stays for years
  • Sun: Full sun or some shade
  • Soil: Rich, moist soil — nettles follow nitrogen
  • Environment: A hardy perennial; a patch is a nursery for butterflies, whose caterpillars feed on the leaves, so leave some standing
  • Planter or ground: Ground; a large sunken pot contains the roots
  • Spacing and depth: 30–45 cm apart
  • Water: Likes moisture
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Pick the young tops in spring, with gloves, before flowering; dig the roots in autumn

Garden notes

  • Nettle patches are nurseries for butterflies: the caterpillars of the small tortoiseshell, peacock, red admiral and comma butterflies feed on nettle leaves, and few other plants can replace it for them.
  • A patch also shelters aphids early in spring, which gives ladybirds and other aphid-eating insects an early meal and gets them established before garden pests build up.
  • The stem fibres are strong enough to spin and weave — nettle cloth is thousands of years old, and the fibre has been used for textiles, rope and fishing nets, and as animal bedding and feed.
  • Chopped nettles are rich in nitrogen and minerals, which makes them a fine compost activator and the base of the traditional nettle liquid feed for the garden.
  • Dried nettle is a nutritious animal feed, fed to poultry and livestock.

Sources

  • European Medicines Agency, nettle leaf and nettle root monographs — traditional uses for urinary flushing, minor joint pain and prostate urinary symptoms
  • NCCIH — nettle root evidence for prostate symptoms rated limited
  • Safarinejad (2005) — six-month trial of nettle root extract in 620 men
  • Roschek et al. (2009) — nettle extract and histamine receptors (laboratory)
  • Mittman (1990) — small trial of freeze-dried nettle leaf for hay fever
  • Randall et al. (2000) — fresh nettle rub for thumb-joint arthritis pain

Plantain

Plantago major

MedicinalCraftFreshLeafSeed

Broadleaf plantain — no relation to the banana-like fruit — is a low rosette of wide, ribbed leaves native to Europe and Asia that has followed people around the world, springing up in lawns, paths and compacted ground everywhere. The leaf is the main medicinal part; the seeds are used too.

History

  • Plantain is one of the nine sacred herbs of Anglo-Saxon England. The Nine Herbs Charm, a healing poem about 1,000 years old, names it as waybread and calls on it against poison and infection.
  • Greek and Roman physicians used it before that — for wounds, bites and sores, the same skin uses it keeps today.
  • It travelled with Europeans wherever they settled, springing up along their paths and wagon tracks, its seed carried in mud and on boots.
  • Native Americans saw it arrive with the settlers and gave it a name that records the fact: white man's footprint.
  • The name stuck because it was true: to this day plantain grows most thickly where feet and wheels have packed the ground.

Traditional medicine

  • The leaf is traditionally used in skin washes and toners, where its astringent tannins gently tighten and cleanse the skin’s surface.
  • The seeds are rich in mucilage, a swelling fibre similar to psyllium (which comes from a close plantain relative), traditionally used to keep the bowels moving gently.

Craft

  • The leaves are the classic salve herb: plantain leaves infused in oil and set with wax make the homemade plantain salve, the best-known balm in folk craft.
  • The leaf is the classic wayside first-aid plant: bruised fresh and laid on the skin, or made into a salve, it has been used for centuries on minor wounds, scrapes and insect bites. It contains allantoin, a compound that supports skin repair, and aucubin, which acts against microbes; a small clinical study of a plantain ointment on burns found it performed comparably to a standard treatment cream.
  • The leaf’s tannins — the astringent compounds that make strong tea taste dry — and its flavonoid antioxidants are behind its traditional use for digestive upsets. In animal studies, plantain extracts have protected the stomach lining, but plantain has not been shown to prevent stomach ulcers in people.
  • In laboratory tests, plantain extracts act against a range of bacteria and some viruses. That is a laboratory finding, not proof that the plant fights infections in the body.
  • When to plant: Usually gathered wild. To grow it, sow seed in spring in a patch of poor, trampled ground where little else thrives
  • Sun: Full sun or some shade
  • Soil: Almost any soil, including compacted ground — its fibrous roots help bind it
  • Environment: A hardy perennial; the ground-hugging rosette tolerates mowing and foot traffic
  • Planter or ground: Ground; a pot works
  • Spacing and depth: Sow on the surface; 25 cm apart
  • Water: Average
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Pick leaves through the season — the youngest for eating, any size for a poultice; the seed heads ripen in late summer

Garden notes

  • Its strong fibrous roots and ground-hugging rosette help bind soil and slow erosion, and the plant tolerates the compacted, trampled ground where little else will grow.
  • The dense mat of leaves shades the soil surface, helping it hold moisture.
  • In laboratory studies, an extract of plantain seed worked as a natural coagulant — a substance that makes fine particles in dirty water clump together so they can be removed — clearing industrial wastewater about as well as some conventional treatments.

Sources

  • Samuelsen (2000) — review of Plantago major traditional use and pharmacology (allantoin, aucubin)
  • Pharmaceuticals review of Plantago major (2023) — antimicrobial and antiviral activity in laboratory tests
  • Clinical study of plantain ointment on burns (2022) — small trial, performance comparable to a standard cream
  • Zarei Mahmoudabadi et al. (2018) — plantain seed extract as a water coagulant (laboratory)

Purple Dead-Nettle

Lamium purpureum

MedicinalCulinaryFreshCookedLeafFlower

A member of the mint family. The “dead” in its name means stingless — unlike its cousin the stinging nettle, it has no stinging hairs and is safe to handle. It is one of the earliest flowers of the year.

History

  • Purple dead-nettle is a European folk herb with a quiet record. It was never a famous medicine — country practice used it, and the great herbals mostly passed it by.
  • Its folk uses were simple: a spring tonic, eaten as one of the first greens of the year, and the fresh leaves laid on wounds and cuts.
  • That is most of its history — a spring food, a minor remedy, and one of the first flowers of the year for bees.

Traditional medicine

  • In European folk tradition it was used mostly on the skin, as a poultice or wash for cuts, scrapes and minor irritation.
  • Folk practice also describes it as astringent and as a gentle diuretic, though these are traditional descriptions, not tested ones.
  • The whole plant above ground is edible — leaves, stems and flowers — with a mild, earthy taste. Foraging guides describe the leaves as a source of vitamins A, C and K, iron and fibre.
  • It is a gentle folk herb rather than a tested medicine — research on this species itself is still very limited, and most laboratory work has been done on its relatives.
  • Caution: large amounts — a strong tea, for example — can have a laxative effect. Take care with identification: its closest lookalikes are henbit, which is also edible, and ground ivy, and while none of the usual lookalikes is toxic, it is still worth waiting for the square stem, the purple-tinged top leaves and the pink-purple flowers to be sure you have the right plant.
  • When to plant: Usually gathered wild. It is a winter annual: seed scattered in autumn gives plants that flower from late winter
  • Sun: Full sun or some shade
  • Soil: Ordinary, moist soil
  • Environment: Hardy; it covers bare soil through the cold months, then recedes by summer. Its early flowers are a critical nectar source for queen bumblebees just out of hibernation
  • Planter or ground: Ground; a pot works
  • Spacing and depth: Scatter seed on the surface in autumn
  • Water: Average
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Gather the leaves and flowers in late winter and spring, while it blooms

Garden notes

  • One of the earliest flowers of the year, blooming from late winter — a critical nectar source for queen bumblebees emerging from hibernation, and for honeybees and digger bees, when almost nothing else is in flower.
  • Its crimson-red pollen is an early source of protein for bees raising their first brood.
  • A winter annual, it covers bare soil through the cold months and then recedes by summer, making room for later plants.
  • Its seeds carry a small oily parcel (an elaiosome, a fatty attachment that ants eat) that ants carry off, planting the seed as they go.

Sources

  • Kew, Plants of the World Online — accepted name and native range (Europe, Asia and North Africa; naturalised in North America)
  • Plants For A Future — purple dead-nettle entry: young leaves eaten raw or cooked as a potherb; the whole plant traditionally astringent, diuretic and purgative; fresh bruised leaves applied to cuts and wounds
  • Cornell University, College of Agriculture and Life Sciences — deadnettle weed profile: identification (square stems, purple-tinged upper leaves), separation from henbit, and pollination by bumblebees, solitary bees and honeybees
  • Penn State Extension — purple dead nettle, henbit and ground ivy as look-alike winter annuals; spring flowers visited by honeybees and bumblebees
  • Hodges (1952) — The Pollen Loads of the Honeybee: a prominent March–April pollen source, when bees need protein to build up their nests; the pollen crimson red
  • Pemberton & Irving (1990) — elaiosomes on the seeds of weeds including red dead-nettle, and the potential for their dispersal by ants

Purslane

Portulaca oleracea

CulinaryFreshCookedLeafSeed

Purslane is a fleshy, low-spreading succulent with spoon-shaped leaves and small yellow flowers, eaten as a vegetable since ancient times and now found in gardens and warm ground all over the world; its exact wild origin is debated, somewhere in the Old World. Leaves, stems and seeds are all edible — and it is a nutrient-rich garden plant.

History

  • Purslane has been eaten since ancient times around the Mediterranean and across Asia — one of the oldest greens in the kitchen.
  • Greek medicine described it early: Dioscorides wrote of it in his herbal about 2,000 years ago, as food and medicine.
  • In China it has its own long record as ma chi xian, a herb used for cooling and for the gut.
  • It travelled with trade and settlement to every warm country, and in most of them it was treated the same way — gathered as a free green, and just as often weeded out as a nuisance.
  • That double standing is its whole history: prized in some kitchens, pulled as a weed in others, and eaten somewhere in the world in every age.

The leaves

  • Purslane leaves carry more alpha-linolenic acid — the plant form of omega-3 fat — than any other leafy green vegetable measured, about 300–400 mg in a 100 g serving. (The body converts only a little of this plant omega-3 into the fish-oil forms, EPA and DHA.)
  • It is mineral-rich for a leafy green: a good source of potassium and magnesium, with useful amounts of calcium, plus vitamins A, C and E.

The stems and seeds

  • The stems are eaten raw or cooked like the leaves. The tiny black seeds are edible, and were ground into seed cakes by Aboriginal Australians.
  • In animal studies, purslane extracts have protected the liver against damage. This effect has not been shown in people.
  • In animal studies, purslane extracts have also protected brain and nerve cells — the kind of early research sometimes described as neuroprotective. This, too, has not been shown in people.
  • Caution: purslane is high in oxalates — natural compounds found in many plants that can add to kidney stones in people prone to them — so if that is a concern, keep portions moderate.
  • When to plant: Sow outside in late spring, once the soil is warm — it hates cold ground
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Poor, dry, sandy soil suits it; it is a pioneer of bare ground
  • Environment: A heat-loving annual; its dense, fleshy mat shades the soil and slows evaporation, helping the ground beneath hold water
  • Planter or ground: Both — a wide, shallow pot suits it
  • Spacing and depth: Sow on the surface or just below; 25 cm apart
  • Water: Dry once settled — the fleshy leaves store water
  • Companion plants: A recognised living ground cover beneath corn, tomatoes, peppers and potatoes, keeping the soil cool and moist around them
  • When to pick: Pick the stems and leaves through summer

Garden notes

  • It is a pioneer plant, among the first to take hold on bare, poor or dry ground, covering and stabilising soil where little else will grow.
  • Its dense, fleshy mat shades the soil and slows evaporation, helping the ground beneath it hold water.
  • Gardeners often let it grow as a living ground cover among taller crops — it is a recognised companion beneath corn, tomatoes, peppers and potatoes, keeping the soil cool and moist around them while its deeper roots draw up minerals from below.
  • Its small flowers are visited by bees and other small pollinating insects.

Sources

  • Uddin et al. (2014) — purslane omega-3 (alpha-linolenic acid) content, highest of leafy greens measured; oxalate content
  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for purslane, raw
  • Iranshahy et al. (2017) — review of purslane pharmacology; liver and nerve effects in animal studies
  • NIH Office of Dietary Supplements — omega-3 fact sheet: limited conversion of plant omega-3 to EPA and DHA
  • University of Nevada, Reno Extension — purslane as a companion plant and living ground cover

Red Clover

Trifolium pratense

MedicinalFlower

A nitrogen-fixing legume: bacteria in its root nodules take nitrogen from the air and add it to the soil, which is why farmers grow it as a cover crop and green manure. The flower heads are the part used.

History

  • Red clover's big history is on the farm. From the 1600s and 1700s it was grown across Europe as a fodder crop, cut for hay and grazed by stock.
  • It earned its place in the rotation: grown in turn with grain crops, it rested and enriched the soil — its roots add nitrogen — and harvests after clover were better for it.
  • Farmers carried it around the world, and it became one of the standard pasture plants of North America, Australia and New Zealand.
  • In folk medicine the flower heads were a remedy for skin troubles such as eczema, and for coughs, including whooping cough.
  • It is the state flower of Vermont — a small honour that records how much the plant meant to farming country.

Traditional medicine

  • Traditionally used for skin conditions such as eczema and psoriasis, and for coughs, including whooping cough.
  • Contains isoflavones — plant compounds, including biochanin A and formononetin, that are structurally similar to the hormone estrogen.
  • Supplements are promoted for menopausal hot flushes, but studies have had inconsistent results, and the US National Center for Complementary and Integrative Health finds no conclusive evidence of benefit for any condition.
  • Caution: because of its estrogen-like activity, avoid red clover supplements in pregnancy and while breastfeeding, and talk to a healthcare provider first if you have a hormone-sensitive condition or take hormone-related medication.
  • Caution: red clover may increase the risk of bleeding alongside blood-thinning medicines such as warfarin. The safety of long-term use is not known.
  • When to plant: Sow seed outside in spring, or late summer, into pasture, a patch, or a bed you want fed
  • Sun: Full sun or some shade
  • Soil: Ordinary soil; it fixes nitrogen, and a deep taproot helps break up compacted layers
  • Environment: A hardy perennial, often short-lived; its flowers are an important nectar source for bumblebees and honeybees
  • Planter or ground: Ground
  • Spacing and depth: Scatter thinly and rake in lightly
  • Water: Average
  • Companion plants: Grass is its classic pasture partner; it feeds the soil for whatever follows it
  • When to pick: Pick the flowers as they open through summer; the leaves are gathered young

Garden notes

  • Its flowers are pollinated by bumblebees and honeybees, and it is an important nectar plant and source of honey.
  • A deep taproot helps break up compacted layers of soil.
  • Grown as a living cover, it protects the soil surface from erosion.

Sources

  • NCCIH — red clover evidence summary: no conclusive benefit for any condition; hot-flush studies inconsistent
