  • Memorial Sloan Kettering, About Herbs — traditional uses, hormone-sensitive cancer and anticoagulant cautions
  • USDA Agricultural Research Service — red clover as a cover crop: nitrogen contribution and pollinator value

Roselle

Hibiscus sabdariffa

MedicinalCulinaryCraftDriedFlowerLeaf

This is Jamaican sorrel — the plant behind the ruby-red Christmas drink. It is a hibiscus, and no relation at all to wood sorrel or to garden sorrel: three different plants that share a name because they share a tart taste. The part used is the fleshy red calyx — the cup that swells around the seed pod after the flower falls.

History

  • Roselle comes from West Africa, where the sour red calyces and the leaves have long been food and drink.
  • Trade carried it across the Atlantic a few centuries ago, and in the Caribbean it found its famous role: sorrel, the spiced red Christmas drink, brewed with ginger and cloves.
  • In Mexico the same drink is agua de jamaica — Jamaica water — named for the island it came by.
  • In Sudan and Egypt it is karkade, served hot or cold, at home and at celebrations — Sudan is still one of its biggest growers.
  • Wherever it landed, it kept the same use — a sour, cooling drink for hot climates — and picked up a local name each time.

Craft

  • The stems yield a jute-like fibre, spun and woven for rope, twine and coarse cloth in the tropics — a second crop from a plant grown for its calyces.
  • Dried and brewed, it makes the cooling drinks known across the tropics as sorrel in the Caribbean, bissap in Senegal, karkadé in Egypt and Sudan, zobo in Nigeria and agua de Jamaica in Mexico. The calyces are rich in vitamin C and in the red anthocyanin pigments that give the drink its colour.
  • Blood pressure is its most studied use. Pooled analyses of the trials find that hibiscus tea and extracts lower blood pressure modestly — on average around seven points off the top reading — with the clearest effects in people whose pressure was raised to begin with. The US National Center for Complementary and Integrative Health stresses that the evidence is limited and the effects small beside prescribed medicines. Roselle is not a treatment for high blood pressure, and it must never be used in place of prescribed medication.
  • It has also been studied for cholesterol and blood sugar, with weaker and mixed results: a pooled review of the cholesterol trials found no significant effect, and the blood-sugar findings are inconsistent. Neither effect is established.
  • The leaves are eaten as a sour green, including in Burmese cooking (chin baung).
  • Caution: avoid roselle in pregnancy. It has never been properly tested in pregnant women, and animal studies raise concerns that strong extracts can affect the womb and the development of offspring. Its safety while breastfeeding is likewise unknown.
  • Caution: roselle can add to the effect of blood-pressure medicines, so pressure may fall too far — talk to a clinician first if you take them. It can sharply reduce how much of the antimalarial medicine chloroquine the body absorbs, and it can change how the body handles some other medicines, including the cholesterol medicine simvastatin. Take care alongside diabetes medicines, as it may lower blood sugar a little, and note that very large doses of strong extracts have raised liver enzymes in animal studies.
  • When to plant: Sow after all frost has passed — or start indoors 6–8 weeks earlier where summers are short
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Good, well-drained soil
  • Environment: A true tropical annual: it needs warmth and a long season, grows fast to two metres or more, and is killed by the first frost
  • Planter or ground: Ground best; a very large pot where summers are hot
  • Spacing and depth: 60–90 cm apart
  • Water: Likes water while it is growing fast
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Pick the fleshy red calyces by hand as they swell around the seed pods, in late summer and autumn

Garden notes

  • A plant of West Africa originally, carried across the tropics centuries ago and now grown wherever summers are long and hot — Sudan, Nigeria, Mexico, Egypt, Thailand and China are among the biggest producers.
  • A true tropical annual: sown after all frost has passed, it needs full sun, warmth and a long season, grows fast to two metres or more in good, well-drained soil, and is killed by the first frost.
  • The harvest is gathered by hand — the fleshy red calyces are picked as they swell around the seed pods. The stems have a second use, yielding a jute-like fibre.
  • The large flowers each open for a single day and are visited by bees and other pollinating insects through the flowering season.

Sources

  • NCCIH — roselle (hibiscus) and blood pressure: evidence limited, effects small, not a substitute for prescribed treatment
  • Ellis et al. (2022) — meta-analysis of hibiscus and blood pressure: systolic about 7 mmHg lower
  • Serban et al. (2015) — pooled blood-pressure reductions with hibiscus
  • McKay et al. (2010) — hibiscus tea trial in adults with pre- or mild hypertension
  • Cochrane review of roselle for hypertension (2021) — evidence judged insufficient under strict criteria
  • Aziz et al. (2013) — meta-analysis: no significant effect of hibiscus on blood lipids
  • Memorial Sloan Kettering, About Herbs — hibiscus interactions with blood-pressure medicines, simvastatin and chloroquine
  • Kew, Plants of the World Online — name and native range (West Africa)

Sage

Salvia officinalis

CulinaryMedicinalCraftFreshDriedLeaf

A low, evergreen subshrub — woody at the base, soft and leafy above — in the mint family, Lamiaceae, alongside lavender, peppermint, lemon balm and thyme. Sage is native to the dry, rocky ground around the northern Mediterranean, and it has been carried into gardens everywhere since. It is a kitchen herb first: the grey-green, wrinkled leaves, strongly scented and slightly bitter, flavour stuffings, sausages, beans and brown butter across Europe, and the leaf is the part everything in this entry is about.

The other parts play small roles. The violet-blue flower spikes that rise in early summer are edible and go into salads as a garnish, but no medicine is made from them. The seed is occasionally eaten in a few regional dishes and is not a remedy. The root is not used at all. What the leaf carries is a strong essential oil, and one compound in that oil matters for safety: thujone, which in quantity is toxic to the nervous system. The amounts in a seasoning of leaves are small; the amounts in the concentrated oil are not, and the two should never be confused.

History

  • The name is the reputation. Salvia comes from the Latin salvere — to be well, to be saved — and officinalis marks a plant kept in the officina, the apothecary's storeroom: this was an official medicine, not a wayside herb.
  • Greek and Roman physicians used it widely. Theophrastus described sages in the fourth century BCE, Dioscorides included sage in his herbal, and the Romans are said to have gathered it with ceremony and called it herba sacra, the sacred herb.
  • Around the year 800, Charlemagne's Capitulare de villis — the ordinance listing what every imperial estate was to grow — named sage, and monastery physic gardens then carried it across Europe.
  • The medieval medical school at Salerno gave it the most quoted line in herbal history, in its Regimen of Health: "Cur moriatur homo cui salvia crescit in horto?" — "Why should a man die who has sage growing in his garden?" An old English saw runs the same way: "He who would live for aye must eat sage in May." Both record a reputation for preserving life, not a finding.
  • Sage was one of the herbs steeped in the Four Thieves vinegar, the blend that legend says protected a band of thieves robbing plague houses. The story is legend; the herb's place in it shows how deep the trust in sage ran.
  • In the kitchen it settled into the cooking of several countries: with veal in Italy's saltimbocca, crisped in brown butter over pasta and pumpkin, and in Britain minced with onion into the stuffing for roast pork and poultry. The grey-green of its leaves also gave the English language a colour — sage.

Traditional medicine

  • For digestion and the mouth and throat, European regulators accept sage leaf at traditional-use strength: a tea for mild indigestion, heartburn and bloating, and a gargle for inflammation of the mouth and throat. Traditional use means the standing rests on long use rather than on trials.

Craft

  • The leaves give a soft grey-green dye for wool and cloth — quiet shades, true to the leaf's own colour.
  • For memory and thinking, the human evidence is real but small, and it belongs to extracts, not to the kitchen herb. In one trial, single doses of a sage extract improved memory and attention scores in 20 healthy volunteers over 65, with a middle dose (333 mg) working best. In another, 42 people with mild to moderate Alzheimer's disease took a sage tincture for 16 weeks and scored better on cognitive tests than those on placebo. Each is a single small trial, none has been repeated at scale, and nothing here shows that sage prevents or treats dementia.
  • For menopausal hot flushes, sage stands on a long tradition of use against sweating, which European regulators recognise at traditional-use strength. The one dedicated trial gave a fresh sage preparation to 71 menopausal women for eight weeks and watched the combined score for the number and intensity of flushes fall by half at four weeks and by about two-thirds at eight. There was no placebo group, so how much of that fall was the sage cannot be separated from the large improvement hot-flush trials routinely show on dummy treatment. A later, smaller placebo-controlled trial also favoured sage, and a pooled analysis of the few trials found flushes came less often on sage without a clear change in their severity. Promising and preliminary — not proven.
  • For blood sugar, one research group in Iran has run small trials of sage leaf extract in people with type 2 diabetes: in the largest, 80 people took 500 mg of extract three times a day for three months and finished with lower fasting glucose and lower HbA1c — a measure of average blood sugar — than the placebo group. The reported effects were unusually large for a herb, the trials all come from that one group, and no independent team has repeated them. Sage is not a diabetes treatment, and the finding is recorded here at exactly that strength.
  • Caution — the oil and thujone: sage essential oil is a concentrate rich in thujone, and thujone in quantity attacks the nervous system. Sage oil swallowed in amounts corresponding to more than about 15 g of leaf has caused a racing heart, vertigo and convulsions. European regulators cap the thujone in sage medicines at under 6 mg a day. The oil is not a food and is never a substitute for the leaf.
  • Caution — pregnancy and breastfeeding: medicinal amounts of sage are not recommended in pregnancy or while breastfeeding, because safety has not been established and thujone is the standing concern. The amounts used in cooking are not the issue; concentrated teas, extracts and the oil are.
  • Caution — medicines: because sage extract can lower blood sugar, it can add to the effect of diabetes medicines and push blood sugar too low. And because thujone can provoke seizures, anyone with epilepsy, or taking medicines for seizures, should keep away from medicinal sage preparations.
  • When to plant: Sow seed in spring — it is slow to sprout — or take cuttings in summer, which root readily; plant out once growing well
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Poor to middling soil with sharp drainage — rich, wet ground makes it soft and short-lived
  • Environment: A plant of dry, stony Mediterranean hillsides; drought does it no harm once the roots are down, but wet winter soil rots the roots — a raised bed or a gravelly corner suits it best
  • Planter or ground: Both — a pot of gritty mix suits it very well
  • Spacing and depth: 45–60 cm apart
  • Water: Dry once settled
  • Companion plants: Cabbage-family plants, carrots and rosemary are its classic neighbours
  • When to pick: Pick the leaves from spring into autumn, at their strongest just before the flowers open; renew plants every four or five years, as they grow woody

Garden notes

  • A plant of dry, stony Mediterranean hillsides: it wants full sun, sharp drainage, and poor to middling soil. Rich, wet ground makes it soft and short-lived.
  • Evergreen and woody-based, it holds its grey leaves through winter in mild districts and sends up violet-blue flower spikes in early summer that bees work heavily.
  • Drought, once the roots are down, does it no harm. The usual way to kill sage is wet winter soil, which rots the roots — a raised bed or a gravelly corner suits it best.
  • Plants tire after four or five years, growing woody and sparse; cuttings root readily in summer and renew the plant exactly.
  • Leaves are picked from spring into autumn and are at their strongest just before the flowers open.

Sources

  • Kew, Plants of the World Online — Salvia officinalis, family placement and native range
  • European Medicines Agency (2016) — European Union herbal monograph on Salvia officinalis L., folium: traditional uses, thujone limits and pregnancy warning
  • Akhondzadeh et al. (2003) — sage extract against placebo in mild to moderate Alzheimer's disease, Journal of Clinical Pharmacy and Therapeutics
  • Scholey et al. (2008) — sage extract and memory and attention in healthy older volunteers, Psychopharmacology
  • Bommer et al. (2011) — fresh sage preparation for menopausal hot flushes, open multicentre trial, Advances in Therapy
  • Kianbakht & Dabaghian (2013) — sage leaf extract and blood sugar in type 2 diabetes, Complementary Therapies in Medicine
  • Lopresti (2017) — review of the human trials of sage for cognition, Drugs in R&D

Self-Heal

Prunella vulgaris

MedicinalCraftDriedFlower

One of the great traditional wound herbs — its old name is “heal-all” — used for centuries across Europe, Asia and North America for wounds, sores and sore throats. In Chinese medicine the dried flower spike, called xiakucao, is a long-standing traditional remedy in its own right.

Traditional medicine

  • There are no solid human trials behind self-heal, so all of its uses remain traditional ones.

Craft

  • The flowers and stems give an olive-green dye for wool and cloth.
  • The plant contains rosmarinic acid, ursolic acid and flavonoids, and in laboratory studies these show anti-inflammatory, antioxidant and antimicrobial activity — interesting chemistry, but laboratory results are not the same as benefit in people.
  • It has a gentle safety record in traditional use; a mild stomach upset is possible.
  • Caution: its safety in pregnancy and while breastfeeding has not been studied, so medicinal use is best avoided then.
  • When to plant: Sow seed in spring, or plant rooted pieces — it creeps and roots as it goes
  • Sun: Full sun or some shade
  • Soil: Ordinary, moist soil; its creeping roots hold banks, stream edges and pond sides
  • Environment: A hardy perennial; low and lawn-friendly, it stays green and flowers even when mown short — though it can turn weedy if left entirely unmanaged
  • Planter or ground: Ground; a wide pot works
  • Spacing and depth: Sow on the surface or just below; set pieces 25 cm apart
  • Water: Likes moisture
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Gather the flowering spikes in summer, as they open

Garden notes

  • Its fibrous, creeping roots and spreading habit hold soil together — the US Department of Agriculture recommends it for erosion control along roadsides, stream banks and pond edges.
  • The purple flower spikes give nectar and pollen to butterflies — whites, sulphurs and skippers — and to native bees, including bumblebees, digger bees, small carpenter bees and sweat bees.
  • Ground-foraging birds eat the small nut-like seeds, and the caterpillars of the gray marvel moth feed on the leaves.
  • A low, lawn-friendly plant that stays green, flowers even when mown short, and spreads into a living ground cover — though it can become weedy if left entirely unmanaged.

Sources

  • USDA Natural Resources Conservation Service, Plant Fact Sheet (2012) — erosion control, pollinator value and ground-cover use
  • Frontiers in Pharmacology review (2022) — Prunella vulgaris traditional uses, chemistry (rosmarinic and ursolic acids, flavonoids) and laboratory pharmacology
  • Chinese Pharmacopoeia (2020 edition) — the dried flower spike, xiakucao, listed as an official traditional medicine

Shepherd's Purse

Capsella bursa-pastoris

MedicinalLeafFlower

One of the commonest weeds in the world — a pioneer of disturbed ground, gardens, paths and field edges, and one of the first plants to colonise bare soil. It can flower in almost any month of the year. The whole above-ground plant is the part used, gathered while in flower.

Traditional medicine

  • European regulators recognise shepherd's purse as a traditional herbal medicine for reducing heavy menstrual bleeding in women whose cycles are regular — and only after a doctor has ruled out serious causes. Heavy bleeding always needs checking first.
  • Following the monograph, the traditional course begins three to five days before the period is due.
  • Its fame as a herb that stops bleeding is centuries old — it was used on wounds in the First World War — but European assessors found no studies showing an actual clotting effect, so that reputation is history, not established fact.
  • In two small clinical trials, an extract added to a standard painkiller (mefenamic acid) reduced menstrual bleeding more than the painkiller alone, with the clearest difference by the third month — promising, but early results from small studies.
  • Caution: the EMA does not recommend shepherd's purse in pregnancy or while breastfeeding — extracts made womb tissue contract in laboratory studies, and its safety in pregnancy has never been tested. It is also not recommended for anyone under 18.
  • Caution: because this is traditionally a bleeding herb, take care alongside blood-thinning medicines and check with a clinician first — though it is fair to say the EMA itself reports no documented interactions and no known side effects.
  • When to plant: Usually gathered wild. To grow it, scatter seed in spring on any spare patch — it asks for nothing
  • Sun: Full sun or some shade
  • Soil: Almost any soil, even poor and disturbed ground
  • Environment: A hardy annual; its early spring flowers feed small bees and flies when little else is out
  • Planter or ground: Ground; a pot works
  • Spacing and depth: Scatter on the surface
  • Water: Average
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Gather the whole plant in spring, while it flowers; the leaves are best young

Garden notes

  • Its early spring flowers feed small bees and flies when little else is out.
  • Birds are fond of the seeds — chaffinches and other small birds eat them — and the seed can survive in the soil for decades, waiting for its chance.
  • It is being studied for cleaning contaminated soils, as part of phytoremediation research — using plants to draw contaminants out of soil.

Sources

  • European Medicines Agency, shepherd's purse monograph — traditional use for heavy menstrual bleeding, after serious conditions are excluded
  • Naafe et al. (2018) — two small trials of shepherd's purse extract with mefenamic acid for menstrual bleeding
  • Botanical Society of Scotland (2022) — distribution and seed persistence
  • Royal Horticultural Society — shepherd's purse as a seed food for small birds
  • Nutrients review of Capsella bursa-pastoris (2024) — phytoremediation studies

Sorrel (Wood Sorrel)

Oxalis spp.

CulinaryFreshLeafFlowerSeed

Wood sorrel — the little lawn weed with three heart-shaped leaflets — is not the garden salad herb of the same name. Jamaican sorrel is a different plant again — a hibiscus, sharing nothing with this one but the name and the sharp taste.

History

  • Wood sorrel has been a wayside nibble for centuries — the sour leaves chewed on walks and gathered as a spring green across Europe.
  • After winter, when scurvy threatened, sour spring greens rich in vitamin C were valued, and wood sorrel was one of them.
  • It was a kitchen herb too: in parts of Europe the sour leaves went into spring sauces and salads, doing the work lemons do now.
  • Tradition links it to Ireland. Wood sorrel is one of the plants claimed as the true shamrock, the three-leafed plant of St Patrick — the claim is old, but so are the claims of its rivals, and no one knows for certain.
  • Its Latin name tells its taste: Oxalis comes from the Greek oxys, sharp or sour.

Traditional medicine

  • It is a traditional thirst-quencher and spring green, chewed on walks to freshen the mouth.
  • It contains vitamin C, and greens like it were once valued in spring against scurvy — that is history and tradition, not a modern treatment claim.
  • The leaves, flowers and young seed pods are eaten in small amounts as a wayside nibble; the sharp, lemony taste comes from oxalic acid.
  • Caution: the same oxalic acid that gives the flavour binds calcium and can interfere with absorbing it, so wood sorrel is a small-amounts plant only. People with kidney stones, gout, kidney problems or rheumatoid arthritis should avoid it or be especially careful. Cooking reduces the soluble oxalates.
  • Caution for pets and livestock: all wood sorrels are listed as toxic to dogs, cats and horses. A nibble usually causes no more than drooling and an upset stomach, but grazing animals that eat a lot of it are at real risk.
  • When to plant: Usually gathered wild. Wood sorrel spreads by seed and thin underground stems — to grow it, sow in spring in a corner where its spread is welcome
  • Sun: Anything from full sun to shade
  • Soil: Poor, disturbed, even compacted soils
  • Environment: A hardy perennial that spreads fast — it makes a quick living cover on bare ground, and needs watching in beds where it is not wanted
  • Planter or ground: Both — a pot keeps it in bounds
  • Spacing and depth: Sow on the surface or just below; the plants spread to fill their corner
  • Water: Average
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Gather the leaves and flowers fresh through the season — small amounts only

Garden notes

  • The ripe seed capsules are spring-loaded — touched, they fling their seeds several feet, which is one reason wood sorrel turns up everywhere.
  • It spreads by seed and by thin underground stems, and will grow in poor, disturbed and compacted soils, in anything from full sun to shade.
  • The small yellow flowers are visited by small bees through a long season.
  • It makes a quick living cover on bare, poor ground where little else volunteers — though that same fast spread means it needs watching in beds where it is not wanted.

Sources

  • NC State University Extension — wood sorrel oxalate caution and seed dispersal
  • ASPCA — Oxalis species toxic to dogs, cats and horses
  • Plants For A Future — oxalic acid binding calcium; small-amounts advice

St. John's Wort

Hypericum perforatum

MedicinalCraftFreshFlower

A sunny meadow and wayside perennial whose flowers offer no nectar but give bees plenty of pollen through the summer. Its medicine interactions are the first thing to know about it. The flowering tops are the part used.

History

  • The plant was used in ancient Greek medicine — for wounds, and for troubled nerves — and Greek and Roman writers kept it in their herbals.
  • Its name comes from St John's Day, June 24, in midsummer, when the plant is in full flower and was gathered for the year.
  • Tradition says bunches of it were hung over doors and windows on St John's Eve to protect the house — against lightning, fire and evil spirits.
  • The flowers steeped in oil turn it red, and that red oil was a household remedy for wounds, burns and nerve pain for centuries.
  • It kept both lives into modern times — the midsummer herb of folklore, and a medicine still studied today for low mood.

Craft

  • The flowers are a dye plant: they give yellow shades, and handled differently, reds — the same red pigment that colours the plant's infused oil.
  • Caution — prescription medicines: St. John's wort speeds up the liver enzymes that clear many medicines from the body, so those medicines can stop working. The list includes the contraceptive pill — breakthrough bleeding and unplanned pregnancies have been reported — transplant anti-rejection medicines, HIV medicines, cancer medicines, the blood thinner warfarin, digoxin, and some antidepressants, whose levels it lowers.
  • Caution — serotonin syndrome: combined with antidepressant medicines (SSRIs, SNRIs and triptans among them), St. John's wort can cause serotonin syndrome — a potentially life-threatening reaction with agitation, fever, fast heartbeat, confusion and muscle stiffness. Anyone taking any prescription medicine must talk to a doctor or pharmacist before using it.
  • St. John's wort has been studied for mild to moderate depression, with mixed results: a large Cochrane review of 29 trials found its extracts worked better than placebo and about as well as standard antidepressants for mild to moderate depression, with fewer side effects — but two large US trials in major depression found no benefit over placebo, and the US National Center for Complementary and Integrative Health calls the evidence not definitive. Depression is a serious illness that needs medical care; this is not a casual tonic, and it is never a substitute for treatment.
  • European regulators accept specified extracts for mild to moderate depressive episodes as a well-established use, and list traditional uses for temporary mental exhaustion, minor skin inflammation such as sunburn and minor wounds (as an infused oil), and mild digestive discomfort.
  • Caution: it increases the skin's sensitivity to sunlight, and that applies to the infused oil on the skin as well as to taking it. It may be unsafe in pregnancy, and European regulators do not recommend it for anyone under 18.
  • When to plant: Sow seed in spring, or plant young plants — and plant it only where its spread can be watched: it is classed as a noxious weed in several western US states and parts of Australia, and it is poisonous to grazing livestock
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Well-drained soil, even poor; its deep roots can hold soil on banks
  • Environment: A hardy perennial that spreads by seed and creeping roots
  • Planter or ground: Ground, in a contained spot
  • Spacing and depth: 45 cm apart
  • Water: Dry once settled
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Gather the flowering tops as they open, around midsummer

Garden notes

  • An honest downside: away from its home ground it can take over — it is classed as a noxious weed in several western US states and parts of Australia, spreads by seed and creeping roots, and is poisonous to grazing livestock, making their skin painfully sensitive to sunlight.
  • In California it was famously brought back under control by two leaf beetles introduced from Europe — one of the classic success stories of biological weed control.
  • Its deep roots can hold soil on banks, but its spreading habit means it needs watching wherever it is planted.

Sources

  • Linde et al. (2008) — Cochrane review: extracts superior to placebo and comparable to standard antidepressants for mild to moderate depression
  • European Medicines Agency, St. John's wort monograph — well-established use for mild to moderate depressive episodes; traditional uses
  • NCCIH — evidence not definitive; two large US trials in major depression found no benefit over placebo
  • Memorial Sloan Kettering, About Herbs — full interaction profile: contraceptive pill, transplant, HIV and cancer medicines, warfarin, digoxin, serotonin syndrome
  • USDA Natural Resources Conservation Service, Plant Guide — noxious-weed status and biological control

Violet

Viola spp.

MedicinalCulinaryCraftDriedLeafFlower

The leaves and flowers are the parts used — in syrups, teas and salads, and in skin preparations. Among the earliest spring flowers, violets feed emerging bees, including a mining bee that specialises in violets.

History

  • The violet was the emblem flower of ancient Athens. Poets called the city violet-crowned Athens, and violets were sold in its markets and worn in garlands.
  • Greek and Roman households used violets in wine and garlands, and the flowers later settled into syrups and sweets.
  • In France, Toulouse became the violet city: from the 1800s its growers made candied violets — whole flowers coated in sugar — that are still its signature sweet.
  • Napoleon took the violet as his emblem. When he was exiled to Elba in 1814, his supporters wore violets as a secret sign, and said he would return when the violets bloomed.
  • He came back in March 1815, as the violets flowered — and the flower stayed a sign of loyalty to him long after.

Traditional medicine

  • Violet syrup is a traditional cough remedy, long given for coughs and sore throats, especially to children.

Craft

  • The flowers scent perfumes — violet was a classic perfume flower of the 1800s — and they are candied: brushed with sugar and dried, a sweet decoration with a long history in France.
  • (The roots and seeds are a different matter — see the caution below.)
  • In one small double-blind trial, violet syrup eased the cough of children aged 2–12 with intermittent asthma when added to their usual treatment — a single small study, offered as encouragement, not proof.
  • The leaves provide vitamins A and C, and the plant's slippery mucilage is soothing to irritated throats and skin — the traditional reason it is classed as a demulcent, a substance that coats and soothes.
  • Violet contains natural salicylate compounds, the aspirin family of chemicals, which fits its old folk use for aches and inflamed skin.
  • Caution: only the leaves and flowers are eaten. The roots and seeds are not food — the root contains an alkaloid, violine, and is strongly sickening and purgative in larger doses, and some violets are high in saponins, which can cause nausea if too much is eaten.
  • When to plant: Plant young plants or divide clumps in spring, in a shady corner
  • Sun: Some shade — a plant for under trees and shady edges
  • Soil: Moist, rich soil with leaf mould worked in
  • Environment: A hardy perennial that spreads by runners and self-seeding into a ground cover; violets are the only food of fritillary caterpillars, so a patch is a butterfly nursery
  • Planter or ground: Both
  • Spacing and depth: 25–30 cm apart
  • Water: Likes moisture
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Pick the flowers as they open in spring; gather the leaves through the season

Garden notes

  • Violets are the only food of fritillary caterpillars — the great spangled, variegated and regal fritillaries all feed exclusively on violet leaves, so a patch of violets is a butterfly nursery.
  • The seeds carry a small oily parcel (an elaiosome, a fatty attachment that ants eat) that ants carry back to their nests, planting the violets in rich nest soil as they go.
  • A mining bee, Andrena violae, specialises in violets.
  • A shade-tolerant ground cover that spreads by runners and self-seeding, holding soil under trees and along shady edges where grass struggles.

Sources

  • Mittal et al. (2015) — peer-reviewed review of Viola odorata: its mucilage, salicylates and vitamin C, and the traditional cough and skin uses
  • Randomised trial of violet (Viola odorata) syrup in children with intermittent asthma — cough improved as an addition to standard treatment (single small trial)

Vitex

Vitex agnus-castus

MedicinalCraftDriedFruit

A deciduous shrub or small tree in the mint family, Lamiaceae — older books shelve it with the verbena family, and it was moved on modern evidence — native to the Mediterranean and east into Central Asia, where it grows along riverbanks and on coastal ground. In summer it carries long spikes of lilac flowers over leaves divided into narrow leaflets spread like the fingers of a hand, and the whole plant smells peppery when brushed. Its other names — chaste tree, chasteberry, monk's pepper — all point at the fruit: small, hard, dark berries the size of peppercorns, and the only part modern medicine uses.

The parts divide cleanly. The ripe fruit is the medicine, gathered in autumn and dried, and every trial in this entry used a standardised extract of it. The leaves were used in ancient practice and in the old chastity customs, but no modern preparation is made from them. The flowers are garden ornament and bee forage. The root is not used. What the fruit does in the body is unusual for a plant: some of its compounds act on dopamine, a chemical messenger in the brain. Dopamine is the brake on prolactin, a hormone best known for driving milk production; prolactin running slightly high in the days before a period is one of the drivers linked to premenstrual breast pain and other symptoms. Ease the brake's target down, and those symptoms ease with it — that is the working explanation, and it is also why this plant comes with medicine interactions most herbs do not have.

History

  • Vitex is one of the oldest recorded medicines for women's complaints. Hippocrates, in the fourth century BCE, recommended it for injuries, inflammation and use after childbirth; four centuries later Dioscorides described it in his herbal for complaints of the womb and for bringing on milk — and recorded its reputation for cooling sexual desire.
  • The name tells that second story twice over. The Greek word for the plant, agnos, meant chaste; it was heard as the Latin agnus, a lamb, and castus — chaste — was added, so the plant's Latin name reads "chaste lamb".
  • At the Thesmophoria, the women-only festival of Demeter at Athens, women strewed chaste tree leaves on the beds they slept on through the rites, to keep chastity for the festival's length. Pliny records the custom.
  • In medieval monasteries the peppery berries were eaten — and used in the kitchen in place of pepper — by monks who believed they helped keep a vow of celibacy. That is the whole origin of the name monk's pepper.
  • The genus name is older and plainer: Vitex comes from the Latin vieo, to weave or plait — the plant's tough, flexible branches were woven into fences and baskets around the Mediterranean.
  • The modern chapter is German. Physicians there took vitex up for cycle complaints in the twentieth century, and German and Swiss firms produced the standardised fruit extracts — named in the trials as Ze 440 and BNO 1095 — that let an ancient remedy be tested in modern randomised trials.

Craft

  • The long, flexible young branches bend without breaking, and are woven into fences, hurdles and baskets.
  • For premenstrual syndrome, vitex has strong trial evidence. In the landmark trial, 170 women took a standardised fruit extract (20 mg a day) or a dummy preparation for three menstrual cycles; 52% of the vitex group had their symptoms at least halved, against 24% on placebo. Reviews gathering the randomised trials since point the same way — one pooled analysis of the three strictest trials found women on vitex about two and a half times more likely to see their symptoms clear than women on placebo — though trial quality varies and the benefit, while real, is modest: symptoms ease; they do not go away fully. European regulators give the specified extract their "well-established use" standing for premenstrual syndrome, the strongest category a herbal medicine can hold in that system, and Germany's Commission E approved the fruit for premenstrual complaints.
  • For breast pain that comes and goes with the cycle, several placebo-controlled trials find vitex eases it, and reviews of those trials agree. It is the symptom that fits the plant's action on prolactin most directly.
  • For irregular cycles, Germany's Commission E approved the fruit, and the plant has a long record of use for cycles that run long, short or missing. The trial evidence here is thinner than for premenstrual syndrome — reviewers who praise the PMS data call the cycle data weak beside it — and the claim is carried at that strength.
  • For menopause, vitex is sometimes sold for hot flushes and other symptoms, but the trials are few and small and reviewers find the evidence insufficient. No benefit is claimed here.
  • Caution — dopamine medicines: vitex acts on the same brain messenger as medicines that raise or block dopamine — treatments for Parkinson's disease on one side and antipsychotic medicines on the other. It can work against either kind, and the US National Center for Complementary and Integrative Health advises people taking dopamine-related medicines to avoid it.
  • Caution — contraceptives and hormone therapy: vitex changes hormone signalling, and regulators advise that it may interfere with hormonal contraceptives and hormone replacement therapy — a pregnancy has been reported to an Australian regulator in a woman taking vitex alongside a contraceptive pill. It is also best avoided by anyone with a hormone-sensitive condition, such as a cancer that responds to hormones.
  • Caution — pregnancy and breastfeeding: vitex is not recommended in pregnancy — its safety there has not been established — and not while breastfeeding, where lowering prolactin can cut the milk supply. The same action that makes it useful before a period makes it the wrong plant here.
  • In the trials, side effects were usually mild and uncommon: nausea, headache, digestive upset, and occasionally itching or a rash.
  • When to plant: Plant a young shrub in spring, in a warm, sheltered place
  • Sun: Full sun — it likes its crown in full sun
  • Soil: Well-drained soil; at home on the stony, seasonally dry ground along watercourses
  • Environment: Deciduous and fast-growing, reaching one to five metres; in cold districts winter kills the top growth back, and it simply flowers on the new wood the following summer
  • Planter or ground: Ground; a large pot in warm districts
  • Spacing and depth: Give it room — 2–3 m across
  • Water: Average — it stands drought well once established, but flowers and fruits most freely with summer water
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Gather the berries through autumn, as they ripen

Garden notes

  • A plant of Mediterranean riverbanks and stream sides: it likes its roots near water but its crown in full sun, and it tolerates the stony, seasonally dry ground along watercourses.
  • Deciduous and fast-growing, it reaches one to five metres. In cold districts winter kills the top growth back to the ground, and the plant simply flowers on the new wood the following summer.
  • It asks for well-drained soil and sun; once established it stands drought well, though it flowers and fruits most freely with summer water.
  • The lilac flower spikes open in summer, when few other shrubs are in bloom, and are rich in nectar — bees and butterflies work them heavily.
  • The berries ripen through autumn and are gathered then; a shrub in a warm, sheltered place will fruit reliably year after year.

Sources

  • Kew, Plants of the World Online — Vitex agnus-castus, family placement and native range
  • Schellenberg (2001) — agnus castus fruit extract for premenstrual syndrome, randomised placebo-controlled trial, BMJ
  • van Die et al. (2013) — systematic review of vitex for premenstrual syndrome, premenstrual dysphoric disorder, cyclical breast pain and menopause, Planta Medica
  • Cerqueira et al. (2017) — systematic review of vitex for premenstrual syndrome and premenstrual dysphoric disorder, Archives of Women's Mental Health
  • Halaska et al. (1999) — vitex against placebo for cyclical breast pain, The Breast
  • European Medicines Agency (2018) — European Union herbal monograph on Vitex agnus-castus L., fructus: well-established use in premenstrual syndrome
  • German Commission E (1992) — monograph approving agnus castus fruit for menstrual cycle irregularities, premenstrual syndrome and breast pain
  • National Center for Complementary and Integrative Health — Chasteberry: Usefulness and Safety, interactions and pregnancy cautions

Yarrow

Achillea millefolium

MedicinalCraftFlowerLeaf

One of the oldest traditional wound herbs — long used on small cuts and superficial wounds, and known as “soldier's woundwort” from its battlefield history. The flower heads and leaves — the parts above ground — are the parts used.

History

  • Legend tells that Achilles used yarrow on his soldiers' wounds at Troy. The story is legend, not record — but it gave the plant its Latin name, Achillea.
  • Greek and Roman writers listed yarrow among their wound herbs, and the legend only says in story form what practice did for real.
  • For centuries after, it was a battlefield herb — soldier's woundwort in English folk speech — and it was still being gathered for wounds in the American Civil War.
  • In China, yarrow had a different honour: its straight stalks were the classic stalks thrown to read the I Ching, the old book of changes — a use kept for thousands of years.
  • Its other Latin name is a plain description: millefolium, thousand leaves, for the leaf cut into a thousand fine parts.

Traditional medicine

  • European regulators list this among its traditional uses.
  • Traditionally used for temporary loss of appetite, and for mild, cramping digestive complaints such as bloating and wind.
  • Traditionally used for minor cramps associated with menstrual periods.
  • These uses are traditional, based on long-standing use: the European Medicines Agency notes there is not enough clinical-trial evidence to confirm them.

Craft

  • The flowers give a yellow dye for wool and cloth.
  • Caution: do not use if you are allergic to plants in the daisy family, such as ragweed or chamomile. Avoid in pregnancy and for children under 12, and take care alongside blood-thinning medicines, as yarrow can add to their effect — check with a clinician first.
  • When to plant: Sow seed in spring, or divide clumps — it spreads and self-seeds
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Dry, poor soil where many plants struggle; rich soil makes it floppy
  • Environment: A hardy perennial, drought-tolerant once established, and used for erosion control on steep slopes
  • Planter or ground: Both
  • Spacing and depth: Sow on the surface or just below; 30–45 cm apart
  • Water: Dry once settled
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Gather the leaves young, and the flowering tops as they open, through summer

Garden notes

  • The flat flower heads attract pollinators and beneficial insects, including hoverflies, ladybirds, lacewings and tachinid flies that help control garden pests.
  • Its flowers also feed butterflies and other wildlife.
  • Used in land restoration, and for erosion control on steep slopes.
  • Drought tolerant once established, and grows in dry, poor soils where many plants struggle.

Sources

  • European Medicines Agency, yarrow herb and flower monographs — traditional-use indications; daisy-family allergy and pregnancy cautions
  • European Medicines Agency, yarrow public summary — conclusions rest on traditional use
  • USDA Natural Resources Conservation Service, Plant Guide — beneficial-insect value, erosion control and drought tolerance

Garlic

Allium sativum

CulinaryMedicinalCraftFreshCookedRoot

A bulb of the onion family, and one of the oldest flavourings in the world — a plant people have grown for at least 5,000 years less as a vegetable than as a force: the pungency that makes it indispensable in the kitchen is the plant's own chemical defence, and it is also where nearly everything studied about garlic begins.

Varieties

Music (hardneck, Porcelain type)

the gardener's standard: big bulbs of four to six fat cloves in white wrappers, strong, clean garlic flavour, and it peels easily. Cold-hardy; stores moderately.

Chesnok Red (hardneck, Purple Stripe type)

purple-striped wrappers over cloves with a rich, sweet depth that keeps its flavour in cooking. One of the best roasting garlics.

Inchelium Red (softneck, Artichoke type)

a mild, well-balanced heirloom from Washington state, with many cloves in layers. An award-winner in taste tests, and the soft neck braids well.

Silver White (softneck, Silverskin type)

the longest keeper of all: dense white bulbs that can store ten months or more. The last garlic in the pantry before the new harvest.

Elephant garlic

not garlic at all, honestly labelled: it is a leek relative (Allium ampeloprasum) bred to grow enormous, mild bulbs. Gentle and sweet roasted, but a different plant with a fraction of true garlic's pungency.

Around the World

Lebanon — toum: garlic whipped with oil, lemon and salt into a white, light sauce, eaten with grilled chicken and fries — garlic as the main event, not the seasoning.
China — Laba garlic: cloves pickled in vinegar until they turn emerald green, made for the Laba festival and eaten with dumplings through the New Year season.
Spain — sopa de ajo, the Castilian garlic soup of bread, paprika, garlic and a poached egg; and ajo blanco, a chilled white soup of garlic, almonds and bread from Málaga.
Korea — garlic at nearly every meal: pickled whole cloves (jangajji), cloves grilled and wrapped in lettuce with barbecue, and black garlic — bulbs aged warm until black, soft and sweet as balsamic.

Origin & Lineage

  • Garlic's wild ancestor grew in Central Asia, in the mountains of what is now Kyrgyzstan and Tajikistan. From there it travelled every road there was: into Egypt, where it fed the pyramid builders and was buried with Tutankhamun; into Greece and Rome, where soldiers and athletes ate it before exertion; and east along the Silk Road into China.
  • Spanish, Portuguese and French ships carried it to the Americas in the 1500s, and it took root in every kitchen it met. Few plants are as universal: it is hard to name a major cuisine that does not begin, somewhere, with garlic in hot oil.
  • All garlic branches into two great families. Hardneck garlic bolts — it sends up a flower stalk — and forms a single ring of large cloves around a stiff stem; it is the cold-hardy kind, with the deepest flavour. Softneck garlic does not bolt, forms many smaller cloves in layers, stores far longer, and is the kind that braids.
  • Garlic barely flowers and almost never sets true seed. Like the banana, it is a clone crop: every bulb ever planted is a piece of an older bulb, so a variety is a living line carried hand to hand for centuries.

Craft

  • Softneck garlic is braided: the soft stems are plaited into strings with the bulbs hanging along them — a way of storing the harvest that doubles as a kitchen ornament. Hardneck kinds, with their stiff stems, are bundled or netted instead.
  • A whole bulb is concentrated nutrition — per 100 g, garlic provides vitamin B6 (about 1.2 mg, over 70% of the daily value), manganese (about 1.7 mg), vitamin C (about 31 mg) and selenium. But honesty about portions matters: garlic is eaten in grams, not hundreds of grams, so its real gift is its compounds, not bulk nutrition.
  • The signature compound is allicin — and allicin does not exist in a whole clove. Inside the intact clove, a substance called alliin sits in one compartment and an enzyme in another; crushing or chopping breaks the walls between them, and the enzyme makes allicin, the source of raw garlic's bite. Heat destroys the enzyme within moments, which is why raw garlic is fiercest. Crushed garlic left to rest about ten minutes before cooking lets the allicin form first — the cook's trick that keeps more of it.
  • Blood pressure: pooled analyses of clinical trials find that garlic supplements — aged garlic extract or standardised powder, roughly 600–1,200 mg a day taken for months — lower the top blood-pressure number by about 5 points on average, and by around 8 points in people who start with high blood pressure. Those are concentrated supplement doses, not the clove or two in a stir-fry, and garlic in any form is not a substitute for prescribed blood-pressure medicine.
  • Cholesterol: the same trial literature finds small reductions in total and LDL cholesterol from garlic supplements — real, but modest, and again at supplement doses.
  • Colds: one trial found fewer colds in people taking a daily garlic supplement, but it stands nearly alone; reviewers judge the evidence too thin to promise anything. Garlic's reputation against colds is older than its proof.
  • Caution: garlic supplements thin the blood slightly, and at supplement doses they can add to the effect of blood-thinning medicines and are usually stopped before surgery. That caution belongs to concentrated supplements — ordinary cooking amounts are food, and are not the concern. Raw garlic can also burn an empty stomach, and raw garlic paste left on skin can blister it.
  • When to plant: Plant cloves in autumn, pointed end up — garlic needs winter cold to divide into cloves properly; a bulb planted in spring often grows as one round, undivided ball
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Well-drained, fertile soil; wet winter ground rots the bulbs
  • Environment: Fully hardy — the winter cold is required, not just endured
  • Planter or ground: Ground best; wide pots work
  • Spacing and depth: Cloves 15 cm apart, about 5 cm (2 in) deep
  • Water: Average — water in dry springs, and stop as the leaves yellow
  • Companion plants: Carrots and lettuce are good neighbours. Keep apart from beans and peas, which garlic holds back
  • When to pick: Harvest in early summer when the lower leaves have yellowed but a few green ones remain; cure dry, with air moving around the bulbs, for several weeks

Garden notes

  • Plant cloves in autumn, pointed end up, a few inches deep, in full sun and well-drained soil. Garlic needs winter cold to divide into cloves properly; a bulb planted in spring often grows as one round, undivided ball.
  • Hardneck types announce summer with the scape — a curling flower stalk that loops like a pig's tail. Cut it when it has curled once: the plant then puts its energy into the bulb, and the scape itself is a harvest — mild, green garlic flavour for pesto, stir-fries and grilling.
  • Young garlic leaves are eaten too, snipped like chives in spring cooking — the whole plant gives, from green leaf to scape to bulb.
  • Harvest when the lower leaves have yellowed but a few green ones remain, loosen with a fork rather than pulling, and cure the bulbs dry with air moving around them for several weeks before storing.
  • Save your biggest, healthiest bulbs as next year's seed garlic. Because garlic is a clone, your planting stock is quite literally your harvest, continued.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — Garlic, raw
  • Song & Milner (2001) — how allicin forms when a clove is crushed
  • Ried et al. (2016) — pooled trials of garlic supplements and blood pressure
  • Ried et al. (2013) — garlic supplements and cholesterol
  • Lissiman et al. (2014) — Cochrane review of garlic for the common cold
  • NCCIH — Garlic: usefulness and safety

Ginger

Zingiber officinale

CulinaryMedicinalFreshDriedRoot

The knobbly "root" in the produce aisle is not a root at all — it is a rhizome, a thick underground stem that creeps sideways through the soil, sending leaves up and true roots down. Ginger is a tropical plant of the same family as turmeric and cardamom, and one of the first spices ever traded across the world.

Varieties

Jamaican

pale, thin-skinned and silky, with a fine, lemony heat. The classic export ginger, long prized for ginger beer and for preserving.

Cochin (Indian)

from Kerala's coast: plump hands with a warm, lemon-touched pungency. Much of it is dried for the spice trade; the fresh form is a staple of Indian kitchens.

Chinese (Canton)

the supermarket standard: big, plump, mild and juicy, with skin thin enough to scrape off with a spoon. Bred by centuries of selection for size and mildness.

Nigerian

smaller, darker and very pungent, with an unusually high oil content. West African cooking's ginger, and increasingly exported for its strength.

Young ginger

not a variety but a harvest stage: rhizomes lifted early, at four to six months, before the skin toughens. Pink-tipped, almost skinless, mild and juicy — the ginger that pickles into the pale slices served with sushi.

Around the World

India — fresh ginger pounded with garlic into the paste that begins half the curries of the subcontinent; adrak chai, tea boiled strong with ginger; ginger in sweets and in the digestive chew of the after-dinner tray.
Japan — gari, the sweet-pickled young ginger eaten between bites of sushi to clear the palate; and beni shōga, red pickled ginger strips over fried noodles and rice bowls.
China — ginger-scallion sauce over poached chicken; ginger fried in sesame oil for the restorative dishes of the month after childbirth; brown-sugar ginger tea taken for chills.
Jamaica & the Caribbean — fiery homemade ginger beer, brewed strong enough to bite; ginger worked into jerk seasoning and into sorrel drink at Christmas.

Origin & Lineage

  • Ginger was first grown in maritime Southeast Asia and spread very early: Austronesian voyagers carried it in their canoes across the Pacific and west to India thousands of years ago, making it one of the travelling plants of the ancient world.
  • From India it moved along the Silk Road and the sea routes to Persia, Arabia and the Mediterranean. By Roman times it was a costly import in Europe; by the medieval period it was common enough that a pound of ginger cost about a sheep — and prized enough to be worth it.
  • Spanish ships carried it to the Caribbean and South America in the 1500s, and Jamaica became so famous for its pale, silky-skinned ginger that "Jamaican ginger" set the standard for the export trade.
  • Cultivated ginger almost never flowers or sets seed, so — like garlic and banana — every plant is a piece of an older plant, a rhizome divided and replanted. Its varieties are regional forms rather than bred cultivars: the same species, shaped by the soils and tastes of Jamaica, India, China and West Africa.
  • Nausea is ginger's strongest ground. Pooled analyses of clinical trials find that about 1 to 1.5 grams of ginger a day — roughly a thumb-sized piece of fresh rhizome, grated — measurably eases nausea in pregnancy, and the American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists lists ginger among the non-drug options for it. For motion sickness and for nausea during chemotherapy, the trial results are mixed but lean positive, as an addition to standard care rather than a replacement for it.
  • The pungency comes from gingerols — the bite compounds of the fresh rhizome. Drying or cooking converts some gingerols into shogaols, which are more pungent still. Dried ginger is not weaker ginger; it just works differently, which is why fresh and dried are not interchangeable in a recipe.
  • Per 100 g fresh, ginger provides about 80 kcal, potassium (415 mg), magnesium (43 mg) and a little vitamin C — but as with garlic, ginger is eaten by the spoonful, not the plateful. Its value is what a small amount does: flavour, warmth and the studied effects above, at almost no caloric cost.
  • Ginger also speeds the emptying of the stomach in clinical studies — food moves on rather than sitting — which fits its long traditional place at the end of rich meals.
  • Trials of ginger for joint pain and for exercise muscle soreness find small reductions in pain — worth having, honestly stated as small.
  • Caution: in the amounts used in food and in the trials above, ginger is considered safe, including in pregnancy. High-dose ginger supplements are a different matter — they can cause heartburn, may add to the effect of blood-thinning medicines, and their use late in pregnancy is debated — so concentrated supplements are worth a word with a clinician first.
  • When to plant: Start a plump rhizome indoors in late winter, grow it on in a wide pot through summer, and bring it in before the first frost
  • Sun: Some shade — light shade suits it best
  • Soil: Rich, moist, well-drained soil
  • Environment: Tropical and slow: it wants eight to ten warm months
  • Planter or ground: Pots — a wide container suits the spreading rhizome
  • Spacing and depth: Set the rhizome just under the surface, its pale buds (the eyes) facing up
  • Water: Likes water — keep it moist and warm while it wakes; sprouts can take weeks
  • Companion plants: No strong companions known
  • When to pick: Young ginger can be lifted from about four months; mature ginger is ready at eight to ten months, when the leaves yellow and fall

Garden notes

  • Ginger is tropical and slow: it wants eight to ten warm months. In temperate gardens it is a pot plant — start a rhizome indoors in late winter, grow it in a wide container through summer, and bring it in before the first frost.
  • Plant a plump, firm piece of rhizome with visible pale buds — the "eyes" — just under the surface of rich, moist, well-drained soil, and keep it warm and shaded while it wakes. It is in no hurry; sprouts can take weeks.
  • Young ginger can be lifted from about four months, breaking off what you need and leaving the rest growing; mature ginger is ready at eight to ten months, when the leaves yellow and fall.
  • The leaves are not a food. With ginger, the harvest is the rhizome alone — the green top is the plant's engine, not an ingredient.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — Ginger root, raw
  • Viljoen et al. (2014) — pooled trials of ginger for nausea in pregnancy
  • American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists — ginger among non-drug options for nausea in pregnancy
  • NCCIH — Ginger: usefulness and safety

Kale

Brassica oleracea (Acephala Group)

CulinaryFreshCookedLeaf

Kale is what cabbage was before it learned to fold. It belongs to one of the most remarkable species in the garden: Brassica oleracea, the wild cabbage of Europe's coasts, which human hands have bred into cabbage, broccoli, cauliflower, Brussels sprouts, kohlrabi and kale — one plant, many bodies. Of them all, kale stayed closest to the wild leaf.

Varieties

Curly kale ('Winterbor', 'Blue Curled Scotch')

the standard: tightly frilled blue-green leaves on sturdy plants, sweetest after frost. The kale of Dutch stamppot and German Grünkohl.

Lacinato ('Nero di Toscana', dinosaur kale)

long, strap-shaped leaves of blue-black green, blistered like reptile skin, from Tuscany. Milder and sweeter than curly kale, and the leaf for ribollita soup and for kale salads.

Red Russian

flat, oak-shaped leaves with purple stems and veins, tender enough to eat raw when young. It came from Siberia with Russian traders to Canada around 1885, and it is the mildest of the common kales.

Redbor

curly kale in deep wine-purple, bred in the Netherlands: ornamental enough for a flower border, fully edible, and its colour deepens with cold.

Siberian

very large, gently frilled leaves on exceptionally cold-hardy plants; the kale for the shortest seasons and the deepest winters.

Around the World

Netherlands — boerenkoolstamppot: curly kale boiled and mashed through potatoes with a knob of butter, served with smoked sausage and gravy — the Dutch winter national dish.
Germany — Grünkohl: kale braised low and slow with onions and served with Pinkel sausage and cured pork, traditionally after the first frost, and the centre of north German kale festivals and kale tours.
Portugal — caldo verde: potatoes simmered and mashed into a broth, with kale (the flat couve) shaved into ribbons so fine they cook in a minute, and a slice of chouriço on top.
Scotland — kale as the household green for centuries: kail broth with barley, kale with oatmeal, and the kale yard outside every cottage door.

Origin & Lineage

  • The wild ancestor still grows on limestone cliffs along the Mediterranean and Atlantic coasts of Europe — a loose rosette of waxy blue-green leaves, salt-tolerant and tough. Leafy kales were grown by the Greeks and Romans; Cato the Elder praised the leaf cabbage above other vegetables more than 2,000 years ago.
  • Curly kale became the winter green of northern Europe, where it sweetens after frost and stands in the garden when little else will. In Scotland the kale yard — the kail yairt — was the household garden itself, and "kail" long meant the dinner, not just the plant.
  • Colonists carried kale to North America, where it stayed a regional green for centuries — a garnish leaf under salad-bar bowls — before its nutrient density made it famous in the 2010s. The plant had not changed; the attention had.
  • Its siblings tell the family's story: cabbage is the same leaf folded into a ball, Brussels sprouts are buds along its stem, broccoli and cauliflower are its flowers bred enormous. Kale kept the original arrangement — an open rosette of leaves — and is the form a Roman farmer would still recognise.
  • Vitamin K above everything: raw kale carries about 390 µg per 100 g — more than three times the daily value in a single serving's worth of leaves. Vitamin K is the vitamin the body uses to clot blood normally and to maintain bone.
  • At only about 35 kcal per 100 g, kale also provides vitamin C (about 93 mg, a full day's worth), vitamin A (about 241 µg RAE, from beta-carotene) and folate — the nutrient profile that put it at the top of the nutrient-density rankings.
  • Calcium worth counting: about 254 mg per 100 g, and kale's calcium is unusually well absorbed. Kale is low in oxalates — the compounds that lock calcium away in spinach — and studies find roughly 40% or more of kale's calcium is taken up by the body, comparing well with the calcium in milk.
  • Kale carries the eye pigments lutein and zeaxanthin in quantity (about 6 mg per 100 g) — the yellow pigments that gather in the retina, shared with green beans and sweet corn.
  • Like all its cabbage kin, kale contains glucosinolates — the family's sulphur defence compounds — which break down when the leaf is chopped into the pungent compounds studied for their protective effects in the diet. Population studies link diets rich in these vegetables with lower rates of several cancers; that is a dietary pattern, not a promise from any single leaf.
  • Caution: kale is one of the richest vitamin K foods there is, so people who take warfarin are advised to keep their intake of it steady from week to week — not to avoid it, but not to swing between none and a great deal either.
  • Caution: kale contains goitrogens, compounds that can interfere with the thyroid's use of iodine. At honest strength: in ordinary servings this is not a concern for healthy people; the risk belongs to eating very large amounts of raw kale day after day, especially where iodine is short. Cooking reduces the goitrogens, and kale is among the milder brassicas for them.
  • When to plant: Sow indoors in early spring or outside from spring; sow again in late summer for autumn and winter picking
  • Sun: Full sun, or a little shade in hot summers
  • Soil: Rich, firm soil that holds moisture
  • Environment: Cool-season and very hardy — frost converts some of the leaf's starches to sugars, so kale picked after a frost is sweeter. Net against cabbage white caterpillars
  • Planter or ground: Both — one plant to a large pot
  • Spacing and depth: 45 cm apart; sow 1 cm deep
  • Water: Likes water in dry spells
  • Companion plants: Onions, garlic and herbs are good neighbours
  • When to pick: From about 55 days, pick the outer leaves and leave the growing crown — one plant gives for months

Garden notes

  • A cool-season plant that improves with cold: frost converts some of the leaf's starches to sugars, so kale picked after a frost is measurably sweeter. Sow in late summer for autumn and winter harvests, or in spring for summer picking.
  • Pick it as cut-and-come-again: take the outer leaves and leave the growing crown, and one plant gives for months. Kale is biennial — in its second spring it flowers, and those flowers are early, generous food for bees; let one plant go and you can save seed.
  • Eat the stems too. The thick midrib is the part cooks strip out for salads — but chopped and cooked a few minutes longer than the leaf, or pickled, the stems are crisp and good. With kale, leaf and stem are both food; only the oldest woody base is for the compost.
  • It shares the cabbage family's pests, above all the cabbage white butterfly, whose caterpillars can skeletonise a plant. A fine net over the row is the simple answer, and it keeps the harvest clean without sprays.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — Kale, raw
  • Heaney & Weaver (1990) — how well kale's calcium is absorbed
  • NIH Office of Dietary Supplements — Vitamin K fact sheet

Corn

Zea mays

CulinaryMedicinalCraftDriedCookedFlowerSeed

First, the plain fact: corn is a grain. Botanically it is a giant grass, and its ear is a cluster of grains — seeds — on a cob. Sweet corn is eaten young and milky, as a vegetable, but the plant's life and history are the life of a cereal, kin to wheat, rice and sorghum, and one of the three grains that feed the world.

Varieties

Sweet corn ('Golden Bantam' and the modern supersweets)

bred to hold sugar in the kernel and eaten young, in the milk stage, as a vegetable. Golden Bantam (1902) made yellow sweet corn America's standard; modern supersweet types stay sweet for days after picking instead of hours.

Dent corn

the world's bulk corn, named for the dent that dimples each kernel as it dries. Field corn: ground for meal and masa, fermented, and fed to livestock. Most corn grown on earth is dent.

Flint corn

hard, glassy kernels in bright colours — reds, blues, oranges, calico mixes. Traditional for polenta meal in Italy and for the ornamental ears of autumn; modern selections like 'Glass Gem' are grown for their beauty and still grind into meal.

Flour corn

soft, starchy kernels that grind easily to fine flour. The corn of the Hopi and other Pueblo peoples, whose blue varieties are bred from centuries of dryland selection.

Popcorn

the oldest type of all, close to teosinte's form: a hard hull seals each kernel so steam builds inside until it bursts. Popped corn has been found in ancient Peruvian and Mexican sites thousands of years old.

Blue corn (Hopi types)

flour corn in deep blue-purple, carrying the anthocyanin pigments; the corn of blue cornmeal, tortillas and Hopi piki bread.

Around the World

Mexico — the homeland: nixtamal ground to masa for tortillas and tamales — the tamales steamed in corn's own husks — pozole of whole hominy kernels, and elote, corn on the cob grilled and dressed with lime, salt, chilli and cheese.
Italy — polenta: cornmeal stirred to a thick porridge in the north, where corn arrived in the 1600s and became the poor person's staple — with pellagra in the regions that never learned nixtamalization.
American South — grits, ground from hominy — which is nixtamalized corn, the old technique surviving under another name — and cornbread from stone-ground meal.
Africa — stiff cornmeal porridges under many names: ugali in East Africa, nsima in Malawi, sadza in Zimbabwe, eaten by hand with greens and relishes — corn, an American plant, become an African staple within a few centuries.

Origin & Lineage

  • Corn was bred from teosinte, a wild grass of the Balsas River valley in Mexico, beginning about 9,000 years ago. Teosinte's ear is almost unrecognisable — a few centimetres long, with a dozen kernels each sealed in a stony case. Indigenous farmers selected it, generation by generation, into an ear of hundreds of exposed kernels that cannot scatter or survive without human hands. Corn is a plant that exists only because people do.
  • It spread the length of the Americas and was transformed everywhere it landed: giant-kernelled corn in the Andes, flour corns in the Southwest, flint corns in the north. The Maya and Aztec worlds ran on it; the very word maize comes from mahís, the Taíno name, carried into Spanish.
  • Mesoamerica's cooks made a discovery as important as the plant: nixtamalization — soaking and cooking the kernels in an alkaline solution, water with slaked lime (calcium hydroxide, from burned limestone) or wood ash. The treatment loosens the hulls so they slip off, adds calcium from the lime, and frees the niacin — vitamin B3 — that untreated corn keeps locked in a form the body cannot use. Where corn later travelled without the technique, into poor regions of Europe and the American South, the niacin-deficiency disease pellagra followed in its wake. Where the technique travelled with it, it did not.
  • After 1492 corn crossed the oceans and became a staple from Africa to China within two centuries. Today its descendants branch into five great kernel types — dent, flint, flour, sweet and popcorn — from one Mexican grass.

Traditional medicine

  • The rest of the plant has its traditional uses: corn silk — the strands from the ear's tip — is dried for a mild traditional tea, and the husks and cobs work in the kitchen (see Around the World and the Growing Guide). The silk tea's reputation as a diuretic is folk use; the evidence for it is thin, and it is stated here as tradition, not treatment.

Craft

  • The husks are a craft material: soaked until soft, they are woven and braided into mats, baskets, dolls and chair seats — a craft carried from Indigenous American makers into folk tradition today.
  • As a vegetable, sweet corn provides about 86 kcal per 100 g, fibre (about 2 g), vitamin C (about 7 mg), folate and B vitamins — a modest, useful profile, and yellow corn carries the eye pigments lutein and zeaxanthin (about 0.6 mg per 100 g), shared with kale and green beans.
  • As a grain, judge it whole: whole-grain cornmeal carries about 7 g of fibre per 100 g, with iron and magnesium — but only if the whole kernel, germ included, is ground. Degerminated meal, stripped for shelf life, loses much of both fibre and minerals. The whole kernel is the point.
  • Blue and purple corns carry anthocyanins — the same pigment family that colours blueberries and Okinawan sweet potatoes — in the outer layers of the kernel. Colour, again, is a fair guide to content.
  • Nixtamalization is that rare thing, a processing step that adds nutrition: masa and hominy gain calcium from the lime and niacin the body can actually use. The tortilla is nutritionally more complete than the raw kernel it came from.
  • Corn's starch makes it an energy food first. Sweet corn's glycaemic index is moderate — around the low 50s — so it raises blood sugar at a middle pace, neither the spike of white bread nor the steadiness of beans. Eaten as one part of a mixed plate, as it almost always is, that is an ordinary place for a staple grain to sit.
  • When to plant: Sow outside after the last frost, into warm soil, or start in pots a few weeks earlier
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Rich soil — a hungry crop; work in compost before sowing
  • Environment: Warm-season, with no patience for frost; plant in blocks, not single rows, so the wind can carry the pollen from the tassels to the silks
  • Planter or ground: Ground — it needs a block of plants to pollinate well
  • Spacing and depth: Sow 2.5 cm deep, 30 cm apart, in blocks
  • Water: Likes water, above all while the ears are swelling
  • Companion plants: Beans and squash are the classic partners — the Three Sisters: corn gives the beans their pole, and squash shades the soil
  • When to pick: About 70–90 days; an ear is ready when the silks have browned and a thumbnail pressed into a kernel gives milky juice

Garden notes

  • A warm-season, hungry, thirsty grass: full sun, rich soil, steady water, and no patience for frost. It grows fast enough to watch.
  • Corn is wind-pollinated, so plant it in blocks, not single rows. The tassels at the top are the male flowers; their pollen must fall on the silks below, and every single strand of silk leads to one kernel — a gap-toothed ear is a map of the silks the pollen missed. This is also the Three Sisters' pole: corn gives the beans their trellis.
  • The whole plant works. Husks wrap tamales and dry into crafts and braided goods; spent cobs simmer into stock and corn-cob jelly and smoulder for smoke flavour; the stalks chop down into mulch and compost that return the plant's bulk to the soil.
  • Save seed from open-pollinated varieties only, and from many plants at once — corn inbreeds quickly, and seed from a handful of ears weakens within a few generations. Hybrid sweet corn seed will not come true, and its kernels are the one part of this plant that belongs to the seed companies.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — Corn, sweet, yellow, raw
  • USDA FoodData Central — Cornmeal, whole-grain, yellow
  • Matsuoka et al. (2002) — corn's domestication from teosinte
  • Grobman et al. (2012) — ancient popped corn in Peru

Collard Greens

Brassica oleracea var. viridis

CulinaryCookedLeaf

A loose-leafed brassica that never learned to make a head — and never needed to. Collards are cabbage's older, plainer branch: broad flat leaves on a sturdy stalk, bred for the leaf alone, and one of the oldest greens in cultivation. In the American South they became far more than a vegetable.

Varieties

Georgia Southern (Georgia)

the heirloom standard of the South: tall plants with large, smooth, blue-green leaves and a mild flavour that sweetens after frost. Slow-cooked greens and big harvests.

Vates

a compact, blue-green type bred in Virginia (the name carries the state's experiment station initials): shorter plants, tender leaves, and notably slow to bolt in spring. Gardens and smaller spaces.

Champion

a Vates-type hybrid selected for dark blue-green colour, heavy yields and extra bolt resistance, with leaves that hold well in the field. Market gardens.

Morris Heading

an old North Carolina heirloom that half-folds its leaves into a loose, open rosette — as close as a collard comes to heading. A curiosity with true heritage flavour.

Tiger Hybrid

fast and vigorous, with bright green, slightly savoyed leaves; ready sooner than the heirlooms. Early harvests.

Around the World

American South — the classic: greens simmered with smoked turkey or ham hock, finished with vinegar and pepper sauce, the pot liquor soaked up with cornbread. A New Year's plate with black-eyed peas and pork is eaten for luck and prosperity.
Brazil — couve: collard leaves rolled tight, shaved into the thinnest ribbons and flash-cooked with garlic — the green that always sits beside feijoada, the black-bean stew.
East Africa — in Kenya, sukuma wiki — "stretch the week" — is kale's close kin cooked with onion and tomato to make a small piece of meat feed everyone; collards step into the same role wherever they grow.
Portugal — caldo verde, the green soup: potato broth with collard-family leaves shaved hair-fine and stirred in at the last moment, so they stay bright.

Origin & Lineage

  • Collards descend from wild cabbage on the coasts of Europe, and they keep its oldest form. Long before anyone bred a cabbage head, Greeks and Romans grew loose-leafed brassicas of exactly this kind — collards are among the earliest branches of the Brassica oleracea family tree, kin to kale, and selected, like it, for leaves rather than a bud.
  • The plant travelled the Atlantic world early: collards were growing in North America by the 1600s, and they were cultivated in Africa as well, where leafy brassicas fitted into long traditions of slow-cooked greens.
  • In the American South, enslaved African cooks made collards their own. Greens simmered low and slow with smoked meat, the cooking liquid — pot liquor — saved and prized: a cuisine of patience and resourcefulness that turned a hardy garden green into a cornerstone of African diaspora foodways, carried through the crossing and the centuries after it. Collards remain a dish of gathering and memory, cooked for New Year's Day — where their green is said to promise money in the year ahead — and for tables where everyone is fed.
  • Modern varieties branch from that heritage — flat-leafed Southern standards, blue-green types bred to shrug off frost, and hybrids bred for tender leaves and slow bolting.
  • Per 100 g raw, collards give: 32 kcal per 100 g raw, with vitamin K at 437 µg — more than three and a half times the daily value — vitamin C at about 35 mg (39% of the daily value), folate at 129 µg (about a third of the daily value) and fibre at about 4 g.
  • Unusually rich in calcium for a leaf: about 232 mg per 100 g, rivalling kale. And unlike spinach, collards are low in oxalates — the compounds that lock calcium away — so more of that calcium is available to the body. That pairing is rare among greens.
  • Rich in vitamin A (about 251 µg RAE per 100 g) in the form of beta-carotene, which the body converts as needed, and in lutein — a pigment concentrated in the eye, where it helps filter light. Collards carry about 4,300 µg of lutein and its partner zeaxanthin per 100 g, among the highest of any common vegetable.
  • Like all their brassica kin, collards carry glucosinolates — the sharp sulfur compounds behind the family bite — which convert as the leaves are chopped and cooked into compounds studied for how they help the body deal with harmful substances. Population studies link regular eating of cruciferous vegetables with lower rates of some cancers; that is a pattern across the whole family, not a proven prevention by collards alone.
  • The whole plant earns its place: the thick stems are food too — chopped fine and cooked a little longer than the leaves, or pickled — and long cooking is not waste here, because the pot liquor holds the vitamins that leach out. Tradition drinks it for good reason.
  • Caution: collards are very high in vitamin K, which matters for blood clotting. People taking warfarin or similar blood thinners should keep their intake of greens like these steady from week to week rather than swinging between none and a great deal, and mention it to their clinician — the same note that applies to kale.
  • When to plant: Sow in spring for summer picking, or in late summer in the South for autumn and winter greens
  • Sun: Full sun
  • Soil: Rich, firm soil that holds moisture
  • Environment: One of the toughest vegetables in the garden: it takes light frost easily and turns sweeter after it, and endures hot Southern summers. Net young plants against cabbage whites
  • Planter or ground: Ground best; large pots work
  • Spacing and depth: 45–60 cm apart; sow 1 cm deep
  • Water: Likes water in dry spells
  • Companion plants: Onions, garlic and herbs are good neighbours
  • When to pick: From about 55 days, pick the lowest leaves first — the plant keeps growing from its crown and gives greens for months

Garden notes

  • One of the toughest vegetables in the garden: a cool-season crop that takes light frost easily, and in fact turns sweeter after it — the plant moves sugars into its leaves as protection against the cold.
  • Pick from the bottom up: harvest the lowest leaves first and the plant keeps growing from its crown, giving greens for months from one sowing. The thick stems go to the pot chopped fine, or to the compost.
