  • Vitamin K: 16.4 µg per 100 g with the peel on — about 14% of the daily value. Vitamin K is needed for normal blood clotting and for maintaining bone. Most of it sits in and just under the peel, which is a reason to eat slicing cucumbers unpeeled.
  • Modest extras: potassium 147 mg, vitamin C 2.8 mg and fibre 0.5 g per 100 g. Cucumber is not a nutrient-dense food, and no disease claim is made for it.
  • Caution — bitterness: cucumbers carry cucurbitacins, intensely bitter defensive compounds concentrated near the stem end and the peel. Cultivated types are bred to hold very little, but heat and drought stress raise the level. A cucumber that tastes strongly bitter should be spat out, not eaten — a heavy dose of cucurbitacin can bring on stomach cramps and diarrhoea.
  • When to plant: Sow outside after the last frost; the first frost kills the plant.
  • Sun: Full sun.
  • Soil: Rich soil that holds moisture and drains well.
  • Environment: A warm-season vine; drought and heat stress are what turn fruit bitter.
  • Planter or ground: Ground, or up a trellis — a trellis keeps fruit straight, clean and easy to find, and saves ground.
  • Spacing and depth: Sow 1–2 cm deep; space plants about 30–45 cm apart on a trellis, wider on the ground.
  • Start inside or outside: Sow outside where they are to grow, or start inside shortly before the last frost.
  • Water: Water steadily.
  • Companions: Beans, peas and corn are classic companions.
  • When to pick: Pick often and pick young.

Garden notes

  • Standard types need bees — each female flower needs pollen carried from a male flower — while greenhouse types set fruit without them.
  • A cucumber left to swell and yellow tells the vine to stop setting new ones, so picking is also pruning.
  • Cucumber climbs by tendrils and takes happily to fences and netting as well as trellises.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for cucumber, with peel, raw
  • Chomicki et al. (2020) — genomic evidence for cucumber's domestication in India
  • Kew, Plants of the World Online — Cucumis sativus: family and native range
  • Pliny the Elder (77 CE) — Roman account of cucumbers grown out of season for Tiberius

Zucchini

Cucurbita pepo

CulinaryCookedFruitFlowerSeed

A summer squash picked young, while the rind is tender and the seeds still soft — the same fruit that, left on the vine, would harden into a marrow. Zucchini is mild, watery and quick-cooking: it grills, sautés, bakes into breads and disappears into sauces, and its golden flowers are a food in their own right. The fruit is the main part eaten; the blossoms are prized, and the seeds are eaten with the young fruit. It is the same species as pumpkin — Cucurbita pepo — and shares that species with acorn and spaghetti squash as well.

Varieties

Black Beauty

Skin
Flesh

The long-standing standard: dark green, glossy and heavy-yielding.

Golden Zucchini

Skin
Flesh

Bright yellow fruit on a compact plant, picked just as young; Gold Rush is the best-known name.

Ronde de Nice

Skin
Flesh

A French heirloom picked round and tiny, made for stuffing.

Costata Romanesco

Skin
Flesh

An Italian ribbed heirloom, nutty-flavoured when small, and grown as much for its large blossoms as for its fruit.

Cocozelle

Skin
Flesh

An old Italian type, green with pale stripes, at its best very young.

Cousa

Skin
Flesh

Short, pale and bulbous-ended, the stuffing squash of Middle Eastern kitchens.

Around the World

Italy — the flowers lead: fiori di zucca, blossoms stuffed with ricotta or mozzarella, battered and fried — and the fruit goes into pasta, frittata and slow-cooked summer stews.
France — the courgette is one of the pillars of ratatouille, with tomato, pepper and aubergine.
Turkey — grated zucchini becomes mücver, crisp fritters with herbs and cheese.
Lebanon and the Levant — cousa squashes are hollowed and stuffed with spiced rice or meat as kousa mahshi.
Mexico — calabacitas cooks summer squash with corn, green chile, tomato and cheese — a dish whose roots run straight back to the milpa, where squash grew beside maize and beans long before Europe knew any of the three.

Origin & Lineage

  • Zucchini's deep ancestry is American: Cucurbita pepo was domesticated independently in Mexico and in eastern North America thousands of years ago, and its seed crossed to Europe after Columbus.
  • In the Americas the ancestral squashes were grown in the milpa — the interplanted field of maize, beans and squash — and the seeds were a valued food in their own right, ground into sauces; that seed tradition survives in Mexican cooking today.
  • But the zucchini itself — the slender green fruit picked small — is an Italian creation, selected near Milan in the late 19th century from older European summer squashes. Its name is the diminutive of zucca, Italian for squash; in France and Britain it is the courgette, "little squash" in French.
  • Italian immigrants carried it to the United States in the 1920s, where it kept the Italian name and eventually took a permanent place in summer gardens.
  • Light eating: 17 kcal per 100 g, and mostly water. Zucchini fills a plate without filling a calorie budget.
  • Vitamin C: 17.9 mg per 100 g with the skin on — 20% of the daily value.
  • Eye pigments: lutein and zeaxanthin, 2,125 µg per 100 g, concentrated in the skin. These are the same pigments that collect in the human retina; stated as content.
  • Also present: potassium 261 mg, folate 24 µg and fibre 1.0 g per 100 g.
  • Caution — bitterness: zucchini grown from home-saved seed can cross with ornamental gourds and turn dangerously bitter with cucurbitacins. In 2015 a man in Germany died after eating a stew made with a bitter home-grown zucchini, and Germany's Federal Institute for Risk Assessment issued a public warning. Shop-bought and seed-catalogue zucchini is bred safe; any zucchini that tastes intensely bitter should be spat out and thrown away, never cooked into submission.
  • When to plant: Sow outside after the last frost.
  • Sun: Full sun.
  • Soil: Rich soil.
  • Environment: A warm-season bush — the most generous plant in a summer garden; one or two bushes out-produce most households.
  • Planter or ground: Ground, or large planters.
  • Spacing and depth: Sow 2–3 cm deep; give each plant about a square metre.
  • Start inside or outside: Sow outside, or start inside a few weeks before the last frost.
  • Water: Water steadily, at the base of the plant.
  • Companions: Corn and beans — the Three Sisters planting.
  • When to pick: Pick at 15–20 cm (6–8 in), every day or two.

Garden notes

  • Fruit left on the vine swell into marrows and slow the plant down — picking keeps it producing.
  • The flowers open in the morning; once the bees have worked them, spare male blossoms can go to the kitchen.
  • Late in the season, watch for powdery mildew on the leaves, and in North America for the squash vine borer in the stems.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for zucchini, with skin, raw
  • Kew, Plants of the World Online — Cucurbita pepo: family and origin
  • Paris (2001) — history of the cultivar groups of Cucurbita pepo, including zucchini's Milanese origin
  • BfR (2015) — German Federal Institute for Risk Assessment warning on bitter home-grown zucchini

Watermelon

Citrullus lanatus

CulinaryFreshDriedCookedFruitSeed

A trailing African vine grown for the largest fruit in the garden: a hard-rinded berry that can pass 10 kg, filled with sweet, watery flesh — red in most modern varieties, yellow in some — and flat, dark seeds. Watermelon is eaten fresh by the slice, but the plant gives more than the slice: the seeds are roasted and eaten in many countries, and the rind is cooked and pickled where nothing is wasted. Among the gourds it is a more distant cousin, from another continent entirely.

Varieties

Seeded

Skin
Flesh

The original form: full-sized fruit with mature dark seeds, and the fullest flavour range of any group.

Seedless

Skin
Flesh

Not seed-free by magic but by chromosome count. A parent plant with doubled chromosomes (four sets) crossed with a normal plant (two sets) gives a plant with three sets, whose fruit grows normally but whose seeds never mature — only soft white traces remain. The method came out of Japanese breeding work begun in 1939; seedless fruit became common in shops from the 1990s. Seedless vines still need a seeded variety planted beside them for pollination.

Yellow- and Orange-Fleshed

Skin
Flesh

Older than the red standard in some regions, and honey-sweet; Yellow Crimson is the best-known name. Red flesh is itself a bred trait — wild and early watermelons were pale.

Icebox

Skin
Flesh

Bred small, at 2–7 kg, to fit a refrigerator and a small household. Sugar Baby, released in 1959, made the type a garden standard.

Giant

Skin
Flesh

Grown for contests rather than kitchens; the heaviest watermelon on record stands near 159 kg.

Around the World

West Africa — some watermelons are grown not for sweet flesh at all but for their seeds: the seed-grown egusi melons of this same species are harvested, dried and ground into the thickening heart of egusi soup in Nigeria and its neighbours.
China — watermelon seeds are roasted and salted as a snack, and the rind is stir-fried rather than discarded.
American South — the rind is pickled, sweet and spiced.
Egypt and the Levant — roasted seeds are a standard snack, in lands that have eaten the fruit since antiquity.
Russia — in the lower Volga, tradition boiled the juice down to nardek, a watermelon "honey."
Japan — square watermelons are grown in boxes as gifts — priced as objects, not as lunch.

Origin & Lineage

  • Watermelon is African. Genetic evidence published in 2021 points to the Kordofan melon of Sudan — a pale, non-bitter wild watermelon still grown there — as the crop's closest relative and likely ancestor, settling a long argument that had placed the origin in southern Africa on the strength of a misidentified relative.
  • Paintings in Egyptian tombs suggest watermelon was already eaten as a sweet dessert fruit in the Nile Valley some 4,000 years ago.
  • Its oldest use was simpler than dessert: in the dry Kalahari, wild watermelons were a stored drink — a water source for people and animals through the dry season.
  • From Africa the fruit travelled to India and to China, which is now the world's largest grower, and it reached the Americas in the 1600s, with Europeans and with enslaved Africans.
  • Even its chemistry carries its name: the amino acid citrulline was first isolated from watermelon juice in 1914, and was named for Citrullus.
  • Hydration and lightness: 30 kcal per 100 g, about 92% water, and 6.2 g of natural sugars. Sweet refreshment at low energy cost.
  • Lycopene: 4,532 µg per 100 g — more, gram for gram, than raw tomato (2,573 µg). Lycopene is the pigment that makes the flesh red. It is stated here as content: studies of lycopene and of watermelon juice exist, but no disease claim is made for eating the fruit.
  • Citrulline: watermelon is the richest common food source of this amino acid — a few hundred milligrams in a cup of flesh, and more in the rind than in the flesh. The body converts citrulline to arginine, which plays a part in normal blood-vessel function; that pathway is stated, not promised. Small trials of concentrated watermelon extracts and juices are not trials of eating the fruit.
  • Also present: vitamin C 8.1 mg, potassium 112 mg and vitamin A 28 µg RAE per 100 g.
  • Seeds and rind: the seeds are edible and nourishing roasted, and the rind is edible cooked or pickled — in much of the world neither is treated as waste.
  • When to plant: Sow outside after all danger of frost.
  • Sun: Full sun.
  • Soil: Rich, sandy, well-drained soil.
  • Environment: A heat-lover that needs a long, warm season; the vines spread for metres.
  • Planter or ground: Ground — the vines need room to run.
  • Spacing and depth: Sow 2–3 cm deep; space hills about 2 m apart.
  • Start inside or outside: Start inside a few weeks before the last frost, or sow outside after it.
  • Water: Water deeply while fruit is sizing.
  • Companions: Corn is the classic companion.
  • When to pick: Read ripeness three ways together — the tendril nearest the fruit dries and browns, the pale ground spot where the melon rests turns creamy yellow, and the rind loses its shine.

Garden notes

  • Bees are essential: only the female flowers fruit, and each needs several bee visits to set well.
  • Seed for saving comes only from seeded types, taken from a fully ripe fruit.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for watermelon, raw
  • Renner et al. (2021) — genomic study identifying the Sudanese Kordofan melon as the watermelon's closest relative
  • Rimando & Perkins-Veazie (2005) — measurement of citrulline in watermelon flesh and rind
  • Kew, Plants of the World Online — Citrullus lanatus: family and native range

Winter Squash

Cucurbita pepo · C. moschata · C. maxima

CulinaryDriedCookedFruitSeedFlower

Winter squash is not one plant but a harvest: the mature, hard-shelled fruit of three Cucurbita species, picked fully ripe in autumn and able to keep for months in a cool room. Where summer squash is eaten young and tender, winter squash is eaten mature — the rind hard, the flesh dense, sweet and deep orange, and the seeds large enough to roast. The flesh is the main part eaten; the seeds — pepitas — are a food in themselves, and the blossoms are eaten in season. Pumpkin is the family's most famous member; this entry covers the rest of the hard-shelled tribe: butternut, acorn, spaghetti, kabocha, delicata and their kin.

Varieties

Butternut

Skin
Flesh

Tan and bell-shaped (Cucurbita moschata), with all the seeds in the bulb and a long neck of solid flesh. Bred by Charles Leggett in Stow, Massachusetts, in the 1940s, from a gooseneck squash crossed with a Hubbard type; the name is said to be his — "smooth as butter, sweet as a nut." Sweet, smooth, and the easiest of the group to peel.

Acorn

Skin
Flesh

Small, ribbed and dark green (Cucurbita pepo), with an acorn's shape. Grown in North America long before it had a variety name; the Table Queen type, sold from 1913, made it a household squash. Halved and baked, it is its own serving bowl.

Spaghetti

Skin
Flesh

A pale yellow cylinder (Cucurbita pepo) whose cooked flesh separates into strands. Bred in China in the 1930s and slow to catch on; it became common in North America only in the 1970s. The mildest and least sweet of the group.

Kabocha

Skin
Flesh

A squat, dark-green Japanese squash (Cucurbita maxima) with dense, almost chestnut-sweet flesh. Its name is said to come from Cambodia, the seed carried to Japan by Portuguese traders in the 1500s.

Delicata

Skin
Flesh

Small and cream-and-green striped (Cucurbita pepo), with a rind thin enough to eat. Introduced by a New York seed house in 1894, shelved for decades as too delicate to ship, and revived in recent years for its sweetness.

Hubbard

Skin
Flesh

The giant of the group (Cucurbita maxima): blue-grey or green, warty, thick-shelled, and able to keep half a year. Named in the mid-1800s for Elizabeth Hubbard of Marblehead, Massachusetts, who brought the seed to gardeners' attention.

Around the World

Japan — kabocha is simmered in dashi as kabocha no nimono and fried as tempura.
Italy — winter squash fills pasta: cappellacci di zucca in Ferrara, tortelli in Mantua.
Argentina — winter squash thickens locro, the maize, bean and squash stew.
New England — acorn halves are baked with butter and maple syrup.
Caribbean — calabaza runs through soups and stews such as sancocho.
Mexico — the seeds take the lead: pepitas are ground into pipián sauces and into sikil p'ak, the Yucatecan pumpkin-seed dip — seed dishes older than the Spanish arrival, and the direct survival of the squash's oldest use.

Origin & Lineage

  • All three species are American, domesticated from wild gourds thousands of years ago — Cucurbita pepo in Mexico and eastern North America, C. moschata in the warm lowlands further south, C. maxima in South America.
  • Indigenous growers selected them into keeping squashes, and that keeping quality was the historical point of the plant: a fruit that could feed a household through winter without refrigeration.
  • Squash stood with maize and beans in the Three Sisters planting of the Haudenosaunee and many other peoples; the flesh was dried in strips for storage, and the seeds were eaten as food and ground into sauces.
  • Europeans met winter squash in the 1500s and carried the seed worldwide within a century — kabocha's line runs from the Americas through Portuguese traders to Japan.
  • The named varieties are much newer work: butternut was bred in Massachusetts in the 1940s, and delicata dates to 1894.
  • Vitamin A: butternut carries 532 µg RAE per 100 g raw — 59% of the daily value — almost all of it as beta-carotene (4,230 µg), the pigment that makes the flesh orange. The body converts beta-carotene to vitamin A, which is needed for vision and for the immune system. The winter squashes as a group average less — 68 µg RAE per 100 g across all varieties — with butternut at the rich end.
  • Vitamin C: 21 mg per 100 g in butternut; 12.3 mg across winter squashes generally.
  • Potassium and fibre: butternut carries 352 mg of potassium and 2.0 g of fibre per 100 g, at 45 kcal.
  • The seeds: pepitas are a concentrated food — protein, magnesium and zinc in a small package — roasted from the cavity of any of these squashes.
  • Caution — bitterness: a winter squash that tastes intensely bitter carries too much cucurbitacin and should not be eaten. Documented cases include two women in France who lost much of their hair after eating bitter squash soup (Assouly, 2018). Bred varieties are safe; the warning belongs to home-saved seed that has crossed with ornamental gourds, and to any squash that tastes wrong.
  • When to plant: Sow outside after the last frost.
  • Sun: Full sun.
  • Soil: Rich soil, with room for the vines to run.
  • Environment: A long, warm season; vining types spread for metres, bush forms take less room.
  • Planter or ground: Ground is best — the vines need room.
  • Spacing and depth: Sow 2–3 cm deep; space hills about 1–2 m apart.
  • Start inside or outside: Start inside a few weeks before the last frost, or sow outside after it.
  • Water: Water deeply while fruit is sizing.
  • Companions: Corn and beans — the Three Sisters planting.
  • When to pick: When the rind resists a thumbnail and the stem has corked and dried.

Garden notes

  • Cure the harvest in a warm, airy spot for ten days to two weeks: the skin hardens further, and the flesh sweetens as starches convert — winter squashes measurably gain sweetness and carotenoids in the first weeks of storage.
  • Store cool and dry. Most keep two to six months: delicata and acorn at the short end, Hubbard and butternut at the long end.
  • Save seed only where the plant grew away from ornamental gourds and from other Cucurbita of the same species, or next year's fruit may come up bitter.

Sources

  • USDA FoodData Central — nutrient values for butternut squash, raw, and winter squash, all varieties, raw
  • Kew, Plants of the World Online — Cucurbita pepo, C. moschata and C. maxima: species and origins
  • Assouly (2018) — case reports of hair loss after eating bitter squash, JAMA Dermatology
